  $\Msol(\XX^\circ)$ can be tested along countably many data, i.e.\ there are a
  countable $\XX^\circ_0 \subset \XX^\circ$, a countable $D \subset \T$ and
  countable sets $\ZZ^\circ_s$ such that $P \in \Msol(\XX^\circ)$ if and only if
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:countabletest}
    E^P\bigl[ (Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s) Z^\circ_s \bigr] = 0
    \qquad \text{for all } Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ_0,\ s \leq t \text{ in } D,\
    Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s .
  \end{equation}
  Let $P \in \Msol(\XX^\circ)$ with $P\pi_0^{-1} = \mu$.  Then
  $P(\,\cdot \mid \pi_0 = x) \in \Msol(\XX^\circ, \delta_x)$ for $\mu$-a.e.\ $x$.
  In particular, if $\Msol(\XX^\circ,\delta_x) = \{P_x\}$ for $\mu$-a.e.\ $x$,
  then $P = \int P_x \, \mu(\dif x)$, and $\Msol(\XX^\circ,\mu)$ is a singleton.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Fix $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ_0$, $s \leq t$ in $D$ and $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$.
  For bounded measurable $h : E \to \R$ the variable $Z^\circ_s\, h(\pi_0)$ is
  bounded and $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable, so the martingale property of $Y^\circ$
  under $P$ gives $E^P[(Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s) Z^\circ_s h(\pi_0)] = 0$.  As $h$
  is arbitrary,
  $E^P[(Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s) Z^\circ_s \mid \pi_0] = 0$ $P$-a.s.  The data being
  countable, one null set serves all of them at once; off it,
  $P(\,\cdot \mid \pi_0 = x)$ satisfies \eqref{eq:countabletest} and has initial
  law $\delta_x$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:disintkall}
  Lemma~\ref{lem:disint} together with Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture} is
  \KA, Theorem~32.10, in abstract form; \KA{} attributes it to Stroock and
  Varadhan and uses it exactly this way, to pass from $P_x$ to
  $P_\mu = \int P_x \mu(\dif x)$ for the local martingale problem of a diffusion.
  The countability hypothesis is his too: he replaces $\hat{C}^\infty$ by a
  countable class of truncated coordinate functions and their products.  Note
  that Corollary~\ref{cor:uniqviadual} currently justifies the same passage via
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}\ref{it:absuniq_a}; Lemma~\ref{lem:disint} is the
  better route, since it needs no Markov property.
\end{remark}

Restarting a solution is available at once, and --- this is worth stating
separately, because it is easy to attribute to the Markov property what belongs
to the tower property --- it needs no shift and no Markov structure whatever.

\begin{lemma}[Restart with memory; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{lem:restartmemory}
  Let $P \in \Msol(\XX^\circ)$, let $r \in \T$ and let $Z \geq 0$ be bounded and
  $\Filt^\circ_r$-measurable with $E^P[Z] = 1$.  Then $Z \cdot P$ is again a
  solution \emph{from $r$ onwards}: for every $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ and all
  $r \leq s \leq t$,
  \[
    E^{Z \cdot P}\bigl[ Y^\circ_t \bigm| \Filt^\circ_s \bigr] \;=\; Y^\circ_s .
  \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Let $G \in \Filt^\circ_s$.  Since $r \leq s$, the variable $Z \one_G$ is bounded
  and $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable, so the martingale property of $Y^\circ$ under
  $P$ gives
  $E^{Z \cdot P}[(Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s)\one_G] = E^P[(Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s) Z \one_G] = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Restarting is not where the Markov structure enters]
  \label{rem:restartnomarkov}
  Lemma~\ref{lem:restartmemory} says that one may condition on any event of
  positive probability observed up to $r$, or reweight by any bounded
  $\Filt^\circ_r$-measurable density, and what remains after $r$ is again a
  solution.  Nothing is forgotten and nothing is assumed.  If one wants the
  conditional laws themselves rather than a reweighting,
  Lemma~\ref{lem:disint} supplies them: for standard Borel $F$ the family
  $P(\,\cdot \mid \Filt^\circ_r)$ exists, and it consists of solutions from $r$
  onwards by the same two lines.

  What a shift adds is therefore not the restart but a \emph{re-indexing}.  A
  hypothesis stated about \emph{initial} distributions --- as
  \eqref{eq:absonedim} is, and as \EK, Theorem~4.4.2 is --- can be applied to the
  restarted object only after that object has been turned into a process starting
  at time $0$, and the map that does this is $\theta_r$, which discards the path
  before $r$.  That is the whole of the Markov structure in
  Section~\ref{ssec:markovlayer}: not the ability to begin again, but the demand
  that beginning again should look like beginning.

  Definition~\ref{def:propagation} is the same idea without the re-indexing: the
  two measures are compared at the time $s$ where they already agree, so nothing
  has to be transported back to $0$ and no shift is needed.  This is why
  Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop} is Markov-free while
  Lemma~\ref{lem:propagation} is not.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{An objection: make the state the path, and everything is Markov}

The obvious way to make a path-dependent problem Markovian is to enlarge the
state so that it \emph{is} the past.  The construction works, and it is worth
carrying out, because the exact form of its failure is the best available
justification for Section~\ref{ssec:absuniq}.

\begin{definition}[The path lift]
  \label{def:pathlift}
  Let $X$ be a process with values in $E$.  Its \emph{path lift} is the process
  \[
    \hat{X}_t \;\coloneqq\; X|_{\T_{\leq t}} \;\in\; \hat{E}_t \coloneqq E^{\T_{\leq t}} ,
    \qquad t \in \T ,
  \]
  with $\hat{E}_t$ carrying the product $\sigma$-field.  This is a fibred state
  space in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:Efibred}, and it is never a constant
  one: the fibres grow with $t$.  In the c\`adl\`ag setting one takes
  $\hat{E}_t = D_E[0,t]$ instead.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Every process lifts to a Markov process; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{lem:liftmarkov}
  Let $X$ be any process, with no hypothesis whatsoever.
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item \label{it:lift_sigma}
    $\sigma(\hat{X}_t) = \Filt^X_t$ for every $t$, and consequently
    $\Filt^{\hat{X}}_t = \Filt^X_t = \sigma(\hat{X}_t)$.
  \item \label{it:lift_markov}
    $\hat{X}$ is a Markov process: for $s \leq t$ and bounded measurable
    $\varphi$ on $\hat{E}_t$,
    \[
      E\bigl[ \varphi(\hat{X}_t) \bigm| \Filt^{\hat{X}}_s \bigr]
        \;=\; E\bigl[ \varphi(\hat{X}_t) \bigm| \hat{X}_s \bigr] .
    \]
  \item \label{it:lift_strong}
    On the canonical space, and for a \emph{strict} stopping time $T$,
    $\sigma(\hat{X}_T) = \Filt^{\circ}_T$, where $\hat{X}_T$ is read as a value in
    the total space $\Sigma\hat{E} = \coprod_t \hat{E}_t$; so $\hat{X}$ is strong
    Markov as well.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \ref{it:lift_sigma}  $\hat{X}_t$ is the family $(X_u)_{u \leq t}$, so
  $\sigma(\hat{X}_t) = \sigma(X_u : u \leq t) = \Filt^X_t$; and for $u \leq t$ the
  variable $\hat{X}_u$ is the restriction of $\hat{X}_t$, hence
  $\sigma(\hat{X}_u) \subset \sigma(\hat{X}_t)$, so
  $\Filt^{\hat{X}}_t = \bigvee_{u \leq t}\sigma(\hat{X}_u) = \sigma(\hat{X}_t)$.

  \ref{it:lift_markov}  By \ref{it:lift_sigma} the two conditioning
  $\sigma$-fields are \emph{equal}.  There is nothing to prove.

  \ref{it:lift_strong}  This is Definition~\ref{def:pasting}: for a strict $T$ one
  has $\Filt^\circ_T = a_T^{-1}(\mathcal{S})$, and $a_T\omega = \omega(\cdot \wedge T)$
  carries the same information as $\hat{X}_T$ together with the value of $T$,
  which is recorded by the fibre index.  This is exactly the point at which the
  total space of Definition~\ref{def:Efibred} is needed.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why this settles nothing]
  \label{rem:liftcollapse}
  Lemma~\ref{lem:liftmarkov}\ref{it:lift_markov} is a tautology --- an identity of
  $\sigma$-fields, not a property of $X$ --- and the tautology propagates to
  everything the Markov layer proves.  Three collapses, in increasing order of
  seriousness.

  \emph{(i) The Markov property.}  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}\ref{it:absuniq_a}
  asserts of the lift only what Lemma~\ref{lem:liftmarkov} already gives for
  nothing.

  \emph{(ii) The uniqueness criterion.}  The \emph{one-dimensional}
  distributions of $\hat{X}$ are the \emph{finite-dimensional} distributions of
  $X$, since $\hat{X}_t$ is the whole path on $\T_{\leq t}$.  So hypothesis
  \eqref{eq:absonedim}, read for the lift, is the \emph{conclusion} of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}\ref{it:absuniq_b}, and the induction over
  $t_1 < \dots < t_n$ that carries that theorem collapses to its own conclusion.

  \emph{(iii) Even the Markov-free theory collapses.}  In
  Definition~\ref{def:propagation} the test functions $h$ range over all bounded
  measurable functions of $\hat\pi_s$, that is over all bounded
  $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable variables --- which absorbs the weight $Z$.  So for
  the lift, propagation of agreement reads
  \[
    P = Q \text{ on } \Filt^\circ_s
      \quad \Longrightarrow \quad P = Q \text{ on } \Filt^\circ_t ,
  \]
  and at $s = 0$ that is uniqueness itself.  Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop}
  becomes ``if the laws agree then the laws agree''.

  The principle behind all three is worth stating on its own, because it is what
  the whole of Section~\ref{sec:uniqueness} trades on.  \emph{A Markov structure
  is a compression statement}: its content is the gap between $\sigma(X_s)$ and
  $\Filt^X_s$, and the theorems are worth exactly as much as that gap is wide.
  The path lift attains the Markov property by closing the gap, and closing the
  gap is the same operation as deleting the theorems.  ``Every process is
  Markov'' is therefore true, and true in a way that converts every hypothesis
  into its own conclusion.

  Two further observations, both of which matter elsewhere.

  \emph{The triviality is filtration-relative.}  Lemma~\ref{lem:liftmarkov} makes
  $\hat{X}$ Markov with respect to \emph{its own} filtration.  Being a solution
  with respect to a larger $\mathbb{G}$, as in Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}, is not
  automatic even for the lift: an ambient filtration may carry information about
  the future.  This is one reason the restart lemma is stated on a general
  filtered space.

  \emph{The lift is not useless, only useless here.}  Where the content of a
  theorem sits somewhere other than in the Markov property, lifting is a genuine
  device.  In Section~\ref{ssec:exduality} the content is the kernel
  representability \ref{it:D3}, which is a statement about the \emph{dual}
  process on an unchanged and typically small space; taking $E_1$ to be a path or
  genealogy space then costs nothing on the side where the difficulty is not.
  That is exactly \DGP's outlook and the subject of
  Remark~\ref{rem:exdualityscope}.  Note also that the lift is never
  time-homogeneous, so it can only be treated in the fibred, inhomogeneous frame
  of Definition~\ref{def:Efibred} --- which is a second reason for setting that
  frame up.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Is the abstract martingale problem a special case of the Markovian one?]
  \label{rem:liftform}
  Lemma~\ref{lem:liftmarkov} invites the question, and the answer separates two
  things that are easy to run together.

  \emph{Formally yes, and emptily.}  Every $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ is
  $\Filt^\circ_t$-adapted, so by \ref{it:lift_sigma} and Doob--Dynkin there are
  measurable $\varphi_t$ on $\hat{E}_t$ with $Y^\circ_t = \varphi_t(\hat\pi_t)$.
  Read on the lift, $Y^\circ$ therefore has the shape \eqref{eq:sectiontest} of
  Definition~\ref{def:markovMP} with $g = 0$.  So the abstract martingale problem
  \emph{is} a Markovian one, on the lifted state space, with vanishing
  compensator.  This is the same emptiness that Section~\ref{ssec:timeindex}
  records when it observes that every process solves the martingale problem for
  \emph{some} family of test processes, namely the family of its own martingales:
  the Markovian form is not a restriction on the process but on the
  \emph{presentation}, and its content is that $\XX_A$ is a small family
  parametrized by $\dom(A)$ whose compensator is generated pointwise from $g$.
  Taking $g = 0$ discards exactly that content.

  \emph{Informatively, with $g \neq 0$.}  The useful lift is not the one that
  makes the compensator vanish but the one that keeps it and lets its integrand be
  a \emph{path} functional.  On the lift that is again literally
  Definition~\ref{def:markovMP}, and the manuscript already contains it:
  Setting~\ref{set:pathjump} is the Markovian martingale problem on the lift,
  with
  \[
    \hat{f}_t(\hat{x}) = f(\hat{x}(t)) \ \text{ acting on the tip} ,
    \qquad
    \hat{g}_u(\hat{x}) = \mathcal{A}_u f(\hat{x}) \ \text{ the path-dependent rate} ,
  \]
  and Examples~\ref{ex:volterra}, \ref{ex:pathdepsemi} and \ref{ex:hawkes} are all
  of this kind.  So the honest formulation is: the lift does not enlarge the class
  of \emph{processes} --- there was never a restriction there --- but it does
  supply a useful \emph{presentation}, provided one lifts the state and keeps the
  generator rather than lifting the state and deleting it.

  This is not a device invented here.  \CT{} lift stochastic Volterra processes
  to Markov processes on a space of forward curves, with a genuine generator and
  a generalized Feller semigroup, and \ALP{} treat the same objects through their
  transform formula; Section~\ref{ssec:exduality} meets both in
  Remark~\ref{rem:hawkesknown}.

  \emph{What still does not follow is any theorem.}  Remark~\ref{rem:liftcollapse}
  applies verbatim to either version: on the lift the hypotheses of
  Section~\ref{ssec:markovlayer} become their own conclusions.  And the classical
  Markov apparatus --- a time-independent operator, a transition semigroup, Feller
  theory --- is unavailable in either version, since the fibres $\hat{E}_t$ grow
  with $t$ and there is nothing for a semigroup to act on.  What the lift is good
  for is recorded at the end of Remark~\ref{rem:liftcollapse} and carried out in
  Section~\ref{ssec:exduality}.
\end{remark}

So far nothing beyond a preorder has been used, and nothing Markovian.  It is
worth pushing that as far as it goes, because uniqueness of the
finite-dimensional distributions is \emph{not} a Markovian fact.  What is
Markovian is only the reduction of its hypothesis to the \emph{one-dimensional}
distributions, and that reduction is the subject of the next subsection.  The
condition the uniqueness argument actually consumes is the following.

\begin{definition}[Propagation of agreement; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{def:propagation}
  A set $\mathcal{N} \subset \Prob(F)$ \emph{propagates agreement} if for all
  $P, Q \in \mathcal{N}$, all $s \leq t$ in $\T$ and every bounded
  $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable $Z \geq 0$,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:propagation}
    \begin{aligned}
      &E^P\bigl[ Z \, h(\pi_s) \bigr] = E^Q\bigl[ Z \, h(\pi_s) \bigr]
        &&\text{for all } h \in \Bdd(E) \\
      \Longrightarrow\quad
      &E^P\bigl[ Z \, f(\pi_t) \bigr] = E^Q\bigl[ Z \, f(\pi_t) \bigr]
        &&\text{for all } f \in \Bdd(E) .
    \end{aligned}
  \end{equation}
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}[Why the weight $Z$ cannot be dropped]
  \label{rem:whyZ}
  It is tempting to state \eqref{eq:propagation} in the simpler unconditional
  form
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:propagationweak}
    P = Q \ \text{ on } \Filt^\circ_s
    \quad \Longrightarrow \quad
    P = Q \ \text{ on } \Filt^\circ_s \vee \sigma(\pi_t) ,
  \end{equation}
  and an earlier version of this manuscript did.  That is a mistake, and it is
  worth recording where the induction of Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop}
  breaks under it.  The induction produces agreement of the finite-dimensional
  distributions, i.e.\ agreement on $\sigma(\pi_{t_1},\dots,\pi_{t_n})$ for finite
  chains; \eqref{eq:propagationweak} demands agreement on all of
  $\Filt^\circ_{t_n} = \sigma(\pi_u : u \leq t_n)$, which involves \emph{all}
  times below $t_n$ and is therefore strictly more than any stage of the
  induction delivers.  Iterating \eqref{eq:propagationweak} adds one future
  coordinate at a time but never closes the gap, since after adding $\pi_{t_{n+1}}$
  one is still short of $\Filt^\circ_{t_{n+1}}$.

  Form \eqref{eq:propagation} repairs this by carrying the past in the weight $Z$
  instead of in a $\sigma$-field: at stage $n$ one takes
  $Z = \prod_{k\leq n} f_k(\pi_{t_k})$, and the hypothesis of
  \eqref{eq:propagation} is then exactly the induction hypothesis.  It is also
  exactly what the restart of Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} supplies, which is no
  accident: the two are the same statement read from opposite ends.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Uniqueness; \eqref{T2a} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{prop:uniqfromprop}
  Let $\mathcal{N} \subset \Prob(F)$ propagate agreement.  Then $\mathcal{N}$
  contains at most one element with a given law of $\pi_0$.  In particular, if
  $\Msol(\XX^\circ)$ propagates agreement then $\Msol(\XX^\circ,\mu)$ has at most
  one element for every $\mu$, and the same holds for
  $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  Let $P, Q \in \mathcal{N}$ with $P\pi_0^{-1} = Q\pi_0^{-1}$.

  \emph{Step 1: the finite-dimensional distributions.}  We show by induction on
  $m$ that
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:uniqinduction}
    E^P\Bigl[ \prod_{k=1}^m f_k(\pi_{t_k}) \Bigr]
      = E^Q\Bigl[ \prod_{k=1}^m f_k(\pi_{t_k}) \Bigr]
  \end{equation}
  for every chain $t_1 \leq \dots \leq t_m$ in $\T$ and all bounded measurable
  $f_k \geq 0$.

  For $m = 0$ both sides are $1$.  For $m = 1$, apply \eqref{eq:propagation} with
  $s = t = t_1$ and $Z = 1$: the hypothesis is $P\pi_{t_1}^{-1} = Q\pi_{t_1}^{-1}$
  --- which for $t_1 = 0$ is the assumption on the initial laws, and for general
  $t_1$ follows once the case $m=1$ is known for $s = 0$, i.e.\ from
  \eqref{eq:propagation} with $s = 0$, $t = t_1$, $Z = 1$.

  Assume \eqref{eq:uniqinduction} for $m = n$, fix a chain
  $t_1 \leq \dots \leq t_{n+1}$ and bounded $f_1,\dots,f_{n+1} \geq 0$, and put
  \[
    Z \;=\; \prod_{k=1}^n f_k(\pi_{t_k}) ,
  \]
  which is bounded, nonnegative and $\Filt^\circ_{t_n}$-measurable because
  $t_k \leq t_n$ for $k \leq n$.  For every bounded measurable $h \geq 0$ the
  identity $E^P[Z\,h(\pi_{t_n})] = E^Q[Z\,h(\pi_{t_n})]$ is
  \eqref{eq:uniqinduction} for $m = n$ with $f_n$ replaced by $f_n h$, hence
  holds; by linearity it holds for all $h \in \Bdd(E)$.  Now
  \eqref{eq:propagation}, applied with $s = t_n$ and $t = t_{n+1}$, gives
  $E^P[Z f_{n+1}(\pi_{t_{n+1}})] = E^Q[Z f_{n+1}(\pi_{t_{n+1}})]$, which is
  \eqref{eq:uniqinduction} for $m = n+1$.

  \emph{Step 2: from the finite-dimensional distributions to the law.}  By
  linearity, \eqref{eq:uniqinduction} holds for all $f_k \in \Bdd(E)$.  Under
  \eqref{T2a} every finite subset of $\T$ is a chain, so the collection
  \[
    \mathcal{K} \;=\; \Bigl\{ \textstyle\prod_{k=1}^m f_k(\pi_{t_k}) \;:\;
      m \geq 0,\ t_k \in \T,\ f_k \in \Bdd(E) \Bigr\}
  \]
  is closed under multiplication and generates
  $\sigma(\pi_t : t \in \T) = \mathcal{S}$ by \eqref{eq:pathsigma}.  Two
  probability measures agreeing on a multiplicative generating class agree, by
  Dynkin's lemma.  Hence $P = Q$.
\end{proof}


\subsection{The Markov layer: shift systems}
\label{ssec:markovlayer}

Everything so far is free of any Markov structure.  What follows adds it, in the
one form the arguments need, and it does two things: it makes
\eqref{eq:propagation} checkable from the one-dimensional distributions
(Lemma~\ref{lem:propagation}), and it produces the Markov property itself
(Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}).  It is what a convergence theory
does not need and a
uniqueness theory cannot do without.

\begin{definition}[Shift system; \eqref{T4} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{def:shiftstable}
  A family of measurable maps $\theta_r : F \to F$, $r \in \T$, is a
  \emph{shift} on $F$ if $\pi_t \circ \theta_r = \pi_{r+t}$ for all $r,t \in \T$.

  A \emph{shift system} for $\XX^\circ$ is a family $(\XX^\circ_r)_{r \in \T}$ of
  families of $(\Filt^\circ_t)$-adapted $\K$-valued processes on $F$, with
  $\XX^\circ_0 = \XX^\circ$, such that for every $r \in \T$ and every
  $\hat{Y}^\circ \in \XX^\circ_r$ there are $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ and an
  $\Filt^\circ_r$-measurable $\kappa$ with
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:shiftstable}
    \hat{Y}^\circ_t \circ \theta_r \;=\; Y^\circ_{r+t} - Y^\circ_r + \kappa ,
    \qquad t \in \T .
  \end{equation}
  We call $\XX^\circ_r$ the \emph{problem shifted to $r$}, and say that
  $\XX^\circ$ is \emph{shift stable} in the narrow sense if
  $\XX^\circ_r = \XX^\circ$ for every $r$ is a shift system.

  The system is \emph{measurable} if there is a set $I$ and, for each $i \in I$
  and $r \in \T$, an element $\hat{Y}^{\circ,r,i} \in \XX^\circ_r$ exhausting
  $\XX^\circ_r$, such that $(\omega,r) \mapsto \hat{Y}^{\circ,r,i}_t(\omega)$ is
  $\Bor(F) \otimes \mathcal{T}$-measurable for every $i$ and $t$.  For an
  a.s.\ finite stopping time $\tau$ one then writes
  $\XX^\circ_\tau = \{ \hat{Y}^{\circ,\tau(\cdot),i} : i \in I \}$.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:shiftsystemwhy}
  Requiring $\XX^\circ_r = \XX^\circ$ --- which is what the first version of this
  manuscript did --- is a real restriction and not a normalization: it forces the
  martingale problem to look the same from every instant, hence forces the clock
  to be shift invariant and the resulting Markov process to be
  \emph{homogeneous}.  Definition~\ref{def:shiftstable} follows
  \JS, III.2.39 instead, where a whole family of shifted triplets
  $(\rho_r B, \rho_r C, \rho_r \nu)$ is carried along and
  $(\rho_r B)_t(\theta_r\omega) = B_{r+t}(\omega) - B_r(\omega)$; the measurability
  clause is their III.2.39(i).  The cost is nil: the proofs below use only that
  the \emph{shifted} process is a $P$-martingale, never that it belongs to the
  original family.  The gain is the time-inhomogeneous case, and with it the
  removal of shift invariance of the clock from the hypotheses; see
  Remark~\ref{rem:absuniqgain}(ii) and Example~\ref{ex:shiftXA}.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[The shifted problem for $\XX_A$; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{T4}]
  \label{ex:shiftXA}
  Let $q$ be a clock on $\T$, let $A \subset \Meas(\T \times E) \times \Meas(E)$
  allow a time-dependent coefficient, and put
  \[
    \XX^\circ \;=\; \Bigl\{ \bigl( f(\pi_t)
      - \int_{\langle 0,t \rangle_\iota} g(s,\pi_s)\, q(\dif s) \bigr)_{t \in \T}
      \;:\; (f,g) \in A \Bigr\} .
  \]
  Define the \emph{pulled-back clock} $q_r$ by $q_r(A) = q(r + A)$, i.e.\ the
  image of $q$ restricted to $\{v : v \geq r\}$ under $v \mapsto v - r$, and set
  \[
    \XX^\circ_r \;=\; \Bigl\{ \bigl( f(\pi_t)
      - \int_{\langle 0,t \rangle_\iota} g(r+u,\pi_u)\, q_r(\dif u) \bigr)_{t \in \T}
      \;:\; (f,g) \in A \Bigr\} .
  \]
  Then $(\XX^\circ_r)$ is a shift system, with $\kappa = f(\pi_r)$: substituting
  $v = r+u$,
  \begin{align*}
    \hat{Y}^\circ_t(\theta_r\omega)
      &= f(\omega(r+t)) - \int_{\langle 0,t \rangle_\iota} g(r+u,\omega(r+u))\, q_r(\dif u) \\
      &= f(\omega(r+t)) - \int_{\langle r,\,r+t \rangle_\iota} g(v,\omega(v))\, q(\dif v) ,
  \end{align*}
  which is $Y^\circ_{r+t}(\omega) - Y^\circ_r(\omega) + f(\omega(r))$.  Note
  $q_0 = q$ and $\XX^\circ_0 = \XX^\circ$.

  The shifted problem always exists.  It coincides with the original one, i.e.\
  $\XX^\circ_r = \XX^\circ$ for all $r$, precisely when $q_r = q$ and $g$ does not
  depend on time --- that is, when the clock is shift invariant in the sense of
  Definition~\ref{def:clock}.  The system is measurable as soon as
  $(r,u,x) \mapsto g(r+u,x)$ is, which for $q = $ Lebesgue measure and
  measurable $g$ is automatic.
\end{example}

\begin{lemma}[Restart; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{T4} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{lem:restart}
  Let $(\XX^\circ_r)$ be a shift system for $\XX^\circ$ and let $\ZZ^\circ$ be a
  determining set for every $\XX^\circ_r$ (Definition~\ref{def:canonical}).  Let
  $X$ be a process with paths in $F$ on a filtered probability space
  $(\Omega,\Filt,\mathbb{G},P)$, solving the martingale problem for $\XX^\circ$
  with respect to $\mathbb{G}$; let $r \in \T$ and let $Z \geq 0$ be bounded and
  $\Gilt_r$-measurable with $E^P[Z] = 1$.  Then
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:restart}
    R \;\coloneqq\; (Z \cdot P) \circ \bigl( X(r+\cdot) \bigr)^{-1}
      \;\in\; \Msol(\XX^\circ_r) .
  \end{equation}
  In the canonical case $\Omega = F$, $\mathbb{G} = \mathbb{F}^\circ$, $X = \pi$
  this reads $R = (Z \cdot P) \circ \theta_r^{-1}$ with $Z$ being
  $\Filt^\circ_r$-measurable.
\end{lemma}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:restarttwolevel}
  The two levels matter and cannot be collapsed.  In
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}\ref{it:absuniq_a} the weight is $Z = \one_{F_0}$ with
  $F_0 \in \Gilt_r$, and $\Gilt_r$ may be strictly larger than
  $\sigma(X(u) : u \leq r)$; such a $Z$ is \emph{not} a functional of the path and
  cannot be transported to $F$.  What can be transported is the \emph{law} of
  $X(r+\cdot)$ under $Z \cdot P$, which is what \eqref{eq:restart} asserts.
\end{remark}


\begin{proof}
  Let $\hat{Y}^\circ \in \XX^\circ_r$, let $s \leq t$ and let
  $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$.  Choose $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ and $\kappa$ as in
  \eqref{eq:shiftstable}, and evaluate everything along $X$; to keep the notation
  readable write $\tilde{V} = V(X)$ for a functional $V$ on $F$, so that
  $\tilde{Y}^\circ_u$ is a $\mathbb{G}$-martingale by hypothesis.

  Since $Z^\circ_s$ is $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable and
  $\pi_u \circ \theta_r = \pi_{r+u}$, the variable
  $(Z^\circ_s \circ \theta_r)(X) = Z^\circ_s\bigl(X(r+\cdot)\bigr)$ is
  $\sigma(X(u) : u \leq r+s)$-measurable, hence $\Gilt_{r+s}$-measurable; so is
  $Z$, because $\Gilt_r \subset \Gilt_{r+s}$, and so is $\tilde{\kappa}$.  Hence
  $W \coloneqq Z \cdot (Z^\circ_s \circ \theta_r)(X)$ is bounded and
  $\Gilt_{r+s}$-measurable, and the martingale property gives
  $E^P[(\tilde{Y}^\circ_{r+t} - \tilde{Y}^\circ_{r+s}) W] = 0$.  Therefore
  \begin{align*}
    E^R\bigl[ \hat{Y}^\circ_t Z^\circ_s \bigr]
      &= E^P\bigl[ Z \, (\hat{Y}^\circ_t \circ \theta_r)(X) \,
                   (Z^\circ_s \circ \theta_r)(X) \bigr]
       = E^P\bigl[ (\tilde{Y}^\circ_{r+t} - \tilde{Y}^\circ_r + \tilde{\kappa}) W \bigr] \\
      &= E^P\bigl[ (\tilde{Y}^\circ_{r+s} - \tilde{Y}^\circ_r + \tilde{\kappa}) W \bigr]
       = E^R\bigl[ \hat{Y}^\circ_s Z^\circ_s \bigr] ,
  \end{align*}
  the second equality by \eqref{eq:shiftstable} and the third by the display
  above.  As $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$ was arbitrary, part~(ii) of
  Definition~\ref{def:canonical} yields
  $E^R[\hat{Y}^\circ_t \mid \Filt^\circ_s] = \hat{Y}^\circ_s$; integrability of
  $\hat{Y}^\circ_t$ under $R$ holds because $Z$ is bounded and
  $\tilde{Y}^\circ_{r+t}, \tilde{Y}^\circ_r, \tilde\kappa \in L^1(P)$.
\end{proof}

Note where the two hypotheses act.  Shift stability is used only to rewrite
$\hat{Y}^\circ_t \circ \theta_r$; the determining set is used only in the last
step, and it is exactly the r\^ole that Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar} plays in
\EK.  Everything else is the tower property.

\begin{remark}[Submartingale problems: a different direction, not a wider one]
  \label{rem:inequalitystable}
  Replacing ``$Y^\circ$ is a $P$-martingale'' by ``$Y^\circ$ is a
  $P$-submartingale'' throughout defines the \emph{submartingale problem}
  $\Msol^{\mathrm{sub}}(\XX^\circ)$, which is the standard formulation of a
  diffusion with reflecting boundary conditions (\SV).  Three things have to be
  said about it, and the first two are easy to run together.

  \emph{(i) It is not a generalization in the direction of
  Remark~\ref{rem:complexvalued}, but transverse to it.}  Formulating
  ``submartingale'' needs an order on the values, so it forces $\K = \R$; and
  complexifying destroys the order.  The two directions are mutually exclusive,
  and a table that lists both as ``free'' --- as
  Section~\ref{ssec:notgeneralized} does --- must be read as offering a choice,
  not a sum.

  \emph{(ii) Over $\R$ it is nevertheless a genuine generalization,} and the
  precise sense is worth recording because it is what makes the label honest:
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:submartsym}
    \Msol(\XX^\circ) \;=\; \Msol^{\mathrm{sub}}\bigl( \XX^\circ \cup (-\XX^\circ) \bigr) ,
  \end{equation}
  since $Y^\circ$ and $-Y^\circ$ are both submartingales exactly when $Y^\circ$ is
  a martingale.  The martingale problem is thus the submartingale problem for a
  \emph{symmetric} test family, and every statement about
  $\Msol^{\mathrm{sub}}$ specializes to one about $\Msol$ by symmetrizing.

  \emph{(iii) Which of the machinery survives, and at what cost.}
  Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture} transfers with no change at all: its proof integrates
  $E^{P_\vartheta}[(Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s)\one_G] = 0$ over $\nu$, and an
  inequality integrates just as well; note that it uses no determining set.
  Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} likewise, the weight
  $W = Z \cdot (Z^\circ_s \circ \theta_r)(X)$ in its proof being nonnegative
  whenever $Z$ and $Z^\circ_s$ are.

  What does \emph{not} come for free is the determining set, and this is where
  the transfer stops being a matter of reading ``$\geq$'' for ``$=$''.
  Definition~\ref{def:canonical}(ii) has to be replaced by its one-sided form:
  $E^P[(Y_t - Y_s)Z^\circ_s] \geq 0$ for all \emph{nonnegative}
  $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$ should imply $E^P[Y_t \mid \Filt_s] \geq Y_s$.  That
  is a strictly stronger demand on $\ZZ^\circ$ than the two-sided one, because a
  finite signed measure that is nonnegative on a $\pi$-system generating
  $\Filt_s$ need not be nonnegative.  It does hold for the family of
  Example~\ref{ex:determining}, but for a reason that has to be supplied:
  Lemma~\ref{lem:semiring} below.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[One-sided determination; \eqref{E2}]
  \label{lem:semiring}
  Let $\mathcal{C}$ be a semiring of subsets of $\Omega$ with
  $\Omega \in \mathcal{C}$, generating the
  $\sigma$-field $\Gilt$, and let $\mu$ be a finite signed measure on $\Gilt$ with
  $\mu(C) \geq 0$ for every $C \in \mathcal{C}$.  Then $\mu \geq 0$.

  The hypothesis $\Omega \in \mathcal{C}$ cannot be dropped: on
  $\Omega = \{1,2\}$ the family $\{\emptyset,\{1\}\}$ is a semiring generating
  $2^\Omega$, and $\mu = \delta_1 - 5\,\delta_2$ is nonnegative on it without
  being nonnegative.

  Consequently, if $E$ satisfies \eqref{E2}, then the products
  $\prod_{k \leq n} h_k(\pi_{t_k})$ with $h_k \in \Cb(E)$, $h_k \geq 0$, are
  one-sided determining in the sense of
  Remark~\ref{rem:inequalitystable}(iii), so that
  Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar} and Example~\ref{ex:determining} survive the
  passage to submartingale problems.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \emph{The signed-measure statement.}  Finite disjoint unions of elements of
  $\mathcal{C}$ form an algebra $\mathcal{A}$ --- closure under complements is
  where $\Omega \in \mathcal{C}$ enters, via the semiring property
  $\Omega \setminus C = \biguplus_i C_i$ --- and $\mu \geq 0$ on $\mathcal{A}$
  by additivity.  Let $\mu = \mu^+ - \mu^-$ be the Hahn--Jordan decomposition with
  negative set $N$.  Since $|\mu|$ is finite and $\mathcal{A}$ generates $\Gilt$,
  for every $\varepsilon > 0$ there is $A \in \mathcal{A}$ with
  $|\mu|(A \triangle N) < \varepsilon$; then
  $\mu(A) \leq \mu(N) + \varepsilon = -\mu^-(\Omega) + \varepsilon$, and $\mu(A) \geq 0$ forces
  $\mu^-(\Omega) < \varepsilon$.  As $\varepsilon$ was arbitrary, $\mu^- = 0$.

  \emph{The application.}  Put $\mu(G) = E^P[(Y_t - Y_s)\one_G]$ for
  $G \in \Filt_s$, a finite signed measure.  Given closed $A_1,\dots,A_n \subset E$
  choose $h^m_k(x) = (1 - m\,d(x,A_k))^+ \in \Cb(E)$, which is legitimate under
  \eqref{E2} and decreases to $\one_{A_k}$; then $\prod_k h^m_k$ decreases to
  $\one_{A_1 \times \dots \times A_n}$ and dominated convergence gives
  $\mu \geq 0$ on closed measurable rectangles.  By regularity of the finite
  measure $|\mu| \circ (\pi_{t_1},\dots,\pi_{t_n})^{-1}$ on a metric space this
  extends to all Borel rectangles, which form a semiring containing the full
  space (all $B_k = E$) and generating
  $\sigma(\pi_{t_1},\dots,\pi_{t_n})$; the first part applies.  Finally, the
  Borel rectangles over \emph{all} finite subsets of $\T_{\leq s}$ again form a
  semiring containing the full space --- a product of two rectangles over
  different time sets is a rectangle over their union --- and it generates
  $\Filt_s$, so one more application of the first part gives $\mu \geq 0$
  there.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What is still lost]
  \label{rem:submartlost}
  With Lemma~\ref{lem:semiring} the structural half of
  Section~\ref{ssec:absuniq} is available for submartingale problems.  Two things
  are not.

  \emph{Disintegration.}  Lemma~\ref{lem:disint} needs the countable test
  \eqref{eq:countabletest}, so that one null set serves all data at once.  In the
  one-sided form the test variables must range over a family rich enough for
  Lemma~\ref{lem:semiring}, and a countable such family exists only if $E$ is
  second countable --- which \eqref{E2} gives, so the lemma does transfer, but the
  hypothesis is no longer the innocuous one it was.

  \emph{Uniqueness.}  Definition~\ref{def:propagation} compares two measures by
  \emph{equality} of expectations, and an inequality propagates nothing: from
  $E^P[Zf(\pi_s)] \geq E^Q[Zf(\pi_s)]$ one cannot conclude anything at $t$.  A
  submartingale problem therefore needs a uniqueness criterion of its own, and
  supplying one is exactly the work done in the reflecting-boundary literature.
  This manuscript does not attempt it; Task~22 of the plan records it.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Agreement propagates under a shift system; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{T4} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{lem:propagation}
  Let $(\XX^\circ_r)$ be a shift system for $\XX^\circ$ with $\ZZ^\circ$
  determining for each $\XX^\circ_r$, and suppose that for every $r \in \T$
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:absonedim}
    P, Q \in \Msol(\XX^\circ_r), \ P \pi_0^{-1} = Q \pi_0^{-1}
    \quad \Longrightarrow \quad
    P \pi_t^{-1} = Q \pi_t^{-1} \ \text{ for all } t \in \T .
  \end{equation}
  Then $\Msol(\XX^\circ)$ propagates agreement.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Let $P, Q \in \Msol(\XX^\circ)$, let $s \leq t$, and let $Z \geq 0$ be bounded
  and $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable with
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:propagationhyp}
    E^P[Z\,h(\pi_s)] = E^Q[Z\,h(\pi_s)] \qquad \text{for all } h \in \Bdd(E) .
  \end{equation}
  Taking $h \equiv 1$ gives $c \coloneqq E^P[Z] = E^Q[Z]$.  If $c = 0$ then
  $Z = 0$ both $P$- and $Q$-a.s.\ and the conclusion is trivial, so assume
  $c > 0$ and replace $Z$ by $Z/c$, i.e.\ assume $E^P[Z] = E^Q[Z] = 1$.

  By Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}, $R_P = (Z\cdot P)\circ\theta_s^{-1}$ and
  $R_Q = (Z\cdot Q)\circ\theta_s^{-1}$ both lie in $\Msol(\XX^\circ_s)$ --- the
  \emph{same} shifted problem, which is what makes \eqref{eq:absonedim}
  applicable.  Since $\pi_0 \circ \theta_s = \pi_s$,
  \[
    E^{R_P}[h(\pi_0)] = E^P[Z\,h(\pi_s)] = E^Q[Z\,h(\pi_s)] = E^{R_Q}[h(\pi_0)]
  \]
  for every $h \in \Bdd(E)$ by \eqref{eq:propagationhyp}, so $R_P$ and $R_Q$ have
  the same initial law.  Hence \eqref{eq:absonedim} gives
  $E^{R_P}[f(\pi_u)] = E^{R_Q}[f(\pi_u)]$ for every $u \in \T$ and
  $f \in \Bdd(E)$.  Taking $u = t - s$, which exists by \eqref{T4}, and using
  $\pi_u \circ \theta_s = \pi_{s+u} = \pi_t$, this reads
  $E^P[Z f(\pi_t)] = E^Q[Z f(\pi_t)]$.  That is \eqref{eq:propagation}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Abstract uniqueness and Markov property; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{T4}, and \eqref{T2a} for \ref{it:absuniq_b}]
  \label{thm:absuniq}
  Let $(\XX^\circ_r)$ be a shift system for $\XX^\circ$, let $\ZZ^\circ$ be
  determining for every $\XX^\circ_r$, and suppose \eqref{eq:absonedim} holds for
  every $r \in \T$.  Then, subject to the integrability provisos of
  Lemmas~\ref{lem:mixture} and \ref{lem:restart}:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item \label{it:absuniq_a}
    every solution is Markov --- in general \emph{time inhomogeneously} so: if
    $X$ has paths in $F$ and solves the martingale problem with respect to
    $(\Gilt_t)$, then for $f \in \Bdd(E)$, $r,t \in \T$,
    \[
      E\bigl[ f(X(r+t)) \bigm| \Gilt_r \bigr]
        \;=\; E\bigl[ f(X(r+t)) \bigm| X(r) \bigr] ;
    \]
  \item \label{it:absuniq_b}
    if $\T$ satisfies \eqref{T2a}, then $\Msol(\XX^\circ,\mu)$ contains at most one
    element, for every $\mu \in \Prob(E)$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \emph{\ref{it:absuniq_a}.}
  Fix $r \in \T$ and $F_0 \in \Gilt_r$ with $P(F_0)>0$, and put
  \[
    Z_1 = \frac{\one_{F_0}}{P(F_0)} , \qquad
    Z_2 = \frac{E[\one_{F_0} \mid X(r)]}{P(F_0)} .
  \]
  Both are nonnegative, bounded by $1/P(F_0)$, of mean $1$, and
  $\Gilt_r$-measurable --- $Z_2$ is even $\sigma(X(r))$-measurable, while $Z_1$
  need not be a functional of the path, which is why
  Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} is stated on $(\Omega,\Filt,\mathbb{G},P)$ and not on
  $F$; see Remark~\ref{rem:restarttwolevel}.  Applying
  Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} at $r$, the two laws
  $R_i = (Z_i \cdot P) \circ (X(r+\cdot))^{-1}$ lie in $\Msol(\XX^\circ_r)$ ---
  the \emph{same} shifted problem for $i=1$ and $i=2$, which is all that
  \eqref{eq:absonedim} needs.
  A direct computation gives, for $B \in \Bor(E)$,
  \[
    R_1 \pi_0^{-1}(B) = \frac{E[\one_{F_0} \one_B(X(r))]}{P(F_0)}
      = R_2 \pi_0^{-1}(B) ,
  \]
  the middle expression for $i=2$ because
  $E[\one_{F_0} \one_B(X(r))] = E\bigl[E[\one_{F_0}\mid X(r)] \one_B(X(r))\bigr]$.
  So \eqref{eq:absonedim} applies and yields
  $E^{R_1}[f(\pi_t)] = E^{R_2}[f(\pi_t)]$, i.e.
  \[
    E\bigl[ \one_{F_0} \, E[f(X(r+t)) \mid \Gilt_r] \bigr]
      \;=\; E\bigl[ \one_{F_0} \, E[f(X(r+t)) \mid X(r)] \bigr] ,
  \]
  where on the right we used the $\sigma(X(r))$-measurability of $Z_2$ once more.
  Since $F_0 \in \Gilt_r$ was arbitrary, the Markov property follows.

  \emph{\ref{it:absuniq_b}.}  Lemma~\ref{lem:propagation} and
  Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Uniqueness is not a Markovian fact]
  \label{rem:uniqnotmarkov}
  The decomposition above is worth reading off, because the classical statement
  hides it.
  \begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{@{}lll@{}}
  \toprule
  & \textbf{needs} & \textbf{Markovian?} \\
  \midrule
  Uniqueness (Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop})
    & \eqref{eq:propagation} & \textbf{no} \\
  \eqref{eq:propagation} from one-dimensional laws (Lemma~\ref{lem:propagation})
    & shift system & yes \\
  Markov property (Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}\ref{it:absuniq_a})
    & shift system & yes \\
  \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \end{center}

  Two consequences.  First, Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop} uses no shift,
  no $\theta_r$, no re-indexing and \emph{no} bundle \eqref{T4}: only \eqref{T2a}, for the
  chains of Remark~\ref{rem:chainonly}.  \EK, Theorem~4.4.2 looks Markovian
  because its hypothesis is stated \emph{unconditionally}, on the one-dimensional
  distributions; and an unconditional hypothesis can be applied at a later time
  only after the restarted process has been re-indexed to start at $0$, which is
  what $\theta_r$ does and where the shift enters.  Restarting \emph{itself} is
  free, by Lemma~\ref{lem:restartmemory}.  State the hypothesis conditionally, as
  \eqref{eq:propagation} does, and the Markov structure disappears from the
  uniqueness half.

  Second, \eqref{eq:propagation} is available in genuinely non-Markovian
  situations, where no shift system exists:
  \begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{Path-dependent equations.}  Pathwise uniqueness gives more than
    uniqueness in law: it gives uniqueness in law of the equation \emph{restarted
    from a prescribed past}, and that is what \eqref{eq:propagation} asks for.
    Plain uniqueness in law is \emph{not} enough --- \eqref{eq:propagation} is a
    conditional statement, and Remark~\ref{rem:whyZ} explains why the
    unconditional one does not suffice.  The coefficients may depend on the whole
    past.
  \item \emph{Change of measure.}  If $\XX^\circ$ arises from a family with
    \eqref{eq:propagation} by an absolutely continuous change of measure with a
    path-dependent density, the property is inherited.
  \item \emph{Semimartingale problems.}  \JS, Chapter~III prove uniqueness for
    triplets $(B,C,\nu)$ that are predictable functionals of the path, with no
    Markov structure in sight.
  \end{itemize}
  None of these is developed here; the point is only that
  Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop} is the statement they would use, and
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}\ref{it:absuniq_b} is not.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Why \ref{it:absuniq_b} needs a linear order]
  \label{rem:chainonly}
  Part \ref{it:absuniq_a} uses nothing about $\T$ beyond \eqref{T0} and \eqref{T4}: the
  proof needs $\theta_r$, the inclusion $\Filt^\circ_r \subset \Filt^\circ_{r+s}$
  and the martingale property at $r+s \leq r+t$, all of which hold in a
  preordered monoid.  There is no topology and no linear order in it.

  Part \ref{it:absuniq_b} is different, and the reason is the induction of
  Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop}, not the shift.  That induction advances
  along $\Filt^\circ_{t_1} \subset \Filt^\circ_{t_2} \subset \dots$, so the times
  must form a \emph{chain}.  On a linearly ordered $\T$ every finite set of times
  is a chain and nothing is lost.  On a merely partially ordered $\T$ one obtains
  equality of the finite-dimensional distributions along chains only, and these
  do not determine the law: on $\T = \Rp^2$ the times $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$ are
  incomparable, and no chain sees their joint distribution.  Replacing $t_n$ by
  $t_1 \vee \dots \vee t_n$, which \eqref{T1} would allow, does not repair this, since
  the next time $t_{n+1}$ need not dominate that join.

  So the slogan ``uniqueness of the one-dimensional distributions implies
  uniqueness'' is \emph{false} for a multiparameter index, and the obstruction is
  the induction, not the martingale problem.  This is the sharpest instance of
  the general pattern of Section~\ref{ssec:timeindex}: the Markov property is
  algebraic and survives on a poset, uniqueness is order-theoretic and does not.
\end{remark}

For the strong Markov property one restarts at a stopping time.  The only extra
input is optional sampling --- which is where path regularity finally enters ---
and it has to be handled with care, because the random times $\tau + s$ are
unbounded and Fact~\ref{fact:optsampl} does not apply to them as it stands.  The
following truncation lemma is the correct tool, and its integrability hypothesis
is the honest one.  An earlier version of this manuscript asked instead for
uniformly integrable increments \emph{on bounded intervals}.  That is a
statement about deterministic windows $[s,t] \subset \T_{\leq T}$, and it says
nothing about the random window $[\tau+s, \tau+t]$, which is unbounded when
$\tau$ is; in particular it does not deliver the integrability that the
truncation argument needs in order to pass to the limit.  What
Lemma~\ref{lem:optsamplafter} shows is that this integrability is not merely
necessary for the bookkeeping but \emph{sufficient} for the conclusion --- no
uniform integrability of the martingale itself is required.

\begin{lemma}[Optional sampling after a finite stopping time; \eqref{T2b} + \eqref{T4}]
  \label{lem:optsamplafter}
  Let $\T$ satisfy \eqref{T2b} and \eqref{T4}, let $Y$ be a right continuous
  $(\Gilt_t)$-martingale, let $\tau$ be an a.s.\ finite $(\Gilt_t)$-stopping
  time, and let $s \leq t$ in $\T$ be such that
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:optafterint}
    Y_{\tau+t} - Y_{\tau+s} \;\in\; L^1(P) .
  \end{equation}
  Then $E\bigl[ (Y_{\tau+t} - Y_{\tau+s}) W \bigr] = 0$ for every bounded
  $\Gilt_{\tau+s}$-measurable $W$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  By \eqref{T2b} there is a countable cofinal increasing sequence $(T_m)$ in $\T$
  (Step~2 of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:absreg}), so
  $\one_{\{\tau \leq T_m\}} \uparrow 1$ a.s.  Fix $m$ and put
  $\sigma_1 = (\tau \wedge T_m) + s \leq \sigma_2 = (\tau \wedge T_m) + t$.  Both
  are $(\Gilt_u)$-stopping times: $\tau \wedge T_m$ is one by
  Fact~\ref{fact:stoppingtimes}, and adding a constant $s$ preserves the property
  because by \eqref{T4} the map $u \mapsto u-s$ is defined and order preserving on
  $\{u \geq s\}$, so $\{\sigma_1 \leq u\} = \{\tau \wedge T_m \leq u-s\} \in
  \Gilt_{u-s} \subset \Gilt_u$ for $u \geq s$, while for $u < s$ the event is
  empty since $\sigma_1 \geq s$.  Both are bounded by $T_m + t$.  Optional sampling at \emph{bounded} stopping times --- the case
  $\tau_2 \leq T$ of Fact~\ref{fact:optsampl}, which needs only right continuity
  and integrability of $Y$ --- gives
  $E[Y_{\sigma_2} \mid \Gilt_{\sigma_1}] = Y_{\sigma_1}$.  The variable
  $W \one_{\{\tau \leq T_m\}}$ is $\Gilt_{\sigma_1}$-measurable.  Indeed, take
  $W = \one_G$ with $G \in \Gilt_{\tau+s}$; since on $\{\tau \leq T_m\}$ the
  times $\sigma_1$ and $\tau+s$ agree,
  \[
    G \cap \{\tau \leq T_m\} \cap \{\sigma_1 \leq u\}
      \;=\; \bigl( G \cap \{\tau + s \leq u\} \bigr) \cap \{\tau \leq T_m\} ,
  \]
  and this lies in $\Gilt_u$ in both of the two cases the linear order allows:
  for $u \geq T_m$ because $G \cap \{\tau+s \leq u\} \in \Gilt_u$ by definition
  of $\Gilt_{\tau+s}$ and $\{\tau \leq T_m\} \in \Gilt_{T_m} \subset \Gilt_u$;
  for $u < T_m$ because then $\{\tau + s \leq u\} \subset \{\tau \leq u\}
  \subset \{\tau \leq T_m\}$ --- using $\tau \leq \tau + s$, i.e.\ $0 \leq s$ and
  order preservation --- so the second intersecting event is redundant and the
  set equals $G \cap \{\tau+s \leq u\} \in \Gilt_u$.  Hence
  \[
    E\bigl[ (Y_{\sigma_2} - Y_{\sigma_1}) W \one_{\{\tau \leq T_m\}} \bigr]
      \;=\; 0 ,
  \]
  and on $\{\tau \leq T_m\}$ the integrand equals
  $(Y_{\tau+t} - Y_{\tau+s})W$, while off it the indicator vanishes.  So
  $E[(Y_{\tau+t} - Y_{\tau+s}) W \one_{\{\tau \leq T_m\}}] = 0$ for every $m$,
  and dominated convergence --- the dominator is
  $|Y_{\tau+t} - Y_{\tau+s}|\,\lVert W \rVert$, integrable by
  \eqref{eq:optafterint} --- gives the assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Abstract strong Markov property; \eqref{T2b} + \eqref{T4}]
  \label{thm:absstrongmarkov}
  In the situation of Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}, assume in addition that
  $F \subset \DE$, that $(\theta_r)$ extends to a measurable map
  $(\omega,r) \mapsto \theta_r\omega$, that the shift system is measurable in the
  sense of Definition~\ref{def:shiftstable}, that every
  $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ has right continuous paths, and that
  \eqref{eq:optafterint} holds for $Y = Y^\circ(X)$ at the stopping time $\tau$
  below, for every $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ and $s \leq t$.  Let $X$ have paths in $F$, solve the
  martingale problem with respect to $(\Gilt_t)$, and let $\tau$ be an a.s.\
  finite $(\Gilt_t)$-stopping time.  Then
  \[
    E\bigl[ f(X(\tau+t)) \bigm| \Gilt_\tau \bigr]
      \;=\; E\bigl[ f(X(\tau+t)) \bigm| X(\tau) \bigr] ,
    \qquad f \in \Bdd(E),\ t \in \T .
  \]
  If moreover $\tau$ takes countably many values and there is a measurable family
  $(P_{x,r})_{x \in E,\, r \in \T}$ with $P_{x,r} \in \Msol(\XX^\circ_r,\delta_x)$,
  then
  \[
    E\bigl[ f(X(\tau+t)) \bigm| \Gilt_\tau \bigr] \;=\;
      \bigl( T_{\tau,\tau+t} f \bigr)(X(\tau)) ,
    \qquad
    \bigl( T_{r,r+t} f \bigr)(x) \;=\; \int f(\pi_t) \dif P_{x,r} ,
  \]
  a two-parameter family of kernels satisfying the Chapman--Kolmogorov relation
  $T_{r,s} T_{s,t} = T_{r,t}$ for $r \leq s \leq t$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \emph{Step 1: the restart identity at a stopping time.}  The proof of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} rests on the single identity
  $E^P[(\tilde{Y}^\circ_{r+t} - \tilde{Y}^\circ_{r+s})W] = 0$ for bounded
  $\Gilt_{r+s}$-measurable $W$.  We need it with $r$ replaced by $\tau$.  Now
  $\tau + s$ and $\tau + t$ are $(\Gilt_u)$-stopping times: by \eqref{T4} the map
  $u \mapsto u - s$ is defined on $\{u \geq s\}$ and order preserving, so
  $\{\tau + s \leq u\} = \{\tau \leq u - s\} \in \Gilt_{u-s} \subset \Gilt_u$ for
  $u \geq s$, and the event is empty otherwise, since by linearity of the order
  $u < s$ forces $\tau + s \geq s > u$.  Since $\tilde{Y}^\circ$ is a
  right continuous martingale and \eqref{eq:optafterint} holds,
  Lemma~\ref{lem:optsamplafter} gives the identity for every bounded
  $\Gilt_{\tau+s}$-measurable $W$.

  \emph{Step 2: the first assertion.}  With $\XX^\circ_\tau$ in place of
  $\XX^\circ_r$ --- meaningful because the shift system is measurable, so that
  $\hat{Y}^{\circ,\tau(\omega)}$ is jointly measurable --- the remainder of the
  proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} and of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}\ref{it:absuniq_a} is unchanged.

  \emph{Step 3: the second assertion.}  Let $\tau$ take the countably many values
  $r_1, r_2, \dots$, and fix $j$.  It suffices to prove the identity on
  $\{\tau = r_j\}$, since these events partition $\Omega$ and lie in $\Gilt_\tau$.
  Let $F_0 \in \Gilt_\tau$ with $F_0 \subset \{\tau = r_j\}$ and $P(F_0) > 0$,
  write $\mu_{F_0} = P\{X(\tau) \in \cdot \mid F_0\}$, and put
  \[
    R_1 = (Z_1 \cdot P) \circ (X(\tau+\cdot))^{-1}
      \ \text{ with } Z_1 = \one_{F_0}/P(F_0) ,
    \qquad
    R_2 = \int P_{x,r_j} \, \mu_{F_0}(\dif x) .
  \]
  Both lie in $\Msol(\XX^\circ_{r_j})$: the first by Steps~1--2, the second by
  Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture}, and it is here that $F_0$ must be carried by a single
  value of $\tau$ --- a mixture of solutions of \emph{different} shifted problems
  need solve none of them.  Since $R_1\pi_0^{-1} = \mu_{F_0} = R_2\pi_0^{-1}$,
  \eqref{eq:absonedim} applies and gives the assertion on $F_0$.  The
  Chapman--Kolmogorov relation follows by applying the first assertion twice.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[On the two restrictions]
  \label{rem:strongmarkovscope}
  Two hypotheses in Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov} deserve comment.

  \emph{Countably many values.}  In the time-homogeneous case
  $\XX^\circ_r = \XX^\circ$ the restriction is vacuous, because then all the
  measures $P_{x,r} = P_x$ solve one and the same problem and Step~3 needs no
  partition.  It is only in the inhomogeneous case that the mixture must be
  confined to a single $r$.  A general $\tau$ is then reached by approximation
  from above by countably-valued stopping times $\tau_n \downarrow \tau$; this
  requires right continuity of $\mathbb{G}$ and of $r \mapsto P_{x,r}$ in a
  suitable sense, and is not developed here.

  \emph{The integrability \eqref{eq:optafterint}.}  This is what Step~1 needs, and
  it concerns the increment over the \emph{random window}
  $\langle \tau+s, \tau+t \rangle_\iota$, not over a bounded interval.  For the
  family $\XX_A$ with bounded $f$ and $g$ the increment is bounded by
  $2\lVert f\rVert + \lVert g\rVert\, q(\langle \tau+s,\tau+t\rangle_\iota)$; if
  the clock is shift invariant this equals
  $2\lVert f\rVert + \lVert g\rVert\, q(\langle s,t\rangle_\iota)$, a
  \emph{constant}, so \eqref{eq:optafterint} is automatic --- this is the case of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:uniqueness}.  For a general clock it requires
  $E\bigl[q(\langle \tau+s,\tau+t\rangle_\iota)\bigr] < \infty$ and is a genuine
  restriction.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[What the abstract form buys]
  \label{rem:absuniqgain}
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \emph{One lemma, four applications.}  In \EK{} the conditioning
    construction in the proof of the Markov property, its variant in the
    induction step of the uniqueness proof, and the two constructions in parts
    \ref{it:uniq_b} and \ref{it:uniq_c} look like four separate arguments.  They
    are one: Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} with, in turn,
    \begin{align*}
      Z &= \one_{F_0} , &
      Z &= E[\one_{F_0}\mid X(r)] , \\
      Z &= \one_G \ \text{for } G \in \Filt^\circ_s , &
      Z &= \one_{F_0} \ \text{at a stopping time} ,
    \end{align*}
    all normalized to mean $1$.  The only property of $Z$ that is ever used is
    that it is nonnegative and $\Filt^\circ_r$-measurable.  The third of these is
    Lemma~\ref{lem:propagation}; \EK{} use $Z = \prod_k f_k(\pi_{t_k})$ there,
    which is the same lemma applied to a tilted measure instead of a conditioned
    one, and is unnecessary once the induction is arranged as in
    Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop}.
  \item \emph{The shift system is the real hypothesis, and it costs nothing.}
    Definition~\ref{def:shiftstable} is where the Markov property comes from, and
    it is checkable.  For $\XX_A$ it never fails: whatever the clock $q$ and
    however $g$ depends on time, the shifted problem exists and is given
    explicitly by Example~\ref{ex:shiftXA}, with the pulled-back clock
    $q_r = q(r + \cdot)$ and the shifted coefficient $g(r+\cdot,\cdot)$.  What
    \emph{does} depend on $q$ and $g$ is whether the shifted problem is the
    \emph{same} problem: $\XX^\circ_r = \XX^\circ$ holds if and only if $q$ is
    shift invariant and $g$ is time independent, and only then is the Markov
    process homogeneous.  The first version of this manuscript required
    $\XX^\circ_r = \XX^\circ$ and therefore had to assume shift invariance of the
    clock; carrying the shifted family instead --- as \JS, III.2.39 do --- removes
    that hypothesis and yields the inhomogeneous case for free.  \EK{} never have
    to say any of this because their clock is Lebesgue measure and their $A$ does
    not depend on time.
  \item \emph{No operator, no boundedness, no path regularity.}  Parts
    \ref{it:absuniq_a} and \ref{it:absuniq_b} use neither $\Cb(E)$ nor $\DE$;
    $F$ may be any Polish path space, for instance $L^p_{\mathrm{loc}}$.  Path
    regularity enters only in Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov}, and there only
    through optional sampling.
  \item \emph{Mixtures.}  Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture} says $\Msol(\XX^\circ)$ is
    convex and stable under measurable mixtures.  This is what makes the passage
    from $\delta_x$ to a general $\mu$ legitimate --- in \EK{} it is invoked
    silently in part~\ref{it:uniq_c} and in Corollary~\ref{cor:uniqviadual}.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{The local martingale problem}
\label{ssec:localmp}

Everything in Section~\ref{ssec:absuniq} is stated for $\Msol(\XX^\circ)$.  The
local analogue is not a corollary, and the reason is worth stating plainly:
\emph{``$Y$ is a local martingale'' is not a condition linear in $P$}, because
the localizing sequence may depend on $P$.  Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture} and
Lemma~\ref{lem:disint} rest on that linearity, and Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} rests
on a martingale identity that a local martingale does not supply.  All three
therefore fail for $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$ without a further hypothesis.

The hypothesis that repairs them is due to Stroock and Varadhan and is used in
this form by \KA, Theorems~32.10 and 32.11: insist that the localization be
performed by stopping times that are \emph{functionals of the path}, hence the
same for every $P$, and that the family of such times be stable under the shift.

\begin{definition}[Localizing system; \eqref{T2b} + \eqref{T4}]
  \label{def:localizing}
  A \emph{localizing system} for $\XX^\circ$ is a family $\Sigma$ of
  \emph{strict} $(\Filt^\circ_t)$-stopping times on $F$ --- functionals of the
  path, not of any measure --- such that the following hold.  If the solution is
  presented on an ambient space $(\Omega,\Filt,\mathbb{G},P)$ rather than on $F$, all
  three conditions are to be read with $\Filt^\circ$ replaced by $\mathbb{G}$ and
  $Y^\circ$ by $\tilde{Y} = Y^\circ(X)$; strictness of the times in $\Sigma$ is
  what makes $\sigma(X)$ a $\mathbb{G}$-stopping time for every such presentation.
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(L\arabic*)]
  \item \label{it:L1}
    \emph{Uniform localization.}  $\Sigma$ contains a sequence
    $\tau_1 \leq \tau_2 \leq \dots$ with $\tau_n \uparrow \infty$ pointwise, and
    \[
      P \in \Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)
      \iff
      Y^{\circ,\tau_n} \coloneqq Y^\circ_{\cdot \wedge \tau_n}
      \text{ is a $P$-martingale for all } Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ,\ n \in \N .
    \]
  \item \label{it:L2}
    \emph{Shift covariance.}  $\sigma \in \Sigma$ and $r \in \T$ imply
    $r + \sigma \circ \theta_r \in \Sigma$.
  \item \label{it:L3}
    \emph{Integrable increments after a restart.}  For every
    $P \in \Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$, $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$,
    $r \in \T$ and $\sigma \in \Sigma$ with $\sigma \geq r$, the process
    $t \mapsto Y^\circ_{(r+t) \wedge \sigma} - Y^\circ_r$ is a martingale for the
    filtration $(\Filt^\circ_{r+t})_{t \in \T}$ under $P$.
  \end{enumerate}
  $\Sigma$ is \emph{strongly} shift covariant if \ref{it:L2} and \ref{it:L3} hold
  with $r$ replaced by any a.s.\ finite $(\Filt^\circ_t)$-stopping time $\tau$,
  the time $\tau + \sigma \circ \theta_\tau$ being required to be a stopping time.
\end{definition}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:localhyp}
  Three comments on Definition~\ref{def:localizing}.

  \emph{\ref{it:L1} is the whole point.}  It turns membership in
  $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$ into a family of identities
  $E^P[(Y^{\circ,\tau_n}_t - Y^{\circ,\tau_n}_s)\one_G] = 0$, each of which is
  linear in $P$.  The implication ``$\Leftarrow$'' is automatic because
  $\tau_n \uparrow \infty$; ``$\Rightarrow$'' is the assumption, and it says that
  \emph{one} sequence localizes every test process under every solution.

  \emph{\ref{it:L3} is not implied by \ref{it:L1}.}  Stopping a $P$-local
  martingale at $\sigma \in \Sigma$ leaves a local martingale, not a martingale:
  $Y^{\circ,\sigma}$ is unstopped before $r$, where nothing controls it.  What
  \ref{it:L3} asks is only that the \emph{increments after $r$} be integrable,
  which is the same kind of hypothesis as in
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov} and is usually visible at once.

  \emph{The diffusion case.}  For $\XX^\circ = \{f(\pi) - f(\pi_0) - \int A f(\pi)\}$
  on $F = C_{\Rp,\R^d}$ take $\Sigma$ generated by the exit times
  $\tau_n = \inf\{t : |\pi_t| \geq n\}$ and their shifts.  Then \ref{it:L1} is
  \KA, Theorem~32.10, \ref{it:L2} holds because
  $r + \tau_n \circ \theta_r = \inf\{u \geq r : |\pi_u| \geq n\}$ is again an exit
  time, and \ref{it:L3} holds because between $r$ and $\sigma$ the path stays in
  $\overline{B}_n$, so that the increments are bounded by
  $2\lVert f \rVert_{\overline{B}_n} + q(\T_{\leq T})\lVert Af \rVert_{\overline{B}_n}$.
  \KA{} uses $\tau^f_n = \inf\{t : |M^f_t| \geq n\}$ instead, which serves equally
  well and has the advantage of not referring to the state space.
\end{remark}

With Definition~\ref{def:localizing} in hand the three lemmas of
Section~\ref{ssec:absuniq} all go through, and their proofs are the earlier ones
with $Y^\circ$ replaced by $Y^{\circ,\tau_n}$.  We give them in full, since the
point of the exercise is that nothing is being waved through.

\begin{lemma}[Local mixtures and disintegration; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E1}]
  \label{lem:localmix}
  Parts \ref{it:lm_conv} and \ref{it:lm_a} need only \eqref{E0}; \eqref{E1} is used in
  \ref{it:lm_b}.
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item \label{it:lm_conv}
    $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$ is convex.  \emph{No hypothesis is needed
    for this.}
  \item \label{it:lm_a}
    If $\Sigma$ satisfies \ref{it:L1}, then $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$ is
    stable under measurable mixtures $P = \int P_\vartheta \nu(\dif\vartheta)$
    subject to $\int E^{P_\vartheta}|Y^{\circ,\tau_n}_t| \, \nu(\dif\vartheta)
    < \infty$.
  \item \label{it:lm_b}
    If in addition membership can be tested along countably many data as in
    \eqref{eq:countabletest}, then for $P \in \Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$ one
    has $P(\,\cdot \mid \pi_0 = x) \in \Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ,\delta_x)$
    for $P\pi_0^{-1}$-a.e.\ $x$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \ref{it:lm_conv}  Let $P, P' \in \Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$,
  $Q = \alpha P + (1-\alpha)P'$, and let $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ be localized by
  $(\tau_n)$ under $P$ and by $(\tau'_n)$ under $P'$.  Put
  $\sigma_n = \tau_n \wedge \tau'_n$.  Then $Y^{\circ,\sigma_n}$ is a martingale
  under $P$ and under $P'$ --- stopping a martingale at a further stopping time
  leaves a martingale --- hence under $Q$, and $\sigma_n \to \infty$ $Q$-a.s.
  because $\tau_n \to \infty$ $P$-a.s.\ and $\tau'_n \to \infty$ $P'$-a.s.  Here
  the two localizing sequences are allowed to differ; it is only for
  \ref{it:lm_a} that this ceases to be possible.

  \ref{it:lm_a}, \ref{it:lm_b}  By \ref{it:L1},
  $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ) = \Msol(\XX^\circ_\bullet)$ with
  $\XX^\circ_\bullet = \{Y^{\circ,\tau_n} : Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ, n \in \N\}$, a
  family of $(\Filt^\circ_t)$-adapted processes --- stopping a process at a
  stopping time preserves adaptedness once the process is progressively
  measurable, which under \eqref{T2b} a c\`adl\`ag adapted process is.  Now apply
  Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture} respectively Lemma~\ref{lem:disint} to
  $\XX^\circ_\bullet$; neither proof uses anything about the family beyond
  adaptedness, and the countability hypothesis for \ref{it:lm_b} is inherited
  because $n$ ranges over $\N$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:convexfree}
  The contrast between \ref{it:lm_conv} and \ref{it:lm_a} is the whole point of
  \ref{it:L1}, and it is easy to get wrong.  A \emph{finite} convex combination
  costs nothing, because two localizing sequences can be combined by
  $\tau_n \wedge \tau'_n$.  A mixture over a continuum cannot: one would need
  $\inf_\vartheta \tau^\vartheta_n$, which need not tend to infinity and need not
  even be measurable.  That is exactly where a localizing sequence common to all
  $P$ becomes indispensable, and it is why \KA{} states his Theorem~32.10 for a
  \emph{kernel} $x \mapsto P_x$ and takes the trouble to exhibit one.
\end{remark}

The hypothesis \ref{it:L1} is less exotic than it looks: in the two places in the
literature where it is used it is not assumed but \emph{constructed}, and always
by the same device --- localizing along the test process itself rather than along
the state space.

\begin{lemma}[Uniform localization for bounded jumps; \eqref{T2b}]
  \label{lem:L1auto}
  Suppose every $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ is c\`adl\`ag with $Y^\circ_0 = 0$ and has
  jumps bounded by a constant $c_{Y^\circ}$.  Write
  $S^{Y^\circ}_t = \sup_{s \leq t} |Y^\circ_s|$ for the running supremum and put
  \[
    \tau^{Y^\circ}_n \;=\; \inf\{ t \in \T : S^{Y^\circ}_t \geq n \} ,
    \qquad n \in \N .
  \]
  Then each $\tau^{Y^\circ}_n$ is a \emph{strict} $(\Filt^\circ_t)$-stopping
  time, and $\Sigma_0 = \{\tau^{Y^\circ}_n : Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ,\ n \in \N\}$
  satisfies \ref{it:L1}.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Fix $Y = Y^\circ$ and drop the superscript.

  \emph{Step 1: $S$ is c\`adl\`ag, nondecreasing and adapted.}  Monotonicity is
  clear.  Right continuity: given $t$ and $\varepsilon > 0$, right continuity of $Y$
  gives $u > t$ with $|Y_s| \leq |Y_t| + \varepsilon \leq S_t + \varepsilon$ for
  $s \in [t,u)$, whence $S_s \leq S_t + \varepsilon$ there.  Left limits exist because
  $S$ is monotone.  For adaptedness note that, again by right continuity of $Y$
  and the density hypothesis in \eqref{T2b},
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:supcountable}
    S_t \;=\; \sup\bigl\{ |Y_s| : s \in (D \cap \T_{\leq t}) \cup \{t\} \bigr\} ,
  \end{equation}
  a countable supremum of $\Filt^\circ_t$-measurable functions.  Indeed
  ``$\geq$'' is trivial, and for ``$\leq$'' let $s \leq t$; if $s = t$ there is
  nothing to prove, and if $s < t$ then $s$ is non-maximal in $\T_{\leq t}$, so
  by \eqref{T2b} there are $s_k \in D$ with $s < s_k$, $s_k \to s$ from the right and
  $s_k \leq t$ (replace $s_k$ by $s_k \wedge t$, which lies in $D$ or equals
  $t$), and $|Y_{s_k}| \to |Y_s|$.

  \emph{Step 2: $\tau_n$ is a strict stopping time.}  We claim
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:debutclosed}
    \{ \tau_n \leq t \} \;=\; \{ S_t \geq n \} ,
  \end{equation}
  which lies in $\Filt^\circ_t$ by \eqref{eq:supcountable}.  If $S_t \geq n$ then
  $t$ belongs to the set whose infimum is $\tau_n$, so $\tau_n \leq t$.
  Conversely let $\tau_n \leq t$.  If $t$ is maximal, then $S_u \geq n$ for some
  $u \leq t$ and monotonicity gives $S_t \geq n$.  Otherwise, for every $u > t$
  there is $v < u$ with $S_v \geq n$, hence $S_u \geq n$; letting $u \downarrow t$
  along $D$ --- possible by \eqref{T2b} --- right continuity of $S$ gives $S_t \geq n$.
  This is where the \emph{closed} threshold $S_t \geq n$ matters:
  $\inf\{t : |Y_t| > n\}$, the choice of \JS, satisfies \eqref{eq:debutclosed}
  only with $\{ S_t > n \}$ replaced by an event that is not
  $\Filt^\circ_t$-measurable, and is a stopping time for $\Filt^\circ_{t+}$
  only.  See Remark~\ref{rem:strictdebut}.

  \emph{Step 3: the stopped process is bounded.}  On $\{s < \tau_n\}$ we have
  $S_s < n$, hence $|Y_s| < n$; consequently $|Y_{\tau_n-}| \leq n$, and
  $|Y_{\tau_n}| \leq |Y_{\tau_n-}| + |\Delta Y_{\tau_n}| \leq n + c_Y$.  Thus
  $|Y^{\tau_n}| \leq n + c_Y$ everywhere.

  \emph{Step 4: \ref{it:L1}.}  A bounded local martingale is a uniformly
  integrable martingale, so for every $P \in \Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$ the
  process $Y^{\tau_n}$ is a $P$-martingale; this is the implication
  ``$\Rightarrow$''.  For ``$\Leftarrow$'' note that $\tau_n \uparrow \infty$
  because a c\`adl\`ag path is bounded on bounded intervals, so $S_t < \infty$
  for each $t$ and hence $\tau_n > t$ for $n > S_t$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Strict versus right continuous d\'ebuts]
  \label{rem:strictdebut}
  The distinction in Step~2 is not pedantry, and it is the one place in the local
  theory where the raw filtration bites.  For a c\`adl\`ag adapted $Y$ and an
  \emph{open} set $G$ the d\'ebut $\inf\{t : Y_t \in G\}$ satisfies
  $\{\tau < t\} = \bigcup_{s \in D,\, s<t} \{Y_s \in G\}$ and is therefore a
  stopping time for $\Filt^\circ_{t+}$, but in general not for $\Filt^\circ_t$: a
  path with $|Y_s| \leq n$ for $s \leq t$ and $|Y_s| > n$ for $s > t$ has
  $\tau = t$, yet it agrees on $\T_{\leq t}$ with a path that never exceeds $n$.
  Replacing the threshold by the running supremum repairs this, because
  $\{S_t \geq n\}$ is decided by the path up to and including $t$.  For
  \emph{continuous} paths and a closed set the d\'ebut is strict without any such
  device, which is why the exit times of Remark~\ref{rem:localhyp} cause no
  trouble.  In the formalization this is the difference between
  \texttt{IsStoppingTime f} and \texttt{IsStoppingTime (f.rightLimit)} and has to
  be tracked.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:L1sources}
  Lemma~\ref{lem:L1auto} is the argument of \JS, III.2.8, where it proves that
  the set of solutions of a semimartingale martingale problem is convex: they
  take $T_n = \inf\{t : |Y_t| > n\}$ and observe that $Y_0 = 0$ together with
  bounded $|\Delta Y|$ makes $Y^{T_n}$ bounded.  \JS{} work throughout with a
  filtration satisfying the usual conditions, so for them this $T_n$ is a
  stopping time; here the raw filtration forces the running-supremum variant of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:L1auto}, and the boundedness argument is unaffected.  \KA{} uses the same device,
  $\tau^f_n = \inf\{t : |M^f_t| \geq n\}$, in his Theorem~32.10.  Both localize
  along the \emph{test process}, not along the state space; the exit times from
  balls in Remark~\ref{rem:localhyp} are the less canonical choice, since they
  refer to $E$ and presuppose that $f$ and $g$ are bounded on compacts.
\end{remark}

\begin{lemma}[Local restart; \eqref{T2b} + \eqref{T4}]
  \label{lem:localrestart}
  Let $(\XX^\circ_r)$ be a shift system for $\XX^\circ$ with $\ZZ^\circ$
  determining for each $\XX^\circ_r$, and let $\Sigma$ be a localizing system.
  Let $X$ be a process on $(\Omega,\Filt,\mathbb{G},P)$ solving the local martingale
  problem for $\XX^\circ$ with respect to $\mathbb{G}$ and $\Sigma$, let $r \in \T$, and
  let $Z \geq 0$ be \emph{bounded} and $\Gilt_r$-measurable with $E^P[Z] = 1$.
  Then
  \[
    R \;\coloneqq\; (Z \cdot P) \circ \bigl( X(r+\cdot) \bigr)^{-1}
      \;\in\; \Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ_r) .
  \]
  On the canonical space this is $R = (Z \cdot P) \circ \theta_r^{-1}$.  The same
  holds with $r$ replaced by an a.s.\ finite $\mathbb{G}$-stopping time $\tau$ and
  $\Gilt_r$ by $\Gilt_\tau$, provided $\Sigma$ is strongly shift covariant.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  By \ref{it:L1} it suffices to show that $\hat{Y}^{\circ,\tau_n}$ is an
  $R$-martingale for every $\hat{Y}^\circ \in \XX^\circ_r$ and $n \in \N$, and by
  the determining property it suffices to show, for $s \leq t$ and
  $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:localrestart}
    E^R\bigl[ (\hat{Y}^{\circ,\tau_n}_t - \hat{Y}^{\circ,\tau_n}_s) Z^\circ_s \bigr]
      \;=\; 0 .
  \end{equation}

  \emph{Step 1: the shifted stopped process.}
  Let $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ and the $\Filt^\circ_r$-measurable $\kappa$ be as in
  \eqref{eq:shiftstable}, and write $\tilde{Y} = Y^\circ(X)$,
  $\tilde\kappa = \kappa(X)$, $\tilde\tau_n = \tau_n(X(r+\cdot))$; these are
  defined because the elements of $\Sigma$ are path functionals, and
  $\tilde\tau_n$ is a $(\Gilt_{r+t})_t$-stopping time because they are strict.
  Put $\tilde\sigma \coloneqq r + \tilde\tau_n$, the ambient realization of
  $\sigma = r + \tau_n \circ \theta_r \in \Sigma$ (by \ref{it:L2}), so that
  $\tilde\sigma \geq r$.  For $\omega \in \Omega$ write
  $u = t \wedge \tilde\tau_n(\omega)$.  Then, by \eqref{eq:shiftstable},
  \[
    \hat{Y}^{\circ,\tau_n}_t \bigl( X(r+\cdot)(\omega) \bigr)
      = \hat{Y}^\circ_u \bigl( X(r+\cdot)(\omega) \bigr)
      = \tilde{Y}_{r+u}(\omega) - \tilde{Y}_r(\omega) + \tilde\kappa(\omega) ,
  \]
  and, $r + \cdot$ being an order embedding that commutes with $\wedge$
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:shiftembed}, using \eqref{T2a}, which \eqref{T2b} supplies),
  \[
    r + u = r + \bigl( t \wedge \tilde\tau_n(\omega) \bigr)
          = (r+t) \wedge \bigl( r + \tilde\tau_n(\omega) \bigr)
          = (r+t) \wedge \tilde\sigma(\omega) .
  \]
  Hence
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:shiftedstopped}
    \hat{Y}^{\circ,\tau_n}_t \circ X(r+\cdot)
      \;=\; \tilde{Y}_{(r+t) \wedge \tilde\sigma} - \tilde{Y}_r + \tilde\kappa ,
    \qquad t \in \T .
  \end{equation}
  This is \KA's display~(17) in the proof of his Theorem~32.11, and it is the
  only computation in the local theory that is not already in
  Section~\ref{ssec:absuniq}.

  \emph{Step 2: conclusion.}
  Put $W \coloneqq Z \cdot \bigl( Z^\circ_s \circ X(r+\cdot) \bigr)$.  As in the
  proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}, $W$ is bounded and $\Gilt_{r+s}$-measurable:
  $Z^\circ_s$ is $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable and $\pi_u \circ X(r+\cdot) = X(r+u)$
  is $\Gilt_{r+u}$-measurable, while $Z$ is bounded, $\Gilt_r$-measurable and
  $\Gilt_r \subset \Gilt_{r+s}$.  \emph{Boundedness of $Z$ is used here and
  cannot be dropped}: \ref{it:L3} supplies a martingale identity against bounded
  test variables only, and the stopped increments need not be in any $L^p$ with
  $p > 1$.  Using \eqref{eq:shiftedstopped} twice, the terms
  $-\tilde{Y}_r + \tilde\kappa$ cancel, and
  \[
    E^R\bigl[ (\hat{Y}^{\circ,\tau_n}_t - \hat{Y}^{\circ,\tau_n}_s) Z^\circ_s \bigr]
      = E^P\Bigl[ \bigl( \tilde{Y}_{(r+t) \wedge \tilde\sigma}
          - \tilde{Y}_{(r+s) \wedge \tilde\sigma} \bigr) W \Bigr]
      = 0
  \]
  by \ref{it:L3}, which says exactly that
  $t \mapsto \tilde{Y}_{(r+t)\wedge\tilde\sigma} - \tilde{Y}_r$ is a
  $(\Gilt_{r+t})$-martingale under $P$.  This is \eqref{eq:localrestart}.

  For a stopping time $\tau$ the argument is unchanged, with $\Gilt_{\tau+s}$ in
  place of $\Gilt_{r+s}$ and with $\tilde\sigma = \tau + \tau_n(X(\tau+\cdot))$, a
  stopping time by hypothesis.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Local uniqueness, Markov and strong Markov property]
  \label{thm:localuniq}
  Let $(\XX^\circ_r)$ be a shift system for $\XX^\circ$ with $\ZZ^\circ$
  determining for each $\XX^\circ_r$, let $\Sigma$ be a localizing system, and
  suppose that for every $r \in \T$
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:localonedim}
    P, Q \in \Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ_r), \ P\pi_0^{-1} = Q\pi_0^{-1}
    \quad \Longrightarrow \quad
    P\pi_t^{-1} = Q\pi_t^{-1} \ \text{ for all } t \in \T .
  \end{equation}
  Then Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq} holds verbatim with $\Msol$ replaced by
  $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}$ throughout --- for the uniqueness half nothing has to be
  redone, since Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop} is a statement about an
  arbitrary family of measures and applies to
  $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$ as it stands; and if $\Sigma$ is strongly shift covariant
  and the $Y^{\circ,\tau_n}$ are right continuous and satisfy the integrability
  \eqref{eq:optafterint} at $\tau$, so does
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq} uses exactly three things:
  Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}, applied with the densities of
  Remark~\ref{rem:absuniqgain}(i); the hypothesis \eqref{eq:absonedim}; and
  \eqref{eq:pathsigma}.  Replace the first by Lemma~\ref{lem:localrestart} and the
  second by \eqref{eq:localonedim}; the third is a statement about $F$ and is
  untouched.  Every density used is an indicator or a conditional expectation of
  one, hence nonnegative, bounded by $1/P(F_0)$ and $\Gilt_r$-measurable, so
  Lemma~\ref{lem:localrestart} applies to each; and
  Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop} is about $\Msol$ only, so it carries over
  with $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}$ unchanged.

  For the strong Markov property, the proof of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov} consists of the stopping time version of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}, which is the second assertion of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:localrestart}, together with Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture} for the
  mixture $\int P_x \mu_{F_0}(\dif x)$; the latter is
  Lemma~\ref{lem:localmix}\ref{it:lm_a}.  Optional sampling
  (Fact~\ref{fact:optsampl}) is applied to the $Y^{\circ,\tau_n}$, which are
  genuine martingales by \ref{it:L1}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What \JS{} do differently]
  \label{rem:jsdiff}
  Three points where \JS, Chapter~III go beyond the account above.

  \emph{(i) The shifted problem --- adopted.}
  \JS, III.2.39 carry a whole family of shifted triplets
  $(\rho_t B, \rho_t C, \rho_t \nu)$ with
  $(\rho_t B)_s(\theta_t \omega) = B_{t+s}(\omega) - B_t(\omega)$ and likewise for
  $C$ and $\nu$, rather than demanding that the shifted problem be the original
  one.  Definition~\ref{def:shiftstable} is that idea in the present notation, and
  Example~\ref{ex:shiftXA} is the corresponding computation for $\XX_A$.  The
  earlier version of this manuscript required $\XX^\circ_r = \XX^\circ$ and
  consequently had to assume that the clock be shift invariant and $g$ time
  independent; both hypotheses are now gone, and the conclusion is the Markov
  property in its time-inhomogeneous form.  The change costs nothing, because
  Lemmas~\ref{lem:restart} and \ref{lem:localrestart} use only that the
  \emph{shifted} process is a $P$-martingale.

  \emph{(ii) Local uniqueness.}
  \JS, Definition~III.2.37 isolate a notion strictly stronger than the uniqueness
  of \eqref{eq:localonedim}: \emph{local uniqueness} holds if for every strict
  stopping time $T$ any two solutions of the \emph{stopped} martingale problem
  agree on $\Filt_T$.  It implies uniqueness ($T \equiv \infty$) and is what
  absolute-continuity and limit-theorem arguments actually require.  Their
  Theorem~III.2.40 shows that uniqueness \emph{does} imply local uniqueness once
  the problem is of Markovian type in the sense of (i) --- precisely, once there
  is a kernel $P_{x,t}$ solving the shifted problem.  This is carried out in
  Section~\ref{ssec:localuniq} --- and, as it turns out, without the Markovian
  type: Theorem~\ref{thm:localuniqueness} needs only a restart kernel, which
  their construction supplies but which does not presuppose a shift.

  \emph{(iii) Strict stopping times.}
  The canonical filtration $\Filt^\circ_t = \sigma(\pi_u : u \leq t)$ is not right
  continuous, and \JS, III.2.35 therefore distinguish \emph{strict} stopping
  times --- those with $\{T \leq t\} \in \Filt^\circ_t$ for the raw filtration ---
  from stopping times for the completed right continuous filtration, with
  $\Filt^\circ_T \subset \Filt_T$ and $\Filt_{T-} \subset \Filt^\circ_T$ on
  $\{T>0\}$.  Definition~\ref{def:localizing} \emph{demands} that the times in
  $\Sigma$ be strict, and this is a genuine restriction, not a consequence of
  their being path functionals: the d\'ebut $\inf\{t : |Y_t| > n\}$ is a path
  functional and is not strict (Remark~\ref{rem:strictdebut}).  Strictness is
  what Lemma~\ref{lem:L1auto} has to work for, and it is also what allows a
  localizing system defined on $F$ to be transported to an ambient filtration
  $\mathbb{G}$, as Lemma~\ref{lem:localrestart} does.  The distinction should be made
  explicit in the formalization, where Mathlib's \texttt{IsStoppingTime} is
  relative to whatever filtration it is given.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:localsummary}
  Two things are worth extracting.

  First, the entire local theory rests on the single identity
  \eqref{eq:shiftedstopped}, which says that shifting a stopped test process
  produces a test process stopped at the \emph{shifted} time.  That is
  \ref{it:L2}, and it is the local form of shift stability.  Everything else is
  either the global argument verbatim or the linearity that \ref{it:L1} restores.

  Second, this route localizes the \emph{test processes} and leaves the operator
  alone.  \EK, Section~4.6 --- and hence Remark~\ref{rem:uniquelocal} --- instead
  replaces $A$ by $A^{(m)} = \{(f\one_{K_m}, g\one_{K_m})\}$.  In the abstract
  setting the present route is the right one, because $\XX^\circ$ is primitive
  here and $A$ is not; it is also the one that \KA{} takes.

  What is \emph{not} obtained is a local version of Theorem~\ref{thm:absreg}:
  Definition~\ref{def:localizing} presupposes stopping times, which presuppose
  the path regularity that Theorem~\ref{thm:absreg} exists to produce.  See
  Remark~\ref{rem:cadlaglocal}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Local uniqueness}
\label{ssec:localuniq}

Theorem~\ref{thm:localuniq} concludes that two solutions with the same initial law
have the same one-dimensional --- hence all --- distributions.  \JS,
Definition~III.2.37 isolate a strictly stronger property, and it is the one that
absolute-continuity and limit arguments actually use.

\begin{definition}[Local uniqueness; \eqref{T2b} + \eqref{T4}]
  \label{def:localuniq}
  Write $\XX^{\circ,T} = \{ Y^{\circ,T} : Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ \}$ for the family
  stopped at a stopping time $T$.  \emph{Local uniqueness} holds for $\XX^\circ$
  if for every strict stopping time $T$ (Remark~\ref{rem:jsdiff}(iii)) and any two
  $P, P' \in \Msol(\XX^{\circ,T})$ with $P\pi_0^{-1} = P'\pi_0^{-1}$ one has
  $P = P'$ on $\Filt^\circ_T$.
\end{definition}

Taking $T \equiv \infty$ recovers uniqueness, so local uniqueness is at least as
strong; \JS{} note that it is strictly stronger in general.  \JS, Theorem~III.2.40
derives it from uniqueness for problems of Markovian type, and it is natural to
expect a shift system to be needed.  It is not.  What is needed are two
properties of the path space --- one of the stopping time, one supplying
solutions after it --- and neither mentions a shift.

\begin{definition}[Strict stopping times; \eqref{T2b}]
  \label{def:pasting}
  A stopping time $T$ on $F$ is \emph{strict} if $F$ is stable under the stopping
  operator $a_T : F \to F$, $\pi_t \circ a_T = \pi_{t \wedge T}$, and
  $\Filt^\circ_T = a_T^{-1}(\mathcal{S})$.  Equivalently, a set lies in
  $\Filt^\circ_T$ exactly when membership depends on the path only through $a_T$.
  This is \JS, Lemma~III.2.43 for the canonical space, and it is what ``strict''
