  buys.  Note the consequence $T \circ a_T = T$: a strict stopping time is
  determined by the stopped path.
\end{definition}

The second ingredient is a supply of solutions that continue a given past.  In
the Markovian case one takes the restarted process to depend on the current
\emph{state}; the whole point of what follows is that it may depend on the whole
\emph{stopped path}, and that nothing is lost by letting it.

\begin{definition}[Restart kernel; \eqref{T2b}]
  \label{def:restartkernel}
  Let $T$ be a strict stopping time.  A \emph{restart kernel at $T$} is a kernel
  $\alpha \mapsto Q_\alpha$ from $(F,\Filt^\circ_T)$ to $(F,\mathcal{S})$ such that
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(R\arabic*)]
  \item \label{it:R1k}
    $Q_\alpha\{\beta \in F : a_T\beta = a_T\alpha\} = 1$ --- the past is kept;
  \item \label{it:R2k}
    for every $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ and all $T(\alpha) \leq s \leq t$,
    $E^{Q_\alpha}[Y^\circ_t \mid \Filt^\circ_s] = Y^\circ_s$ --- it is a solution
    from $T$ onwards, in the sense of Lemma~\ref{lem:restartmemory}.
  \end{enumerate}
  By \ref{it:R1k} and Definition~\ref{def:pasting}, $T$ is $Q_\alpha$-a.s.\ equal
  to the constant $T(\alpha)$, so the times in \ref{it:R2k} are deterministic:
  indeed $T \circ a_T = T$ gives $T(\beta) = T(a_T\beta) = T(a_T\alpha) = T(\alpha)$
  on the set of full measure in \ref{it:R1k}.  Two further points.  First, the
  event in \ref{it:R1k} is measurable: $\{(\alpha,\beta) : a_T\alpha = a_T\beta\}$
  is the preimage of the diagonal of $F \times F$ under $a_T \times a_T$, and the
  diagonal is measurable because $\mathcal{S}$ is countably generated and
  separating --- which is \eqref{E1}.  Second, \ref{it:R2k} is a martingale property
  only \emph{from $T(\alpha)$ onwards}; nothing is asked of $Y^\circ$ before that
  time, where the path is frozen at $a_T\alpha$ anyway.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Pasting with memory; \eqref{T2b}]
  \label{lem:pasting}
  Let $T$ be a strict stopping time, let $P \in \Msol(\XX^{\circ,T})$ and let
  $(Q_\alpha)$ be a restart kernel at $T$.  Assume the integrability condition
  \[
    \int_F E^{Q_\alpha}\bigl[ \lvert Y^\circ_t \rvert \bigr] \, P(\dif\alpha)
      \;<\; \infty
    \qquad\text{for every } Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ \text{ and } t \in \T .
  \]
  Then
  \[
    Q \;\coloneqq\; \int_F Q_\alpha \, P(\dif\alpha) \;\in\; \Msol(\XX^\circ) ,
    \qquad\text{and}\qquad Q = P \ \text{ on } \Filt^\circ_T .
  \]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \emph{Agreement on $\Filt^\circ_T$.}  Let $A \in \Filt^\circ_T$.  By
  Definition~\ref{def:pasting}, $\one_A = \one_A \circ a_T$, and by \ref{it:R1k},
  $Q_\alpha(A) = \one_A(\alpha)$.  Hence $Q(A) = P(A)$.

  \emph{The martingale property.}  Fix $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$, $s \leq t$ and a
  bounded $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable $W$, and split as before,
  \[
    Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s
      = \underbrace{\bigl( Y^{\circ,T}_t - Y^{\circ,T}_s \bigr)}_{(\mathrm{I})}
      + \underbrace{\bigl( Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_{t \wedge T} \bigr)
                    - \bigl( Y^\circ_s - Y^\circ_{s \wedge T} \bigr)}_{(\mathrm{II})} .
  \]

  \emph{The term $(\mathrm{I})$.}  We claim that for every $\alpha$
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:pastingI}
    E^{Q_\alpha}\bigl[ (\mathrm{I}) W \bigr]
      \;=\; (\mathrm{I})(\alpha) \, W(\alpha) \, \one_{\{T(\alpha) > s\}} .
  \end{equation}
  Fix $\alpha$ and recall from Definition~\ref{def:restartkernel} that
  $Q_\alpha$-a.s.\ $a_T\beta = a_T\alpha$ and $T(\beta) = T(\alpha)$.

  If $T(\alpha) \leq s$, then $Q_\alpha$-a.s.\
  $(\mathrm{I})(\beta) = Y^\circ_{T(\beta)}(\beta) - Y^\circ_{T(\beta)}(\beta) = 0$
  and both sides of \eqref{eq:pastingI} vanish.

  If $T(\alpha) > s$, then $Q_\alpha$-a.s.\ $W(\beta) = W(\alpha)$, because $W$ is
  $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable, hence a function of $\beta$ restricted to
  $\T_{\leq s} \subset \T_{\leq T(\beta)}$, on which $\beta$ and $\alpha$ agree.
  Likewise $(\mathrm{I})(\beta) = (\mathrm{I})(\alpha)$, because $Y^\circ$ is
  adapted, so $Y^\circ_{u \wedge T}$ is a function of the path on
  $\T_{\leq T}$, i.e.\ of $a_T$.  This proves \eqref{eq:pastingI}.

  Integrating \eqref{eq:pastingI} over $P(\dif\alpha)$ and using that
  $\one_{\{T > s\}}W$ is bounded and $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable --- $\{T > s\}$
  lies in $\Filt^\circ_s$ because $T$ is a stopping time --- we get
  $E^Q[(\mathrm{I})W] = E^P[(Y^{\circ,T}_t - Y^{\circ,T}_s)\one_{\{T>s\}}W] = 0$
  because $Y^{\circ,T}$ is a $P$-martingale.

  \emph{The term $(\mathrm{II})$.}  Fix $\alpha$ and write $r = T(\alpha)$, which
  by Definition~\ref{def:restartkernel} is the $Q_\alpha$-a.s.\ value of $T$.
  \begin{itemize}
  \item If $r \leq s$, then $(\mathrm{II}) = Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s$ and $W$ is
    bounded and $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable with $s \geq r$, so \ref{it:R2k} gives
    $E^{Q_\alpha}[(\mathrm{II})W] = 0$.
  \item If $r > s$, then $(\mathrm{II}) = Y^\circ_{t} - Y^\circ_{t \wedge r}$,
    which vanishes for $t \leq r$; for $t > r$ it is $Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_r$, and
    $W$ is $\Filt^\circ_r$-measurable on this event because $s < r$ and
    $\Filt^\circ_s \subset \Filt^\circ_r$.  Again \ref{it:R2k}, now at the time
    $r$, gives $E^{Q_\alpha}[(\mathrm{II})W] = 0$.
  \end{itemize}
  Integrating over $P(\dif\alpha)$ gives $E^Q[(\mathrm{II})W] = 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Uniqueness implies local uniqueness; \JS, Theorem III.2.40]
  \label{thm:localuniqueness}
  Suppose that a restart kernel exists at every strict stopping time, and that
  $\Msol(\XX^\circ,\mu)$ contains at most one element for every
  $\mu \in \Prob(E)$.  Then local uniqueness holds in the sense of
  Definition~\ref{def:localuniq}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Let $T$ be a strict stopping time and $P \in \Msol(\XX^{\circ,T})$ with
  $P\pi_0^{-1} = \mu$.  Lemma~\ref{lem:pasting} produces
  $Q \in \Msol(\XX^\circ)$ with $Q = P$ on $\Filt^\circ_T$; in particular
  $Q\pi_0^{-1} = \mu$, since $\pi_0$ is $\Filt^\circ_T$-measurable.  By hypothesis
  $Q$ is the unique element of $\Msol(\XX^\circ,\mu)$, hence depends on $\mu$
  alone; therefore so does $P|_{\Filt^\circ_T} = Q|_{\Filt^\circ_T}$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The Markovian case]
  \label{cor:pastingmarkov}
  Let $(\XX^\circ_r)$ be a shift system, measurable in the sense of
  Definition~\ref{def:shiftstable} and \emph{full}, i.e.\ satisfying in addition
  that for every $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$ and $r$ there are
  $\hat{Y}^\circ \in \XX^\circ_r$ and an $\Filt^\circ_r$-measurable $\kappa$ with
  $\hat{Y}^\circ_u \circ \theta_r = Y^\circ_{r+u} - Y^\circ_r + \kappa$.  Let
  $(P_{x,r})$ be a measurable family with $P_{x,r} \in \Msol(\XX^\circ_r,\delta_x)$,
  and suppose $F$ admits concatenation at $T$: a measurable
  $\gamma(\alpha,\beta)$ agreeing with $\alpha$ before $T(\alpha)$, with
  $\beta$ shifted after it, and with $\theta_T\gamma(\alpha,\beta) = \beta$.  Then
  \[
    Q_\alpha \;=\; \text{law of } \gamma(\alpha,\beta) ,
    \qquad \beta \sim P_{\alpha_{T(\alpha)},\, T(\alpha)} ,
  \]
  is a restart kernel at $T$, and Theorem~\ref{thm:localuniqueness} applies.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  \ref{it:R1k} holds because $a_T\gamma(\alpha,\beta) = a_T\alpha$.  For
  \ref{it:R2k} write $r = T(\alpha)$.  Fullness gives
  $\hat{Y}^\circ \in \XX^\circ_r$ and an $\Filt^\circ_r$-measurable $\kappa$ with
  $Y^\circ_{r+u} - Y^\circ_r = \hat{Y}^\circ_u \circ \theta_r - \kappa$.  Under
  $Q_\alpha$ the shifted path $\theta_r$ has law $P_{\alpha_r,r}$, under which
  $\hat{Y}^\circ$ is a martingale for $(\Filt^\circ_u)_u$, and $\kappa$ is
  $Q_\alpha$-a.s.\ the constant $\kappa(\alpha)$, being
  $\Filt^\circ_r$-measurable and hence a function of $a_T$.

  It remains to transport the filtrations.  Under $Q_\alpha$ the path on
  $\T_{\leq r}$ is $Q_\alpha$-a.s.\ the deterministic $a_T\alpha$, so for $u \in \T$
  the $\sigma$-field $\Filt^\circ_{r+u}$ coincides $Q_\alpha$-a.s.\ with
  $\theta_r^{-1}\Filt^\circ_u$: the generators $\pi_v$ with $v \leq r+u$ are
  either deterministic ($v \leq r$) or of the form $\pi_w \circ \theta_r$ with
  $w = v - r \leq u$, the latter by Lemma~\ref{lem:shiftembed}.  Hence for
  $r \leq s \leq t$, writing $s = r + u$ and $t = r + v$,
  \[
    E^{Q_\alpha}\bigl[ Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s \bigm| \Filt^\circ_s \bigr]
      = E^{P_{\alpha_r,r}}\bigl[ \hat{Y}^\circ_v - \hat{Y}^\circ_u
          \bigm| \Filt^\circ_u \bigr] \circ \theta_r
      = 0 ,
  \]
  which is \ref{it:R2k}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What memory buys, and what it does not]
  \label{rem:pastingassumed}
  \label{rem:localuniqcomment}
  Three comments.

  \emph{The shift has left the statement.}  Definition~\ref{def:restartkernel},
  Lemma~\ref{lem:pasting} and Theorem~\ref{thm:localuniqueness} mention no
  $\theta_r$, no shifted family and no concatenation; the restarted measures are
  indexed by the \emph{stopped path}, not by the state, and condition
  \ref{it:R2k} is exactly the conclusion of Lemma~\ref{lem:restartmemory}.  The
  Markovian version, with a state-indexed kernel and concatenation, is now the
  corollary it should be.  The proof is also shorter: because a strict stopping
  time satisfies $T \circ a_T = T$, it is $Q_\alpha$-a.s.\ \emph{deterministic},
  so no optional sampling and no case analysis with $\theta_T$ are needed.

  \emph{What is genuinely assumed.}  The existence of a restart kernel is a
  hypothesis and cannot be otherwise.  Unlike the restart of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:restartmemory}, which merely reweights a solution one already
  has, pasting must \emph{supply} solutions after $T$ for every possible past;
  and the conditional distributions of a solution of the full problem are of no
  use here, since producing such a solution is the point.  This is the one place
  in Section~\ref{sec:uniqueness} where something must be given from outside.

  \emph{Why strict stopping times.}  Definition~\ref{def:pasting} is used twice:
  to know that $\Filt^\circ_T$-measurable functions see only $a_T$, and to know
  that $T$ is determined by the stopped path.  For the raw filtration
  $\Filt^\circ_t = \sigma(\pi_u : u \leq t)$ this is \JS, Lemma~III.2.43; for a
  completed or right continuous filtration it is false, which is the practical
  reason for \JS's insistence on the distinction.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Non-Markovian test objects}
\label{ssec:testobjects}

The point of the arrangement above is that it can be tested.  We run it against
the two examples of \CPS{} that are not Markovian, and record for each which
results apply and which fail.

\begin{example}[Volterra equations; \CPS, Example 3.13]
  \label{ex:volterra}
  Let $F = L^p_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Rp,\R^d) \times D_{\R^k}[0,\infty)$ with
  coordinate process $(\pi^X,\pi^Z)$, let $K : \Rp \to \R^{d \times k}$ be a
  convolution kernel and $g_0 : \Rp \to \R^d$, and let
  \[
    L f(x,z) = \langle b(x), \nabla f(z) \rangle
      + \tfrac{1}{2}\operatorname{tr}\bigl( a(x) \nabla^2 f(z) \bigr)
      + \int \bigl( f(z+y) - f(z) - \langle h(y), \nabla f(z) \rangle \bigr)
        \nu(x,\dif y) .
  \]
  Take for $\XX^\circ$ the processes
  $f(\pi^Z_t) - \int_0^t Lf(\pi^X_s,\pi^Z_s)\dif s$, $f \in C^2_b(\R^k)$,
  together with the requirement that
  $\int_0^t \pi^X_s \dif s = \int_0^t g_0(s)\dif s + \int_0^t K_{t-s}\pi^Z_s\dif s$
  for all $t$.  Then $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$ is the set of laws of
  solutions of the Volterra equation.

  This is not a Markov problem, and not by accident: the kernel $K_{t-s}$ makes
  $\pi^X_t$ depend on the whole past of $\pi^Z$.  In the language of
  Section~\ref{ssec:markovlayer}, \emph{there is no shift system}: shifting by $r$
  turns $K_{t-s}$ into a kernel that also sees $\pi^Z$ before $r$, which no
  member of a family $\XX^\circ_r$ of the form required by
  Definition~\ref{def:shiftstable} can reproduce.  What survives is exactly what
  Section~\ref{ssec:absuniq} proves:
  \begin{itemize}
  \item Definitions~\ref{def:absMP} and \ref{def:canonical} apply as they stand,
    on a path space that is not $\DE$ and on which $\pi^X$ is not even right
    continuous;
  \item Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture} (mixtures) and Lemma~\ref{lem:disint}
    (disintegration) apply, the latter because $F$ is Polish, hence \eqref{E1};
  \item \textbf{Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop} applies}, provided the
    Volterra equation is uniquely solvable in law \emph{from any prescribed past}
    --- which under Lipschitz conditions on $b,a,\nu$ it is, by the Picard
    iteration, since the kernel term $\int_{[0,t)}K_{t-s}\dif Z_s$ splits into a
    known contribution from the past and a contraction on the future.  That is
    \eqref{eq:propagation}, and Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop} turns it into
    uniqueness of the whole law with no Markov structure.  Plain uniqueness in law
    would not suffice; see Remark~\ref{rem:whyZ};
  \item Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} applies, and \CPS, Section~4.2 is precisely a
    stability theorem for Volterra equations obtained from it;
  \item Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq} does \emph{not} apply, and the conclusion is
    false: a Volterra process is not Markov.  Nor do
    Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov} or Section~\ref{sec:duality}, both of which
    use the shift.  Section~\ref{ssec:localuniq} applies as soon as a restart
    kernel is available.
  \end{itemize}
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Semimartingales with path-dependent characteristics]
  \label{ex:pathdepsemi}
  Let $F = \DE$ and let $(B,C,\nu)$ be a triplet of \emph{predictable functionals
  of the path}, as in \JS, Chapter~III.  The associated $\XX^\circ$ consists of
  the processes of \JS, III.2.7, and $\Msol_{\mathrm{loc}}(\XX^\circ)$ is the set
  of laws of semimartingales with these characteristics.  Again there is no shift
  system unless the triplet happens to be shift covariant in the sense of
  \JS, III.2.39, which for a genuinely path-dependent triplet it is not.

  Here Section~\ref{ssec:localmp} is available in full --- the localizing system
  of Lemma~\ref{lem:L1auto} is constructed from the test processes themselves and
  needs no shift, which is exactly the argument \JS{} give in their III.2.8 ---
  and so is Section~\ref{ssec:localuniq}, provided a restart kernel
  (Definition~\ref{def:restartkernel}) can be exhibited.  For a path-dependent
  triplet that is a genuine requirement rather than a formality: one must produce,
  for every stopped path, a semimartingale continuing it with the prescribed
  characteristics.  But it is a requirement on the \emph{data}, not a Markov
  hypothesis, and \JS{} verify it in their setting.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Where the Markov structure is used, in one place]
  \label{rem:markovaudit}
  Collecting the two examples with the results of
  Sections~\ref{sec:cadlag}--\ref{sec:convergence}:

  \medskip
  \noindent
  \begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.50\textwidth}p{0.42\textwidth}@{}}
  \toprule
  \textbf{Needs no Markov structure} & \textbf{Needs a shift system} \\
  \midrule
  Definitions~\ref{def:absMP}, \ref{def:canonical}, \ref{def:regclass}
    & Definition~\ref{def:shiftstable} \\
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absreg} (c\`adl\`ag modification)
    & Lemma~\ref{lem:propagation} \\
  Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture}, Lemma~\ref{lem:disint}
    & Example~\ref{ex:shiftXA} \\
  Lemma~\ref{lem:restartmemory} (\textbf{restart with memory})
    & Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq} (Markov property) \\
  Definition~\ref{def:propagation}, Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop}
    (\textbf{uniqueness}) & Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} (restart with re-indexing) \\
  Definition~\ref{def:localizing}, Lemma~\ref{lem:L1auto},
    Lemma~\ref{lem:localmix} & Lemma~\ref{lem:localrestart},
    Theorem~\ref{thm:localuniq} \\
  Lemma~\ref{lem:chain} (chain identity)
    & Corollary~\ref{cor:pastingmarkov} \\
  Definition~\ref{def:restartkernel}, Lemma~\ref{lem:pasting},
    Theorem~\ref{thm:localuniqueness} (\textbf{local uniqueness})
    & --- \\
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}, Theorem~\ref{thm:clockchange}
    & Proposition~\ref{prop:haar} and Section~\ref{ssec:dualcont} \\
  \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}

  \medskip
  \noindent
  The left column is the whole of Sections~\ref{sec:MP} and
  \ref{sec:convergence}, all of Section~\ref{sec:cadlag}, the uniqueness half of
  Section~\ref{sec:uniqueness} and the analytic core of
  Section~\ref{sec:duality}.  The right column is: the shift system and what is
  built on it.  Every entry there produces a statement whose \emph{conclusion} is
  Markovian --- the Markov property, the reduction to one-dimensional
  distributions, pasting, duality --- so nothing in it could have been avoided by
  a better argument.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The Markovian case}
\label{ssec:uniqmarkov}

\begin{theorem}[\EK, Theorem 4.4.2; \eqref{T2b} + \eqref{T4} + shift invariant clock, for homogeneity]
  \label{thm:uniqueness}
  Assume \eqref{E2} and let $A \subset \Bdd(E) \times \Bdd(E)$.  Suppose that
  for each $\mu \in \Prob(E)$ any two solutions $X$, $Y$ of the martingale
  problem for $(A,\mu)$ have the same one-dimensional distributions, that is,
  for each $t \in \T$,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:onedim}
    P\{X(t) \in \Gamma\} \;=\; P\{Y(t) \in \Gamma\},
    \qquad \Gamma \in \Bor(E) .
  \end{equation}
  Then the following hold.
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item \label{it:uniq_a}
    Any solution of the martingale problem for $A$ with respect to a filtration
    $(\Gilt_t)$ is a Markov process with respect to $(\Gilt_t)$, and any two
    solutions of the martingale problem for $(A,\mu)$ have the same
    finite-dimensional distributions.  In particular, \eqref{eq:onedim} implies
    uniqueness.
  \item \label{it:uniq_b}
    If moreover $A \subset \Cb(E) \times \Bdd(E)$, $X$ is a solution of the
    martingale problem for $A$ with respect to $(\Gilt_t)$, and $X$ has sample
    paths in $\DE$, then for every a.s.\ finite $(\Gilt_t)$-stopping time $\tau$,
    \begin{equation}
      \label{eq:strongmarkov}
      E\bigl[ f(X(\tau + t)) \bigm| \Gilt_\tau \bigr]
        \;=\; E\bigl[ f(X(\tau + t)) \bigm| X(\tau) \bigr] ,
      \qquad f \in \Bdd(E),\ t \in \T .
    \end{equation}
  \item \label{it:uniq_c}
    If, in addition to the conditions of \ref{it:uniq_b}, for each $x \in E$
    there exists a solution $P_x \in \Prob(\DE)$ of the $\DE$ martingale problem
    for $(A,\delta_x)$ such that $x \mapsto P_x(B)$ is Borel measurable for each
    $B \in \Bor(\DE)$, then, setting
    $T(t) f(x) = \int f(\omega(t)) P_x(\dif\omega)$,
    \begin{equation}
      \label{eq:strongmarkov2}
      E\bigl[ f(X(\tau + t)) \bigm| \Gilt_\tau \bigr] \;=\; T(t) f(X(\tau))
    \end{equation}
    for all $f \in \Bdd(E)$, $t \in \T$ and a.s.\ finite $(\Gilt_t)$-stopping
    times $\tau$; that is, $X$ is strong Markov.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  We check the hypotheses of Theorems~\ref{thm:absuniq} and
  \ref{thm:absstrongmarkov} for $\XX^\circ = \XX_A$, i.e.\ for
  \[
    \XX^\circ = \Bigl\{ \bigl( f(\pi_t)
      - \int_{\langle 0,t \rangle_\iota} g(\pi_s)\, q(\dif s)
      \bigr)_{t \in \T} \;:\; (f,g) \in A \Bigr\}
  \]
  on the path space $F = E^{\T}$ (respectively $F = \DE$ in
  \ref{it:uniq_b}--\ref{it:uniq_c}).

  \emph{Determining set.}  Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar} says precisely that the
  products $\prod_{k} h_k(\pi_{t_k})$, over arbitrary finite subsets
  $\{t_k\} \subset \T_{\leq s}$, form a determining set for $\XX_A$ in the sense
  of Definition~\ref{def:canonical}: \eqref{eq:fdd} for all such products is
  equivalent to the martingale property.  Note that this is stronger than what
  Definition~\ref{def:canonical} asks --- it delivers the martingale property for
  ${}^{*}\Filt^X$, not merely for the canonical filtration.

  \emph{Shift system.}  Example~\ref{ex:shiftXA} exhibits one, with
  $\theta_r\omega = \omega(r+\cdot)$, the pulled-back clock $q_r$ and
  $\kappa = f(\pi_r)$.  Since $A \subset \Cb(E) \times \Bdd(E)$ does not depend on
  time and $q$ is assumed shift invariant, $q_r = q$ and $\XX^\circ_r = \XX^\circ$
  for every $r$; the shift system is the constant one, and the Markov process
  obtained is homogeneous.  Dropping either assumption leaves
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq} intact and yields a time-inhomogeneous Markov process
  instead --- this is the only point at which \EK, Theorem~4.4.2 is genuinely less
  general than Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}.

  \emph{Integrability.}  $f$ and $g$ are bounded, so every $Y^\circ \in \XX^\circ$
  satisfies
  $|Y^\circ_t| \leq \lVert f \rVert + \lVert g \rVert \, q(\T_{\leq t})$, which is
  finite by Definition~\ref{def:clock}; all the integrability provisos are
  automatic, and the increments of $Y^\circ$ on $\T_{\leq T}$ are bounded, hence
  uniformly integrable.

  \emph{Conclusion.}  Hypothesis \eqref{eq:onedim} is \eqref{eq:absonedim} by
  Remark~\ref{rem:filtirrelevant}, so
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}\ref{it:absuniq_a} gives the Markov property and
  \ref{it:absuniq_b} the equality of the finite-dimensional distributions, which
  is \ref{it:uniq_a}.  For \ref{it:uniq_b} and \ref{it:uniq_c}, $A \subset
  \Cb(E)\times\Bdd(E)$ and paths in $\DE$ make each $Y^\circ(X)$ a right
  continuous martingale whose increment over
  $\langle \tau+s,\tau+t \rangle_\iota$ is bounded by
  $2\lVert f\rVert + \lVert g\rVert q(\langle s,t\rangle_\iota)$, the clock
  being shift invariant here; so \eqref{eq:optafterint} is automatic and
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov} applies; its second assertion is
  \eqref{eq:strongmarkov2} with $T(t)f(x) = \int f(\omega(t)) P_x(\dif\omega)$.
  Its restriction to stopping times with countably many values is vacuous here:
  the shift system is the constant one, so all the measures $P_{x,r} = P_x$ solve
  one and the same problem and the partition of Step~3 there is unnecessary.  See
  Remark~\ref{rem:strongmarkovscope}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[\EK, Corollary 4.4.3; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{cor:DEuniqueness}
  Assume \eqref{E3} and let $A \subset \Bdd(E) \times \Bdd(E)$.  Suppose that for
  each $\mu \in \Prob(E)$ any two solutions $X$, $Y$ of the martingale problem
  for $(A,\mu)$ with sample paths in $\DE$ (respectively $\CE$) satisfy
  \eqref{eq:onedim}.  Then for each $\mu \in \Prob(E)$ any two such solutions
  have the same distribution on $\DE$ (respectively $\CE$).
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  The processes $\tilde X$, $\tilde Y$ constructed in the proof of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:uniqueness} again have sample paths in $\DE$ (resp.\ $\CE$)
  whenever $X$ and $Y$ do, so the induction goes through and $X$, $Y$ have the
  same finite-dimensional distributions.  By Theorem~\ref{thm:fdd} these determine
  the law on $\DE$ (resp.\ $\CE$).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:uniquelocal}
  For the \emph{local} martingale problem, Theorem~\ref{thm:uniqueness} holds in
  the following localized form.  Suppose $A \subset \Meas(E) \times \Meas(E)$ and
  let $(K_m)$ be an increasing sequence of compact sets with
  $\tau_m = \inf\{t : X(t) \notin K_m \text{ or } X(t-) \notin K_m\}$ and
  $\tau_m \to \infty$ a.s.\ for every solution $X$.  If for each $\mu$ and each
  $m$ any two solutions of the local martingale problem for $(A,\mu)$ agree in
  the one-dimensional distributions of the \emph{stopped} processes
  $X(\cdot \wedge \tau_m)$, then the conclusions \ref{it:uniq_a}--\ref{it:uniq_c}
  hold.  The proof is the above one applied to the stopped martingale problem for
  $A^{(m)} = \{(f \one_{K_m}, g \one_{K_m}) : (f,g) \in A\}$, followed by
  $m \to \infty$.  This is the content of \EK, Section~4.6.  It is worth stating
  separately in the formalization, since the reduction ``local $\rightsquigarrow$
  global via stopping'' is reused in Section~\ref{sec:convergence}.

  This route modifies the operator.  Section~\ref{ssec:localmp} gives the
  alternative, which leaves $A$ alone and stops the test process instead; it is
  the one that fits the abstract setting, and the one \KA{} uses.
\end{remark}

% =====================================================================
\section{Uniqueness via duality}
\label{sec:duality}
% =====================================================================

Duality reduces the computation of $E[f(X(t),y)]$ for a process $X$ to the
computation of $E[f(x,Y(t))]$ for a second, typically simpler, process $Y$.
Combined with Theorem~\ref{thm:uniqueness} it is one of the main practical
routes to uniqueness.

Here, unlike in Sections~\ref{sec:cadlag} and \ref{sec:uniqueness}, there is
nothing to strip away on the probabilistic side: Theorem~\ref{thm:duality} below
already assumes only that $X$ and $Y$ are \emph{measurable} processes, uses no
path regularity, no Polish structure and no boundedness, and would be stated
verbatim in the \CPS{} setting.  What can be generalized is the \emph{clock}.
Section~\ref{ssec:antidiag} therefore isolates the analytic core as a statement
about a function of two time variables, valid for an arbitrary measure
$q$, and reads off exactly which $q$ make the duality relation true.  The answer
turns out to be sharp, and it is not the answer one gets in
Sections~\ref{sec:cadlag} and \ref{sec:uniqueness}.

\subsection{The anti-diagonal lemma}
\label{ssec:antidiag}

Duality is the assertion that a certain function of two time variables is
constant on anti-diagonals.  The following identity is that assertion stripped of
all analysis: it is an exact telescoping, with no hypothesis beyond the two
increment representations, and every version of the duality lemma is obtained
from it by choosing a chain.

\begin{lemma}[Chain identity; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E0} + clock]
  \label{lem:chain}
  Let $\T$ be a preordered set with a least element $0$, let $q$ be a measure on
  $\T$ for which the sets $[s,s') \coloneqq \T_{<s'} \setminus \T_{<s}$ are
  measurable, and let $\Phi, \gamma_1, \gamma_2 : \T \times \T \to \R$ satisfy,
  for all $s \leq s'$ and $t \leq t'$,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:incrementrep}
    \Phi(s',t) - \Phi(s,t) = \int_{[s,s')} \gamma_1(r,t) \, q(\dif r),
    \qquad
    \Phi(s,t') - \Phi(s,t) = \int_{[t,t')} \gamma_2(s,r) \, q(\dif r),
  \end{equation}
  all integrals being assumed to exist.  Let $t \in \T$ and let
  \[
    0 = s_0 \leq s_1 \leq \dots \leq s_m = t ,
    \qquad
    t = t_0 \geq t_1 \geq \dots \geq t_m = 0
  \]
  be a \emph{staircase} from $(0,t)$ to $(t,0)$.  Then
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:chain}
    \Phi(t,0) - \Phi(0,t)
      = \sum_{k=0}^{m-1} \Bigl[
          \int_{[s_k,s_{k+1})} \! \gamma_1(r,\, t_{k+1}) \, q(\dif r)
          \;-\;
          \int_{[t_{k+1},\, t_k)} \! \gamma_2(s_k,\, r) \, q(\dif r)
        \Bigr] .
  \end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Telescope along the staircase:
  $\Phi(t,0) - \Phi(0,t)
    = \sum_{k} \bigl[ \Phi(s_{k+1}, t_{k+1}) - \Phi(s_k, t_k) \bigr]$, the sum
  collapsing because $(s_0,t_0) = (0,t)$ and $(s_m,t_m) = (t,0)$.  Split each
  summand through the corner $(s_k, t_{k+1})$:
  \[
    \bigl[ \Phi(s_{k+1},t_{k+1}) - \Phi(s_k,t_{k+1}) \bigr]
    - \bigl[ \Phi(s_k,t_k) - \Phi(s_k,t_{k+1}) \bigr] .
  \]
  Now apply the first representation in \eqref{eq:incrementrep} to the first
  bracket, which is legitimate because $s_k \leq s_{k+1}$, and the second to the
  second, legitimate because $t_{k+1} \leq t_k$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the staircase form buys]
  \label{rem:staircase}
  The version of \eqref{eq:chain} one meets in the literature takes
  $t_k = t - s_k$, the \emph{anti-diagonal} staircase, and therefore needs \eqref{T4}
  so that $t - s_k$ is defined.  Nothing in the proof requires this: the two
  coordinates may be stepped through independently, and the identity is then a
  statement about an arbitrary preorder with a clock.  Which staircase one
  chooses is the whole art of the section, and the point of
Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless} below is that the anti-diagonal one is rarely the
  right choice: the correct staircase is the one that is anti-diagonal \emph{in
  clock time} --- available whenever the clock is atomless, and, as
  Remark~\ref{rem:atomsnotchange} shows, genuinely obstructed by atoms.
\end{remark}

The duality relation is the case in which the two brackets in \eqref{eq:chain}
cancel.  Under the balance condition $\gamma_1 = \gamma_2 = \gamma$ the $k$-th
summand is
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:cancel}
  \int_{[s_k,s_{k+1})} \gamma(r,\, t_{k+1}) \, q(\dif r)
  \;-\;
  \int_{[t_{k+1},\,t_k)} \gamma(s_k,\, r) \, q(\dif r) ,
\end{equation}
in which the two integrals run over the block $[s_k,s_{k+1})$ and over the
partner block $[t_{k+1},t_k)$.  So the duality relation holds as soon as the
staircase can be chosen so that a block and its partner receive the same
$q$-mass, at every scale.  On the anti-diagonal staircase $t_k = t - s_k$ that is
translation invariance of $q$; but the anti-diagonal staircase is not forced, and
it is not the right one.

\begin{proposition}[Haar clocks; \eqref{T2a} + \eqref{T4}, $\iota = \mathrm{p}$]
  \label{prop:haar}
  Assume \eqref{eq:incrementrep} with $\gamma_1 = \gamma_2 = \gamma$.
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item \label{it:haar_disc}
    If $\T = \N_0$ and $q$ is counting measure, then $\Phi(t,0) = \Phi(0,t)$ for
    every $t$, for every $\gamma$ whatsoever, and no integrability hypothesis is
    needed.
  \item \label{it:haar_cont}
    If $\T = \Rp$ and $q$ is Lebesgue measure, then $\Phi(t,0) = \Phi(0,t)$ for
    almost every $t$, under the integrability hypothesis \eqref{eq:calcint};
    this is Lemma~\ref{lem:calculus} below.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  \ref{it:haar_disc}  Take the anti-diagonal staircase $s_k = k$,
  $t_k = t - k$.  Then $[s_k,s_{k+1}) = \{k\}$ and
  $[t_{k+1},t_k) = \{t-k-1\}$, so \eqref{eq:cancel} reads
  $\gamma(k,t-k-1) - \gamma(k,t-k-1) = 0$.  Every summand vanishes identically;
  no regularity of $\gamma$ is used.

  \ref{it:haar_cont}  Refine the anti-diagonal staircase; see
  Lemma~\ref{lem:calculus}.
\end{proof}

The two cases of Proposition~\ref{prop:haar} are the ones in the literature, and
they are the ones for which the anti-diagonal staircase works.  It is tempting to
conclude --- as an earlier version of this manuscript did --- that duality
\emph{requires} a translation invariant clock, on the grounds that
\eqref{eq:cancel} does not cancel otherwise.  That inference is invalid: the
failure of one staircase to cancel says nothing about $\Phi(t,0) - \Phi(0,t)$.
The two results below show that translation invariance is not necessary --- for
an atomless clock by a reduction to Lemma~\ref{lem:calculus}, for a purely
atomic one by the linear algebra of Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicdual}.  Whether
\emph{every} clock admits duality remains open, and
Remark~\ref{rem:atomsnotchange} explains where the two arguments stop.

\begin{corollary}[Atomless clocks, by time change; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E0} + clock, $\iota = \mathrm{p}$]
  \label{cor:atomless}
  Assume \eqref{T3} and let $q$ be an \emph{atomless} clock.  Let $\Phi,\gamma$ satisfy
  \eqref{eq:incrementrep} with $\gamma_1 = \gamma_2 = \gamma$, with $\gamma$
  measurable for the order $\sigma$-field in each variable --- automatic if
  $\mathcal{T}$ is taken to be that $\sigma$-field, which
  Definition~\ref{def:clock} permits.  Put $Q(s) = q(\T_{<s})$ and
  $Q^{\leftarrow}(z) = \sup\{t : Q(t) \leq z\}$.  If
  $\Psi = \Phi(Q^{\leftarrow}(\cdot),Q^{\leftarrow}(\cdot))$ and $\psi = \gamma(Q^{\leftarrow}(\cdot),Q^{\leftarrow}(\cdot))$
  satisfy the integrability hypothesis \eqref{eq:calcint}, then
  \[
    \Phi(t,0) \;=\; \Phi(0,t)
    \qquad\text{for $Q$-almost every } t \in \T .
  \]
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Since $q$ is atomless, $Q$ is continuous; by \eqref{T3} its domain is an interval, so
  $Q$ maps $\T_{\leq t^*}$ onto $[0,L]$, $L = Q(t^*)$, and $Q^{\leftarrow}$ is a right
  inverse: $Q(Q^{\leftarrow}(z)) = z$.  Monotonicity of $Q$ gives
  $Q^{\leftarrow}(z) \geq a \iff z \geq Q(a)$, whence the change-of-variables formula
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:quantile}
    \int_{[s,s')} g \, \dif q
      \;=\; \int_{[Q(s),\,Q(s'))} g\bigl(Q^{\leftarrow}(z)\bigr) \dif z
  \end{equation}
  for order-measurable $g$: for $g = \one_{[a,a')}$ both sides equal
  $Q(s'\wedge a') - Q(s\vee a)$, and Fact~\ref{fact:monotoneclass} does the rest.

  Now $q([s,s')) = 0$ forces $\Phi(s,\cdot) = \Phi(s',\cdot)$ as well as
  $\Phi(\cdot,s) = \Phi(\cdot,s')$, by \eqref{eq:incrementrep}; hence
  $\Psi(Q(s),Q(t)) = \Phi(s,t)$.  And for $x \leq x'$, writing
  $s = Q^{\leftarrow}(x)$ and $s' = Q^{\leftarrow}(x')$,
  \[
    \Psi(x',y) - \Psi(x,y)
      = \int_{[s,s')} \gamma\bigl(r,Q^{\leftarrow}(y)\bigr) q(\dif r)
      = \int_x^{x'} \psi(z,y) \dif z
  \]
  by \eqref{eq:quantile}, and symmetrically in $y$.  So $\Psi$ satisfies the
  hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:calculus} on $[0,L]^2$ with
  $\nabla\Psi = (\psi,\psi)$, which gives $\Psi(\ell,0) = \Psi(0,\ell)$ for
  almost every $\ell$; and $\Psi(L,0) = \Phi(t^*,0)$,
  $\Psi(0,L) = \Phi(0,t^*)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[How much this is, and how much it is not]
  \label{rem:atomsnotchange}
  Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless} is a change of variables and should be read as
  one.  Under \eqref{T3} an atomless clock is the image of Lebesgue measure under the
  continuous increasing map $\tau$, so a martingale problem with such a clock is
  a \emph{deterministic time change} of one with Lebesgue clock, and the
  corollary says no more than that duality is invariant under that time change.
  It is worth stating because it settles the question
  Proposition~\ref{prop:haar} raises --- which clocks admit duality --- for
  everything except atoms, and because the earlier version of this manuscript got
  that question wrong in the opposite direction.  It is not worth calling a
  theorem, and an intermediate version of this manuscript did, under the name
  ``every clock admits duality''.

  \emph{Atoms are not a change of variables, and this is not a gap in the
  argument.}  With an atom at $a$ the map $Q$ jumps, $\tau$ is no longer a right
  inverse, and the interval $[Q(a),Q(a)+q(\{a\})]$ has no preimage.  Filling it
  by interpolation --- which the intermediate version attempted --- is impossible.
  Take $\T = [0,2]$, $q = \delta_1$, $\iota = \mathrm{p}$, and
  $\Phi(s,t) = M(i(s),i(t))$ with $i(s) = \one_{\{s>1\}}$ and $M(0,0) = 0$,
  $M(1,0) = M(0,1) = c$, $M(1,1) = d$.  Both representations
  \eqref{eq:incrementrep} hold with the single function $\gamma$ pinned by
  $\gamma(1,t) = M(1,i(t)) - M(0,i(t))$ and
  $\gamma(s,1) = M(i(s),1) - M(i(s),0)$, consistently at $(1,1)$, and $d$ is
  \emph{free}; any extension to $[0,1]^2$ satisfying \eqref{eq:rectrepinline}
  below would force $d = 2c$.

  The reason is structural.  A pair $\Psi,\psi$ with
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:rectrepinline}
    \Psi(x',y) - \Psi(x,y) = \int_x^{x'} \!\psi(z,y)\dif z ,
    \qquad
    \Psi(x,y') - \Psi(x,y) = \int_y^{y'} \!\psi(x,z)\dif z
  \end{equation}
  satisfies $\partial_x\Psi = \partial_y\Psi$, hence is a function of $x+y$, and
  therefore has $\Psi(L,0) = \Psi(0,L)$ built in.  Producing one across a gap
  \emph{presupposes} the conclusion.  So the atomic case is not a harder instance
  of the same argument; it is a different question, and
  Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicdual} answers it by different means.

  \emph{Status of Section~\ref{ssec:antidiag}.}
  \begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{@{}ll@{}}
  \toprule
  \textbf{Clock} & \textbf{Status} \\
  \midrule
  Haar (Lebesgue, counting) & proved, Proposition~\ref{prop:haar} \\
  atomless, \eqref{T3} & proved, Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless} (a time change) \\
  purely atomic, atoms a chain & proved, Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicdual} \\
  purely atomic, atoms incomparable & verified symbolically; not proved \\
  order-dense atoms, or mixed & open \\
  \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  Nothing in this manuscript depends on the last two rows:
  Theorem~\ref{thm:duality} and Corollary~\ref{cor:uniqviadual} use Lebesgue
  measure, Corollary~\ref{cor:dualdiscrete} counting measure, and
  Section~\ref{ssec:exduality} uses only the additivity \eqref{eq:clockadd}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The role of Haar measure, correctly stated]
  \label{rem:haarrole}
  Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless} confirms that translation invariance of $q$ is not
  necessary for duality; it is a property of a particular \emph{proof}, namely it is what
  makes the anti-diagonal staircase $t_k = t - s_k$ coincide with the staircase
  that is anti-diagonal in clock time,
  \[
    Q(s_k) + Q(t_k) \;=\; Q(t) \qquad \text{for all } k ,
  \]
  which is the one \eqref{eq:cancel} actually wants.  Equivalently: a martingale
  problem with clock $q$ is a time change of one with Lebesgue clock, and duality
  is invariant under that time change.  This is the same phenomenon as in
  Section~\ref{ssec:diffclocks}, seen from the analytic side.

  Two consequences worth recording.  First, the conclusion is genuinely
  ``$Q$-almost every $t$'': on an interval where $q$ vanishes, $\Phi$ is constant
  in both arguments and there is nothing to prove.  Second, for a purely atomic
  clock --- the case Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless} excludes --- the conclusion
  holds at \emph{every} $t$ and without any integrability hypothesis, exactly as
  in Proposition~\ref{prop:haar}\ref{it:haar_disc}; see
  Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicdual}.  The two are complementary and neither
  covers a mixed clock.
\end{remark}

For a purely atomic clock there is a second, purely algebraic route to the
conclusion, and it is instructive because it does not go through
Lemma~\ref{lem:calculus} at all.  Its combinatorial core is the following
statement about a finite grid, in which the clock has become a list of masses and
nothing else is left of the index set.

\begin{lemma}[Atom grid]
  \label{lem:atomgrid}
  Let $M \geq 1$, let $m_1,\dots,m_{M-1}$ be non-zero reals, and let
  $\Phi : \{1,\dots,M\}^2 \to \R$ satisfy
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:atomgrid}
    m_j \bigl( \Phi(i{+}1,j) - \Phi(i,j) \bigr)
      \;=\; m_i \bigl( \Phi(i,j{+}1) - \Phi(i,j) \bigr) ,
    \qquad 1 \leq i,j \leq M-1 .
  \end{equation}
  Then $\Phi(i,j) = \Phi(j,i)$ for all $i,j$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Equation \eqref{eq:atomgrid} is linear in $\Phi$ and invariant under
  transposition: written at $(j,i)$ it reads
  $m_i(\Phi(j{+}1,i) - \Phi(j,i)) = m_j(\Phi(j,i{+}1) - \Phi(j,i))$, which is
  \eqref{eq:atomgrid} at $(i,j)$ for the function $(i,j) \mapsto \Phi(j,i)$.  So
  \[
    w(i,j) \;\coloneqq\; \Phi(i,j) - \Phi(j,i)
  \]
  satisfies \eqref{eq:atomgrid} as well, and $w(j,i) = -w(i,j)$.  We show
  $w \equiv 0$ by induction on the distance $d = |i-j|$ from the diagonal, the
  induction hypothesis being used at the two levels $d$ and $d-1$ at once.

  For $d = 0$, $w(i,i) = 0$ by antisymmetry.  For $d = 1$ and
  $1 \leq j \leq M-1$, equation \eqref{eq:atomgrid} at $(j,j)$ reads
  $m_j(w(j{+}1,j) - w(j,j)) = m_j(w(j,j{+}1) - w(j,j))$; since $m_j \neq 0$ and
  $w(j,j) = 0$ this gives $w(j{+}1,j) = w(j,j{+}1) = -w(j{+}1,j)$, hence
  $w(j{+}1,j) = w(j,j{+}1) = 0$.

  Let $d \geq 1$ and suppose $w$ vanishes at distances $d-1$ and $d$.  Let
  $j \geq 1$ with $j+d+1 \leq M$ and put $i = j+d$; then $i \leq M-1$ and
  $j \leq M-d-1 \leq M-1$, so \eqref{eq:atomgrid} is available at $(i,j)$ and
  reads
  \[
    m_j \bigl( w(j{+}d{+}1,\,j) - w(j{+}d,\,j) \bigr)
      \;=\; m_{j+d} \bigl( w(j{+}d,\,j{+}1) - w(j{+}d,\,j) \bigr) .
  \]
  Here $w(j{+}d,j) = 0$ at distance $d$, and $(j{+}d) - (j{+}1) = d-1$, so
  $w(j{+}d,j{+}1) = 0$ at distance $d-1$.  Hence $m_j\, w(j{+}d{+}1,j) = 0$, and
  $m_j \neq 0$ gives $w(j{+}d{+}1,j) = 0$; antisymmetry gives
  $w(j,j{+}d{+}1) = 0$.  Every pair at distance $d+1$ in $\{1,\dots,M\}^2$ is of
  this form, which completes the induction.
\end{proof}

The induction sweeps the \emph{diagonals} $i - j = d$ outwards, not the
anti-diagonals; that it needs two consecutive levels at once is why no single
staircase in \eqref{eq:chain} exhibits the cancellation --- a staircase sees one
anti-diagonal at a time.  Note also that positivity of the masses is nowhere
used, only $m_i \neq 0$, and that the list stops at $m_{M-1}$: the mass of the
last atom never enters.

\begin{proposition}[Atomic clocks; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E0} + clock, either $\iota$]
  \label{prop:atomicdual}
  Let $q = \sum_k m_k \delta_{a_k}$ be a purely atomic clock and let
  $\Phi, \gamma : \T \times \T \to \R$ satisfy \eqref{eq:incrementrep} with
  $\gamma_1 = \gamma_2 = \gamma$.  Let $t^* \in \T$ and assume that the atoms of
  $q$ in $\T_{\leq t^*}$ are finitely many and pairwise comparable, in the sense
  that of any two distinct such atoms $a,b$ either $a \in \T_{<b}$ or
  $b \in \T_{<a}$.  Then
  \[
    \Phi(t^*,0) \;=\; \Phi(0,t^*) .
  \]
  This holds at \emph{every} such $t^*$, for either convention $\iota$, and under
  no integrability hypothesis beyond the existence of the integrals in
  \eqref{eq:incrementrep} and no regularity of $\gamma$ whatsoever.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  Take $\iota = \mathrm{p}$ first.  Let $a_1,\dots,a_N$ be the atoms lying in
  $\T_{<t^*}$, indexed so that $a_i \in \T_{<a_j}$ for $i < j$, which the
  comparability hypothesis permits.  Put $u_0 = 0$, $u_i = a_i$ for
  $1 \leq i \leq N$ and $u_{M} = t^*$ with $M = N+1$; this is an increasing
  chain.  Since $\T_{<0} = \emptyset$, the set $[u_0,u_1)$ carries no atom, and
  for $1 \leq i \leq N$ the set $[u_i,u_{i+1}) = \T_{<u_{i+1}} \setminus
  \T_{<u_i}$ carries the single atom $a_i$: the atoms in $\T_{<a_{i+1}}$ are
  exactly $a_1,\dots,a_i$ and those in $\T_{<a_i}$ exactly $a_1,\dots,a_{i-1}$,
  and likewise with $a_{N+1}$ read as $t^*$.

  Write $\widehat\Phi(i,j) = \Phi(u_i,u_j)$ and
  $\widehat\gamma(i,j) = \gamma(u_i,u_j)$ on $\{0,\dots,M\}^2$, and set
  $\widehat m_0 = 0$, $\widehat m_i = m_i$ for $1 \leq i \leq N$.  Because
  $u_i = a_i$ carries the mass of the step from $u_i$ to $u_{i+1}$,
  \eqref{eq:incrementrep} becomes
  \[
    \widehat\Phi(i{+}1,j) - \widehat\Phi(i,j) = \widehat m_i\, \widehat\gamma(i,j) ,
    \qquad
    \widehat\Phi(i,j{+}1) - \widehat\Phi(i,j) = \widehat m_j\, \widehat\gamma(i,j)
  \]
  for $0 \leq i,j \leq M-1$.  Multiplying the first by $\widehat m_j$ and the
  second by $\widehat m_i$ makes both right-hand sides
  $\widehat m_i \widehat m_j \widehat\gamma(i,j)$, so $\widehat\Phi$ satisfies
  \eqref{eq:atomgrid} on $1 \leq i,j \leq M-1$ with the non-zero masses
  $\widehat m_1,\dots,\widehat m_{M-1}$; note that $\widehat\gamma$ has
  disappeared.  Lemma~\ref{lem:atomgrid} gives
  $\widehat\Phi(i,j) = \widehat\Phi(j,i)$ on $\{1,\dots,M\}^2$.  Finally
  $\widehat m_0 = 0$ gives $\widehat\Phi(1,j) = \widehat\Phi(0,j)$ and
  $\widehat\Phi(i,1) = \widehat\Phi(i,0)$, whence
  \[
    \Phi(t^*,0) = \widehat\Phi(M,0) = \widehat\Phi(M,1) = \widehat\Phi(1,M)
      = \widehat\Phi(0,M) = \Phi(0,t^*) .
  \]

  For $\iota = \mathrm{o}$ use the shorter chain $u_0 = 0$, $u_i = a_i$
  ($1 \leq i \leq N$), and put $M = N$.  Now $(u_{i-1},u_i] = \T_{\leq u_i}
  \setminus \T_{\leq u_{i-1}}$ carries the single atom $a_i$, so
  \eqref{eq:incrementrep} reads
  $\widehat\Phi(i,j) - \widehat\Phi(i{-}1,j) = m_i \widehat\gamma(i,j)$ and
  symmetrically, for $1 \leq i,j \leq M$, and eliminating $\widehat\gamma$ as
  before gives
  \[
    m_j \bigl( \widehat\Phi(i,j) - \widehat\Phi(i{-}1,j) \bigr)
      = m_i \bigl( \widehat\Phi(i,j) - \widehat\Phi(i,j{-}1) \bigr) ,
    \qquad 1 \leq i,j \leq M .
  \]
  Reflect the grid: $\Phi^{\sharp}(i,j) = \widehat\Phi(M{-}i,M{-}j)$ and
  $m^{\sharp}_i = m_{M-i}$.  Substituting $(M{-}i,M{-}j)$ for $(i,j)$ turns the
  display into $m^{\sharp}_j(\Phi^{\sharp}(i{+}1,j) - \Phi^{\sharp}(i,j)) =
  m^{\sharp}_i(\Phi^{\sharp}(i,j{+}1) - \Phi^{\sharp}(i,j))$ for
  $0 \leq i,j \leq M-1$, which is \eqref{eq:atomgrid} on the shifted index
  $\{1,\dots,M{+}1\}$ with the non-zero masses
  $m^{\sharp}_0,\dots,m^{\sharp}_{M-1} = m_M,\dots,m_1$.  Lemma~\ref{lem:atomgrid}
  makes $\Phi^{\sharp}$, hence $\widehat\Phi$, symmetric on $\{0,\dots,M\}^2$; in
  particular $\widehat\Phi(M,0) = \widehat\Phi(0,M)$.  Since $(a_N,t^*]$ carries
  no atom, $\Phi(t^*,\cdot) = \Phi(a_N,\cdot)$ and $\Phi(\cdot,t^*) =
  \Phi(\cdot,a_N)$, so this is $\Phi(t^*,0) = \Phi(0,t^*)$.  If $N = 0$ then
  $(0,t^*]$ carries no atom and both sides equal $\Phi(0,0)$ outright.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Atomic clocks, and why the conventions do not clash here]
  \label{rem:atomicdual}
  Four comments on Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicdual}.

  \emph{It proves more than duality.}  What the grid argument delivers is
  $\Phi(s,t) = \Phi(t,s)$ at every pair of points of the chain, not merely at
  $(t^*,0)$; equivalently, $\gamma(s,t) = \gamma(t,s)$ there, which is what
  \eqref{eq:cancel} wants and what the anti-diagonal staircase fails to see.  For
  equal masses \eqref{eq:atomgrid} says precisely that $\Phi$ is constant on
  anti-diagonals of the atom grid, so the whole content sits in unequal masses.
  The symmetry is the exact discrete counterpart of the atomless case, where
  \eqref{eq:rectrepinline} makes $\Psi$ a function of $x+y$; but here it holds at
  every point and without an integrability hypothesis, as
  Remark~\ref{rem:haarrole} predicts.

  \emph{The conventions agree, and now for a reason.}  With
  $\iota = \mathrm{o}$ the $k$-th summand of \eqref{eq:cancel} on the
  anti-diagonal staircase becomes $\gamma(k{+}1,t{-}k{-}1) - \gamma(k,t{-}k)$ and
  the sum telescopes to $\gamma(t,0) - \gamma(0,t)$ rather than to $0$.  The
  proof above shows that the two conventions differ by nothing more than the
  reflection $(i,j) \mapsto (M{-}i,M{-}j)$ of the grid together with a reversal of
  the list of masses, under which \eqref{eq:atomgrid} is invariant; so the same
  lemma settles both, and $\gamma(t,0) = \gamma(0,t)$ falls out.  Section
  \ref{sec:duality} is therefore convention-agnostic, and the bundle table
  records it as such.

  \emph{What the comparability hypothesis is for.}  The proposition needs a chain
  of atoms below $t^*$, not a linearly ordered $\T$: it lives on \eqref{T0}, and
  under \eqref{T2a} the hypothesis is automatic.  This is why duality is not
  confined to a linearly ordered index even though
  Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless} lives on \eqref{T3}.  Atoms that are
  \emph{incomparable} are a different matter and are not covered; for the
  smallest instance --- $\T = \{0,a,b,t^*\}$ with $a,b$ incomparable and an atom
  at each --- the three relations along $[0,t^*)$, $[a,t^*)$ and $[b,t^*)$ force
  $m_a \gamma(a,t) = m_b \gamma(b,t) = 0$ and the conclusion is immediate, and
  the two-dimensional index $\{0,1,2\}^2$ has been verified symbolically, but no
  argument covering a general preorder is offered here.  Nor can one run through
  the symmetry of the first comment: on $\{0,1,2\}^2$ the conclusion
  $\Phi(t,0) = \Phi(0,t)$ holds at every $t$ while $\Phi(s,t) = \Phi(t,s)$ fails,
  at the incomparable pair $((1,2),(2,1))$ among others.  Symmetry is a
  phenomenon of the chain, not of the atomic clock.

  \emph{What is not covered.}  Finitely many atoms below each $t$ excludes an
  order-dense set of atoms, and with it the possibility of transferring the
  argument: where the atoms are dense, no interval $[s,s')$ carries exactly one
  of them, \eqref{eq:atomgrid} has no discrete analogue, and there is no grid to
  induct along.  That is the same obstruction as in the mixed case, seen from the
  atomic side.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Scope of Section~\ref{sec:duality}]
  \label{rem:dualscope}
  It is worth saying plainly what Section~\ref{sec:duality} needs, since the
  matter is easy to get wrong.

  It needs the \emph{balance condition} $\gamma_1 = \gamma_2$ --- in the
  probabilistic form of Theorem~\ref{thm:duality}, that the two generators act on
  the same duality function $f$ so as to produce the same $g = h$ --- and it needs
  integrability.  It does \emph{not} need a translation invariant clock: both
  Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless} (atomless clocks) and
  Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicdual} (atomic ones) cover clocks that are not Haar.
  Nor does the conclusion need a linearly ordered index: the atomic argument asks
  only that the atoms below a point form a chain.  The
  opposite claim, made in an earlier version of this manuscript on the strength
  of the non-cancellation of \eqref{eq:cancel} for $\T = \Rp^d$, was a fallacy ---
  the non-cancellation of a particular staircase is not the failure of the
  conclusion.

  What genuinely remains open is the \emph{mixed} case, a clock with both an
  atomic and a diffuse part, together with an order-dense set of atoms and a set
  of atoms that is not a chain; Remark~\ref{rem:atomsnotchange} explains why the
  two available arguments do not combine.  Nothing in this manuscript depends on it: the
  applications in Section~\ref{sec:convergence} use Lebesgue clocks, and
  Section~\ref{ssec:exduality} uses \eqref{eq:clockadd} only.
\end{remark}

\subsection{The continuous-time theory}
\label{ssec:dualcont}

\begin{lemma}[\EK, Lemma 4.4.10; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{lem:calculus}
  Let $\Phi : [0,\infty) \times [0,\infty) \to \R$ be absolutely continuous in $s$
  for each fixed $t$ and absolutely continuous in $t$ for each fixed $s$, and
  set $(\gamma_1,\gamma_2) = \nabla\Phi$.  Suppose that
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:calcint}
    \int_0^T \! \int_0^T |\gamma_i(s,t)| \dif s \dif t < \infty,
    \qquad i = 1,2, \quad T > 0 .
  \end{equation}
  Then for almost every $t \geq 0$,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:calcconc}
    \Phi(t,0) - \Phi(0,t) \;=\; \int_0^t \bigl( \gamma_1(s,t-s) - \gamma_2(s,t-s) \bigr) \dif s .
  \end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  By \eqref{eq:calcint} and Fubini,
  \begin{align*}
    \int_0^T \!\! \int_0^t &\bigl( \gamma_1(t-s,s) - \gamma_2(s,t-s) \bigr) \dif s \dif t \\
    &= \int_0^T \!\! \int_0^t \! \gamma_1(t-s,s) \dif s \dif t
       - \int_0^T \!\! \int_0^t \! \gamma_2(s,t-s) \dif s \dif t \\
    &= \int_0^T \!\! \int_s^T \! \gamma_1(t-s,s) \dif t \dif s
       - \int_0^T \!\! \int_s^T \! \gamma_2(s,t-s) \dif t \dif s \\
    &= \int_0^T \bigl( \Phi(T-s,s) - \Phi(0,s) \bigr) \dif s
       - \int_0^T \bigl( \Phi(s,T-s) - \Phi(s,0) \bigr) \dif s \\
    &= \int_0^T \bigl( \Phi(s,0) - \Phi(0,s) \bigr) \dif s ,
  \end{align*}
  where the third equality uses absolute continuity in the first resp.\ second
  variable, and the fourth is the substitution $s \mapsto T-s$ in the first two
  integrals.  Differentiating with respect to $T$ gives \eqref{eq:calcconc} for
  a.e.\ $T$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[\EK, Theorem 4.4.11; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{thm:duality}
  Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent measurable processes with values in $E_1$ and
  $E_2$ respectively.  Let $f,g,h \in \Meas(E_1 \times E_2)$,
  $\alpha \in \Meas(E_1)$ and $\beta \in \Meas(E_2)$.  Suppose that for each
  $T > 0$ there exist an integrable random variable $\Gamma_T$ and a constant
  $C_T$ such that
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dual1}
    \begin{aligned}
      \sup_{r,s,t \leq T} \bigl( |\alpha(X(r))| + 1 \bigr) |f(X(s),Y(t))| &\leq \Gamma_T , \\
      \sup_{r,s,t \leq T} \bigl( |\beta(Y(r))| + 1 \bigr) |f(X(s),Y(t))| &\leq \Gamma_T , \\
      \sup_{r,s,t \leq T} \bigl( |\alpha(X(r))| + 1 \bigr) |g(X(s),Y(t))| &\leq \Gamma_T , \\
      \sup_{r,s,t \leq T} \bigl( |\beta(Y(r))| + 1 \bigr) |h(X(s),Y(t))| &\leq \Gamma_T ,
    \end{aligned}
  \end{equation}
  and
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dual2}
    \int_0^T |\alpha(X(u))| \dif u + \int_0^T |\beta(Y(u))| \dif u \;\leq\; C_T .
  \end{equation}
  Suppose that
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualmg1}
    f(X(t),y) - \int_0^t g(X(s),y) \dif s
  \end{equation}
  is a $({}^{*}\Filt^X_t)$-martingale for each $y \in E_2$, and
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualmg2}
    f(x,Y(t)) - \int_0^t h(x,Y(s)) \dif s
  \end{equation}
  is a $({}^{*}\Filt^Y_t)$-martingale for each $x \in E_1$ (the integrals in
  \eqref{eq:dualmg1} and \eqref{eq:dualmg2} being assumed to exist).  Then for
  almost every $t \geq 0$,
  \begin{multline}
    \label{eq:dualconc}
    E\Bigl[ f(X(t),Y(0)) \exp\Bigl\{ \int_0^t \alpha(X(u)) \dif u \Bigr\} \Bigr]
    - E\Bigl[ f(X(0),Y(t)) \exp\Bigl\{ \int_0^t \beta(Y(u)) \dif u \Bigr\} \Bigr] \\
    = E\Bigl[ \int_0^t \bigl\{ g(X(s),Y(t-s)) - h(X(s),Y(t-s))
        + \bigl( \alpha(X(s)) - \beta(Y(t-s)) \bigr) f(X(s),Y(t-s)) \bigr\} \\
      \times \exp\Bigl\{ \int_0^s \alpha(X(u)) \dif u
        + \int_0^{t-s} \beta(Y(u)) \dif u \Bigr\} \dif s \Bigr].
  \end{multline}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Set
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:Phidual}
    \Phi(s,t) = E\Bigl[ f(X(s),Y(t))
      \exp\Bigl\{ \int_0^s \alpha(X(u)) \dif u \Bigr\}
      \exp\Bigl\{ \int_0^t \beta(Y(u)) \dif u \Bigr\} \Bigr] .
  \end{equation}
  \emph{Step 1: freezing $Y$.}
  Write $e_\alpha(s) = \exp\{\int_0^s \alpha(X(u))\dif u\}$ and
  $e_\beta(t) = \exp\{\int_0^t \beta(Y(u))\dif u\}$, and fix $t$.  Both $e_\beta(t)$
  and $Y(t)$ are $\sigma(Y)$-measurable, and $\sigma(Y)$ is independent of
  $\sigma(X)$; so every expectation below may be computed by conditioning on
  $\sigma(Y)$, freezing $Y(t) = y$ and $e_\beta(t)$, and using that
  $M^y_r = f(X(r),y) - \int_0^r g(X(v),y)\dif v$ is a
  $({}^{*}\Filt^X_r)$-martingale under the conditioned measure --- which it is,
  because conditioning on an independent $\sigma$-field leaves the law of $X$ and
  the filtration ${}^{*}\Filt^X$ unchanged.  We suppress the conditioning from the
  notation.

  \emph{Step 2: the increment identity.}  For $h > 0$ split
  \begin{multline}
    \label{eq:dualsplit}
    f(X(s{+}h),y) e_\alpha(s{+}h) - f(X(s),y) e_\alpha(s) \\
      = \underbrace{\bigl[ f(X(s{+}h),y) - f(X(s),y) \bigr] e_\alpha(s)}_{(A)}
      + \underbrace{f(X(s{+}h),y) \bigl[ e_\alpha(s{+}h) - e_\alpha(s) \bigr]}_{(B)} .
  \end{multline}
  For $(A)$, write $f(X(s{+}h),y) - f(X(s),y) = M^y_{s+h} - M^y_s
  + \int_s^{s+h} g(X(v),y)\dif v$.  Since $e_\alpha(s)$ is
  ${}^{*}\Filt^X_s$-measurable and bounded by $e^{C_T}$, the martingale term
  contributes $0$, so
  \begin{align*}
    E[(A)] &= \int_s^{s+h} E\bigl[ g(X(r),y) e_\alpha(s) \bigr] \dif r \\
      &= \int_s^{s+h} E\bigl[ g(X(r),y) e_\alpha(r) \bigr] \dif r \\
      &\qquad + \int_s^{s+h} E\bigl[ g(X(r),y) (e_\alpha(s) - e_\alpha(r)) \bigr] \dif r .
  \end{align*}
  For $(B)$, the chain rule gives
  $e_\alpha(s{+}h) - e_\alpha(s) = \int_s^{s+h} \alpha(X(r)) e_\alpha(r) \dif r$.
  For each $r \in [s,s+h]$ write
  \[
    f(X(s{+}h),y) \;=\; f(X(r),y) + \bigl( M^y_{s+h} - M^y_r \bigr)
      + \int_r^{s+h} g(X(v),y)\dif v .
  \]
  The martingale term again contributes $0$, since $\alpha(X(r))e_\alpha(r)$ is
  ${}^{*}\Filt^X_r$-measurable, and one obtains
  \begin{align*}
    E[(B)] &= \int_s^{s+h} E\bigl[ f(X(r),y)\alpha(X(r)) e_\alpha(r) \bigr] \dif r \\
      &\qquad + \int_s^{s+h} \! \int_r^{s+h}
          E\bigl[ g(X(v),y) \alpha(X(r)) e_\alpha(r) \bigr] \dif v \dif r .
  \end{align*}
  Multiplying by $e_\beta(t)$, integrating over the law of $Y$ and using Fubini ---
  legitimate because all integrands are dominated by $\Gamma_T e^{C_T}$ by
  \eqref{eq:dual1}--\eqref{eq:dual2} --- we obtain
  \begin{multline}
    \label{eq:Fincrement}
    \Phi(s+h,t) - \Phi(s,t) \;=\;
      \underbrace{\int_s^{s+h} \!\! E\bigl[ f\alpha(X(r),Y(t)) e_\alpha(r) e_\beta(t) \bigr] \dif r}_{T_1} \\
      + \underbrace{\int_s^{s+h} \!\! \int_r^{s+h} \!\!
          E\bigl[ g(X(v),Y(t)) \alpha(X(r)) e_\alpha(r) e_\beta(t) \bigr] \dif v \dif r}_{T_2} \\
      + \underbrace{\int_s^{s+h} \!\! E\bigl[ g(X(r),Y(t)) e_\alpha(r) e_\beta(t) \bigr] \dif r}_{T_3} \\
      + \underbrace{\int_s^{s+h} \!\!
          E\bigl[ g(X(r),Y(t)) \bigl( e_\alpha(s) - e_\alpha(r) \bigr) e_\beta(t) \bigr] \dif r}_{T_4} ,
  \end{multline}
  where $f\alpha$ abbreviates $(x,y) \mapsto f(x,y)\alpha(x)$.

  \emph{Step 3: $T_2$ and $T_4$ are $O(h^2)$.}  Let $t, s+h \leq T$.  The third
  line of \eqref{eq:dual1} gives
  $|\alpha(X(r))| \, |g(X(v),Y(t))| \leq \Gamma_T$, and \eqref{eq:dual2} gives
  $e_\alpha(r) e_\beta(t) \leq e^{C_T}$.  Hence
  \[
    |T_2| \;\leq\; e^{C_T} E[\Gamma_T] \int_s^{s+h} (s+h-r) \dif r
          \;=\; \tfrac{1}{2} h^2 e^{C_T} E[\Gamma_T] .
  \]
  For $T_4$ use $e_\alpha(s) - e_\alpha(r) = -\int_s^r \alpha(X(u)) e_\alpha(u)\dif u$
  and the same two bounds:
  \[
    |T_4| \;\leq\; \int_s^{s+h} \!\! \int_s^r
      E\bigl[ |g(X(r),Y(t))| \, |\alpha(X(u))| \, e_\alpha(u) e_\beta(t) \bigr] \dif u \dif r
      \;\leq\; \tfrac{1}{2} h^2 e^{C_T} E[\Gamma_T] .
  \]

  \emph{Step 4: passing to the limit.}  Since
  $T_1 + T_3 = \int_s^{s+h} E[(f\alpha + g)(X(r),Y(t)) e_\alpha(r) e_\beta(t)]\dif r$,
  summing \eqref{eq:Fincrement} over a partition
  $0 = s_0 < \dots < s_m = s$ gives
  \[
    \Bigl| \Phi(s,t) - \Phi(0,t)
      - \int_0^s E\bigl[ (f\alpha + g)(X(r),Y(t)) e_\alpha(r) e_\beta(t) \bigr] \dif r \Bigr|
    \;\leq\; \tfrac{1}{2} e^{C_T} E[\Gamma_T] \sum_{i=1}^m (s_i - s_{i-1})^2
  \]
  --- the main terms telescoping \emph{exactly}, with no limit needed --- and the
  right-hand side is at most
  $\tfrac{1}{2} e^{C_T} E[\Gamma_T] \, s \max_i (s_i - s_{i-1}) \to 0$.  This is
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:Fpartial1}
    \Phi(s,t) - \Phi(0,t)
      = \int_0^s E\bigl[ \bigl( f(X(r),Y(t)) \alpha(X(r)) + g(X(r),Y(t)) \bigr)
          e_\alpha(r) e_\beta(t) \bigr] \dif r .
  \end{equation}
  Symmetrically,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:Fpartial2}
    \Phi(s,t) - \Phi(s,0)
      = \int_0^t E\bigl[ \bigl( f(X(s),Y(r)) \beta(Y(r)) + h(X(s),Y(r)) \bigr)
          e_\alpha(s) e_\beta(r) \bigr] \dif r .
  \end{equation}
  Thus $\Phi$ satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:calculus}, with $\gamma_1$ and
  $\gamma_2$ the integrands in \eqref{eq:Fpartial1} and \eqref{eq:Fpartial2}; the
  integrability \eqref{eq:calcint} follows from \eqref{eq:dual1} and
  \eqref{eq:dual2}.  Now \eqref{eq:calcconc} is exactly \eqref{eq:dualconc}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[\EK, Remark 4.4.12]
  \label{rem:dualext}
  Equation \eqref{eq:dualconc} can frequently be extended to more general
  $\alpha$ and $\beta$ by approximating them by bounded functions.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Duality relation; \EK, Corollary 4.4.13; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{cor:dualrel}
  If, in addition to the conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:duality},
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualbalance}
    g(x,y) + \alpha(x) f(x,y) \;=\; h(x,y) + \beta(y) f(x,y) ,
    \qquad (x,y) \in E_1 \times E_2 ,
  \end{equation}
  then for all $t \geq 0$
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualrel}
    E\Bigl[ f(X(t),Y(0)) \exp\Bigl\{ \int_0^t \alpha(X(u)) \dif u \Bigr\} \Bigr]
    = E\Bigl[ f(X(0),Y(t)) \exp\Bigl\{ \int_0^t \beta(Y(u)) \dif u \Bigr\} \Bigr].
  \end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Under \eqref{eq:dualbalance} the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:dualconc}
  vanishes, so \eqref{eq:dualrel} holds for almost every $t$.  It extends to all
  $t$ because $t \mapsto F(t,0)$ and $t \mapsto F(0,t)$ are continuous, which
  follows from \eqref{eq:Fpartial1} and \eqref{eq:Fpartial2}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:dualischain}
  Corollary~\ref{cor:dualrel} is Proposition~\ref{prop:haar}\ref{it:haar_cont}
  applied to the $\Phi$ of \eqref{eq:Phidual}, with
  $\gamma_1$ and $\gamma_2$ the integrands of \eqref{eq:Fpartial1} and
  \eqref{eq:Fpartial2}; the balance condition \eqref{eq:dualbalance} is exactly
  $\gamma_1 = \gamma_2$.  The whole of Theorem~\ref{thm:duality} is thus
  Lemma~\ref{lem:chain} plus the computation \eqref{eq:Fincrement} that produces
  the increment representations \eqref{eq:incrementrep} from the martingale
  hypotheses.  Only the latter uses probability; and it uses no path regularity,
  no topology on $E_1$, $E_2$ and no boundedness, so it is already stated at the
  level of generality of \CPS.  What it does use, and what
  Remark~\ref{rem:dualscope} isolates, is the balance condition and
  integrability --- not, as one might guess from the shape of \eqref{eq:cancel},
  translation invariance of the clock; see Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless} and
  Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicdual}.
\end{remark}

The discrete counterpart, which \EK{} do not state, now costs nothing.

\begin{corollary}[Duality for Markov chains; $\T = \N_0$, \eqref{E0}, $\iota = \mathrm{p}$]
  \label{cor:dualdiscrete}
  Let $P$ and $Q$ be transition kernels on measurable spaces $E_1$ and $E_2$, and
  let $f \in \Bdd(E_1 \times E_2)$ satisfy the duality relation
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualdiscrete}
    \int f(x',y) \, P(x,\dif x') \;=\; \int f(x,y') \, Q(y,\dif y') ,
    \qquad (x,y) \in E_1 \times E_2 .
  \end{equation}
  Let $X$ and $Y$ be independent Markov chains with kernels $P$ and $Q$ and
  $X(0) = x$, $Y(0) = y$.  Then
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualdiscreteconc}
    E\bigl[ f(X(n),y) \bigr] \;=\; E\bigl[ f(x,Y(n)) \bigr] ,
    \qquad n \in \N_0 .
  \end{equation}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Put $\Phi(n,m) = E[f(X(n),Y(m))]$.  Writing $P$ for the action of the kernel on
  the first variable and $Q$ for its action on the second, the Markov property
  and independence give
  $\Phi(n+1,m) - \Phi(n,m) = E[((P-I)f)(X(n),Y(m))]$ and
  $\Phi(n,m+1) - \Phi(n,m) = E[((Q-I)f)(X(n),Y(m))]$, which is
  \eqref{eq:incrementrep} for $q = $ counting measure with
  $\gamma_1(n,m) = E[((P-I)f)(X(n),Y(m))]$ and $\gamma_2$ its analogue.  By
  \eqref{eq:dualdiscrete}, $(P-I)f = (Q-I)f$, so $\gamma_1 = \gamma_2$, and
  Proposition~\ref{prop:haar}\ref{it:haar_disc} applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:dualdiscretemp}
  In the language of Section~\ref{sec:MP}, \eqref{eq:dualdiscrete} says that
  $(f(\cdot,y),\, g(\cdot,y)) \in A$ and $(f(x,\cdot),\, g(x,\cdot)) \in B$ hold
  with the \emph{same} $g = (P-I)f = (Q-I)f$, where $A$ and $B$ denote the
  one-step generators; the associated test process is then the Doob
  decomposition
  \[
    f(X(n),y) - \sum_{k < n} g(X(k),y) ,
  \]
  i.e.\ the compensator in the predictable convention $[0,t)$.  So
  Corollary~\ref{cor:dualdiscrete} is the martingale-problem duality theorem for
  $\T = \N_0$; the predictable convention is the one in which its proof is
  shortest, not the only one in which it is true
  (Remark~\ref{rem:atomicdual}).
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Stopped version; \EK, Corollary 4.4.14; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{cor:dualstopped}
  Let $(\Filt_t)$ and $(\Gilt_t)$ be independent filtrations, let $X$ be
  $(\Filt_t)$-progressive with $(\Filt_t)$-stopping time $\tau$, and let $Y$ be
  $(\Gilt_t)$-progressive with $(\Gilt_t)$-stopping time $\sigma$.  Suppose
  \eqref{eq:dual1} and \eqref{eq:dual2} hold with $X$, $Y$ replaced by
  $X(\cdot \wedge \tau)$, $Y(\cdot \wedge \sigma)$, and that
  $f(X(t \wedge \tau),y) - \int_0^{t \wedge \tau} g(X(s),y) \dif s$ is an
  $(\Filt_t)$-martingale for each $y$, and
  $f(x,Y(t \wedge \sigma)) - \int_0^{t \wedge \sigma} h(x,Y(s)) \dif s$ is a
  $(\Gilt_t)$-martingale for each $x$.  Then the analogue of
  \eqref{eq:dualconc} holds with $X$, $Y$ replaced by the stopped processes and
  the integrand multiplied by the indicators $\one_{\{s \leq \tau\}}$ resp.\
  $\one_{\{t-s \leq \sigma\}}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Rewrite the martingale in the hypothesis as
  $f(X(t \wedge \tau),y) - \int_0^t \one_{\{s \leq \tau\}} g(X(s),y) \dif s$ and
  repeat the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:duality}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:dualstopped}
  Corollary~\ref{cor:dualstopped} is the practically relevant form: the
  estimates \eqref{eq:dual1} are often unavailable globally but easy on the
  exit times $\tau$ from compact sets.  One then lets $\tau, \sigma \to \infty$
  along an exhausting sequence; the integrand on the right of the stopped version
  of \eqref{eq:dualconc} tends to zero, and the only difficulty is to justify the
  interchange of limits and expectations (\EK, Remark 4.4.16).  This is also the
  route by which duality is applied to \emph{local} martingale problems.
\end{remark}

We can now close the circle with Section~\ref{sec:uniqueness}.

\begin{corollary}[Uniqueness via duality; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{cor:uniqviadual}
  Assume \eqref{E2}, let $A \subset \Bdd(E) \times \Bdd(E)$, let $(E_2,\mathcal{E}_2)$ be
  a measurable space, and let $B \subset \Meas(E_2) \times \Meas(E_2)$.  Suppose
  there is $f \in \Meas(E \times E_2)$ such that:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item $\{ f(\cdot,y) : y \in E_2 \} \subset \Bdd(E)$ is separating for
    $\Prob(E)$;
  \item $(f(\cdot,y), g(\cdot,y)) \in A$ for every $y \in E_2$, and
    $(f(x,\cdot), h(x,\cdot)) \in B$ for every $x \in E$, with $g = h$;
  \item for every $y \in E_2$ there is a solution $Y^y$ of the martingale problem
    for $(B, \delta_y)$, and for every solution $X$ of the martingale problem for
    $A$ with $X$ independent of $Y^y$ the hypotheses
    \eqref{eq:dual1}--\eqref{eq:dual2} hold with $\alpha = \beta = 0$.
  \end{enumerate}
  Then for every $\mu \in \Prob(E)$ the martingale problem for $(A,\mu)$ has at
  most one solution, every solution is Markov, and under the additional
  hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:uniqueness}\ref{it:uniq_b}--\ref{it:uniq_c} it
  is strong Markov.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Let $X$ solve the martingale problem for $(A,\delta_x)$ and let $Y^y$ be as in
  (iii), independent of $X$.  By (ii), \eqref{eq:dualbalance} holds with
  $\alpha = \beta = 0$, so Corollary~\ref{cor:dualrel} gives
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualxy}
    E\bigl[ f(X(t), y) \bigr] \;=\; E\bigl[ f(x, Y^y(t)) \bigr] ,
    \qquad t \geq 0 .
  \end{equation}
  The right-hand side does not depend on $X$.  Hence $\int f(\cdot,y) \dif \nu_t$
  is the same for the one-dimensional distributions $\nu_t = P X(t)^{-1}$ of any
  two solutions, and by (i) these coincide.  For general $\mu$, integrate
  \eqref{eq:dualxy} over $\mu(\dif x)$, which is legitimate because the
  one-dimensional distributions of a solution for $(A,\mu)$ are mixtures of those
  for $(A,\delta_x)$ by Theorem~\ref{thm:uniqueness}\ref{it:uniq_a}.  Now apply
  Theorem~\ref{thm:uniqueness}.
\end{proof}

% =====================================================================
\section{Existence}
\label{sec:convergence}
% =====================================================================

Sections~\ref{sec:uniqueness} and \ref{sec:duality} are a uniqueness theory, and
by themselves they are vacuous: nothing so far exhibits a single solution of a
single martingale problem.  This section collects the five routes to existence,
in increasing order of cost.

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item \emph{From a transition semigroup} (Section~\ref{ssec:exsemigroup}).
  Immediate, and correspondingly weak: it presupposes the object one usually
  wants to construct.
\item \emph{From a dual process} (Section~\ref{ssec:exduality}).  Produces the
  semigroup that (a) presupposes, out of a \emph{known} process on a second state
  space and a duality function.  Cheap, because Section~\ref{sec:duality} has
  already done the analytic work; conditional on one hypothesis, kernel
  representability, which is where all the difficulty is concentrated.
\item \emph{Explicit construction} (Sections~\ref{ssec:jump} and
  \ref{ssec:hawkes}).  Jump processes are built by hand out of a Markov chain and
  exponential holding times, and the martingale property is verified by a two-line
  computation with the exponential distribution; the same computation survives a
  \emph{path-dependent} rate, which gives Hawkes processes.  These are the only
  places in the manuscript where a solution is written down.
\item \emph{From stochastic differential equations}
  (Section~\ref{ssec:exsde}).  The most important route in practice and the most
  expensive here, since it presupposes stochastic integration; we state the chain
  of results and prove none of it.
\item \emph{By convergence} (Sections~\ref{ssec:EKconv}--\ref{ssec:CPSconv}).
  Weak limits of solutions of approximating problems.  Conditional, like~(a), but
  on much less.
\end{enumerate}

Routes (c) and (d) are the two that produce a solution out of nothing, and it is
worth noticing before we start that they are the same argument at two levels of
difficulty: both are Picard--Lindel\"of.  In (c) it is the Picard iteration for
the linear equation $\dot v = Av$ in $\Bdd(E)$, with $A$ bounded
(Remark~\ref{rem:jumpgronwall}); in (d) it is the Picard iteration for a
Lipschitz stochastic differential equation.  What differs is the space on which
the contraction acts.  Route (b) is of a different nature: it produces nothing
out of nothing either, but what it presupposes lives on a \emph{different} state
space, which in the applications is a simpler one --- a finite particle system
dual to a measure-valued diffusion, say.

\subsection{From a transition semigroup}
\label{ssec:exsemigroup}

\begin{proposition}[\EK, Proposition 4.1.7; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{prop:exsemigroup}
  Let $X$ be a Markov process with measurable transition semigroup
  $\{T(t)\}$ on $\Bdd(E)$ and full generator $\hat{A}$ in the sense of
  Definition~\ref{def:fullgenerator}.  Then $X$ solves the martingale problem for
  $\hat{A}$.
\end{proposition}

This is Remark~\ref{rem:fullgenerator}, restated as an existence statement, and it
is recorded only for completeness: it turns a Markov process into a solution, and
producing the Markov process is the whole difficulty.  The next section produces
one; the three after that do not presuppose one.

\subsection{From a dual process}
\label{ssec:exduality}

There is a route that produces exactly the object
Proposition~\ref{prop:exsemigroup} presupposes, and it produces it out of the
material that Section~\ref{sec:duality} has already assembled.  It is due to
\DGP, and its point is that no approximating sequence of processes of a
\emph{different kind} --- jump processes to approximate diffusions, and the like
--- is needed: the dual process, which one has anyway for uniqueness, delivers
existence as well.

The idea in one line: if $Y$ is a known process on $E_2$ and $H$ is a duality
function, then the right-hand side $E^y[H(x,Y_t)]$ of the duality relation is a
candidate for the left-hand side $E^x[H(X_t,y)]$ of a process $X$ that does not
yet exist; if that candidate is \emph{represented by a kernel} in $x$, the
kernels form a transition semigroup and $X$ exists.  Everything else is
bookkeeping, and all of it survives an arbitrary \emph{shift invariant} clock ---
including one with atoms, the obstruction of Remark~\ref{rem:atomsnotchange} being
confined to Section~\ref{ssec:antidiag}.

\begin{setting}[Duality data; \eqref{T2a} + \eqref{T4} + shift invariant clock + \eqref{E1} on $E_1$, \eqref{E0} on $E_2$]
  \label{set:dualdata}
  Let $\T$ satisfy \eqref{T2a} and \eqref{T4} --- the linear order is used exactly once, in
  Step~1 of Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality}, and
  Remark~\ref{rem:exdualityscope}(ii) explains why it cannot be dropped there ---
  let $q$ be a clock which is \emph{shift invariant},
  $q(r + \cdot) = q(\cdot)$, and fix $\iota$.  Let $(E_1,\mathcal{E}_1)$ satisfy \eqref{E1} and $(E_2,\mathcal{E}_2)$
  satisfy \eqref{E0}.  Let
  \[
    H, g : E_1 \times E_2 \to \R , \qquad \beta : E_2 \to \R
  \]
  be bounded and measurable, and let $(P^y)_{y \in E_2}$ be a time-homogeneous
  Markov family on a path space $F_2$ over $E_2$ with $P^y\{\pi_0 = y\} = 1$.
  Write
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:FKfactor}
    V_t \;=\; \exp\Bigl\{ - \int_{\langle 0,t \rangle_\iota} \beta(\pi_u) \, q(\dif u) \Bigr\} ,
  \end{equation}
  the \emph{Feynman--Kac factor}, and note $0 < V_t \leq e^{\lVert\beta\rVert q(\T_{\leq t})} < \infty$,
  the finiteness being Definition~\ref{def:clock}.  Define, for
  $\varphi \in \{H,g\}$,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualsemigroup}
    (P_t\varphi)(x,y) \;\coloneqq\; E^y\bigl[ \varphi(x,\pi_t) V_t \bigr] ,
    \qquad x \in E_1, \ y \in E_2, \ t \in \T .
  \end{equation}
  We assume throughout:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(D\arabic*)]
  \item \label{it:D1}
    \emph{Balance.}  For every $x \in E_1$ the process
    \[
      t \;\longmapsto\; H(x,\pi_t) V_t
        - \int_{\langle 0,t\rangle_\iota} g(x,\pi_u) V_u \, q(\dif u)
    \]
    is a $P^y$-martingale, for every $y \in E_2$.
  \item \label{it:D2}
    \emph{Separation.}  $\operatorname{span}\{H(\cdot,y) : y \in E_2\}$ is
    separating on $\Prob(E_1)$.
  \item \label{it:D3}
    \emph{Kernel representability.}  There is a family $(\mu_t)_{t \in \T}$ of
    probability kernels on $E_1$ with $(t,x) \mapsto \mu_t(x,\Gamma)$ jointly
    measurable for every $\Gamma \in \mathcal{E}_1$, such that
    \[
      (P_t\varphi)(x,y) \;=\; \int_{E_1} \mu_t(x,\dif x') \, \varphi(x',y) ,
      \qquad \varphi \in \{H,g\} ,
    \]
    for all $x,y,t$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{setting}

Three comments before the statements.  \ref{it:D1} is the balance condition of
Section~\ref{sec:duality} in integrated form: it says that the pair
$(H(x,\cdot), g(x,\cdot))$, twisted by the Feynman--Kac factor, is a test process
for $Y$ --- in the differential notation of \DGP{} it reads
$G_1 H(\cdot,y)(x) = G_2 H(x,\cdot)(y) + \beta(y)H(x,y)$ with
$g = G_1H(\cdot,y)$.  \ref{it:D2} is a mild non-degeneracy of the duality
function.  \ref{it:D3} is the only substantial hypothesis, and it is the one that
cannot be checked by bookkeeping; Proposition~\ref{prop:rieszmarkov} gives the
standard sufficient condition.

\begin{lemma}[The semigroup identity; \eqref{T4} + shift invariant clock]
  \label{lem:dualsemigroup}
  In Setting~\ref{set:dualdata}, for $\varphi \in \{H,g\}$, $s,t \in \T$,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualsg}
    (P_{s+t}\varphi)(x,y) \;=\; E^y\bigl[ (P_t\varphi)(x,\pi_s) \, V_s \bigr] .
  \end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \emph{Step 1: the factor splits.}  By clock additivity \eqref{eq:clockadd},
  \[
    V_{s+t} \;=\; V_s \cdot
      \exp\Bigl\{ -\int_{\langle s,s+t\rangle_\iota} \beta(\pi_v) \, q(\dif v) \Bigr\} ,
  \]
  and substituting $v = s+u$, which is a bijection of $\langle 0,t\rangle_\iota$
  onto $\langle s,s+t\rangle_\iota$ by Lemma~\ref{lem:shiftembed}, the second
  factor equals
  $\exp\{-\int_{\langle 0,t\rangle_\iota}\beta(\pi_{s+u}) q(\dif u)\} = V_t \circ \theta_s$.
  \emph{This is the one place where shift invariance of $q$ is used}: without it
  the substitution changes the measure and the shifted factor is not $V_t$.

  \emph{Step 2: the Markov property.}  $\varphi(x,\pi_{s+t}) = (\varphi(x,\pi_t))\circ\theta_s$
  and $V_s$ is $\Filt^\circ_s$-measurable, so
  \[
    (P_{s+t}\varphi)(x,y)
      = E^y\bigl[ \varphi(x,\pi_t)\circ\theta_s \cdot (V_t\circ\theta_s) \cdot V_s \bigr]
      = E^y\Bigl[ E^{\pi_s}\bigl[ \varphi(x,\pi_t) V_t \bigr] V_s \Bigr] ,
  \]
  by the Markov property of $(P^y)$ and its time homogeneity.  The inner
  expectation is $(P_t\varphi)(x,\pi_s)$, which is \eqref{eq:dualsg}.  All
  integrands are bounded by Setting~\ref{set:dualdata}, so every expectation is
  finite.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The kernels form a transition semigroup; + \eqref{E1}]
  \label{prop:dualCK}
  In Setting~\ref{set:dualdata}, $\mu_0(x,\cdot) = \delta_x$ and
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualCK}
    \mu_{s+t}(x,\cdot) \;=\; \int_{E_1} \mu_s(x,\dif x') \, \mu_t(x',\cdot) ,
    \qquad s,t \in \T, \ x \in E_1 .
  \end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  \emph{Initial condition.}  $\langle 0,0\rangle_\iota = \emptyset$ gives
  $V_0 = 1$, and $P^y\{\pi_0 = y\} = 1$ gives
  $(P_0H)(x,y) = H(x,y)$.  So $\int \mu_0(x,\dif x')H(x',y) = H(x,y)$ for every
  $y$, and \ref{it:D2} forces $\mu_0(x,\cdot) = \delta_x$.

  \emph{Chapman--Kolmogorov.}  Fix $x,y,s,t$ and compute, using \ref{it:D3} at
  $x'$, then Fubini --- legitimate because $H$ is bounded and $\mu_s(x,\cdot)$ is
  a probability measure --- then \ref{it:D3} at $x$, then
  Lemma~\ref{lem:dualsemigroup}:
  \begin{align*}
    \int \mu_s(x,\dif x') \int \mu_t(x',\dif x'') H(x'',y)
      &= \int \mu_s(x,\dif x') \, (P_tH)(x',y) \\
      &= \int \mu_s(x,\dif x') \, E^y\bigl[ H(x',\pi_t) V_t \bigr] \\
      &= E^y\Bigl[ \Bigl( \int \mu_s(x,\dif x') H(x',\pi_t) \Bigr) V_t \Bigr] \\
      &= E^y\bigl[ (P_sH)(x,\pi_t) \, V_t \bigr] \\
      &= (P_{t+s}H)(x,y)
       \;=\; \int \mu_{s+t}(x,\dif x'') H(x'',y) ,
  \end{align*}
  the commutativity of $\T$ being used once, in $t+s = s+t$.  Since this holds
  for every $y$, \ref{it:D2} gives \eqref{eq:dualCK}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Existence by duality; \DGP, Theorem 2.1; \eqref{T2a} + \eqref{T4} + shift invariant clock + \eqref{E1}]
  \label{thm:exduality}
  In Setting~\ref{set:dualdata}, let $\nu \in \Prob(E_1)$.  Then there is a Markov
  process $X$ on $E_1$ with initial distribution $\nu$ and transition kernels
  $(\mu_t)_{t \in \T}$, and it solves the martingale problem for
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualMP}
    \XX^{(1)} \;=\; \Bigl\{ \, H(\pi_t,y)
      - \int_{\langle 0,t\rangle_\iota} g(\pi_u,y) \, q(\dif u)
      \ : \ y \in E_2 \, \Bigr\} .
  \end{equation}
  Moreover $X$ and $Y$ are in duality with potential $\beta$:
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualityrel}
    E\bigl[ H(X_t,y) \bigm| X_0 = x \bigr]
      \;=\; E^y\bigl[ H(x,\pi_t) V_t \bigr] ,
    \qquad x \in E_1, \ y \in E_2, \ t \in \T .
  \end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  \emph{Step 1: the process.}  By Proposition~\ref{prop:dualCK} the family
  $(\mu_t)$ is a measurable transition semigroup on $E_1$, measurability being
  \ref{it:D3}.  Under \eqref{T2a} every finite subset $t_1 \leq \dots \leq t_n$ of $\T$
  is a chain, so the finite-dimensional distributions
  $\nu \otimes \mu_{t_1} \otimes \mu_{t_2 - t_1} \otimes \dots$ are defined for
  \emph{all} finite subsets and form a projective family, consistency being
  Chapman--Kolmogorov plus marginalization; \emph{this is the one step that
  needs the linear order}, see Remark~\ref{rem:exdualityscope}(ii).  Since $E_1$
  satisfies \eqref{E1} it is standard Borel, so the Kolmogorov extension theorem
  (Fact~\ref{fact:kolmogorov}) applies; this is \EK, Theorem~4.1.1.  Let $X$ be
  the resulting Markov process.  Then \eqref{eq:dualityrel} is \ref{it:D3} for
  $\varphi = H$.

  \emph{Step 2: the one-dimensional identity.}  Fix $x$ and $y$ and take
  $P^y$-expectations in \ref{it:D1} between $0$ and $t$; Fubini is legitimate
  because $g$ and $V$ are bounded on $\T_{\leq t}$ and $q(\T_{\leq t}) < \infty$.
  This gives
  \[
    (P_tH)(x,y) \;=\; H(x,y)
      + \int_{\langle 0,t\rangle_\iota} (P_u g)(x,y) \, q(\dif u) .
  \]
  Now apply \ref{it:D3} to both sides --- \emph{here it is used for $g$ as well as
  for $H$, which is why Setting~\ref{set:dualdata} asks for both}:
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:dualonedim}
    \int \mu_t(x,\dif x') H(x',y)
      \;=\; H(x,y)
        + \int_{\langle 0,t\rangle_\iota}
          \Bigl( \int \mu_u(x,\dif x') g(x',y) \Bigr) q(\dif u) .
  \end{equation}

  \emph{Step 3: the martingale property.}  Let $s \leq t$ in $\T$.  By the Markov
  property of $X$ and \eqref{eq:dualonedim} applied at $X_s$ with $t-s$ in place of
  $t$,
  \begin{align*}
    E\bigl[ H(X_t,y) \bigm| \Filt^X_s \bigr]
      &= \int \mu_{t-s}(X_s,\dif x') H(x',y) \\
      &= H(X_s,y) + \int_{\langle 0,t-s\rangle_\iota}
          E\bigl[ g(X_{s+u},y) \bigm| \Filt^X_s \bigr] q(\dif u) ,
  \end{align*}
  using the Markov property once more inside the integral.  Substituting
  $v = s+u$ and using shift invariance of $q$ together with
  Lemma~\ref{lem:shiftembed}, the last integral is
  $E[\int_{\langle s,t\rangle_\iota} g(X_v,y) q(\dif v) \mid \Filt^X_s]$, Fubini
  being available as in Step~2.  Hence
  \[
    E\Bigl[ H(X_t,y) - \int_{\langle 0,t\rangle_\iota} g(X_v,y) q(\dif v)
      \Bigm| \Filt^X_s \Bigr]
    \;=\; H(X_s,y) - \int_{\langle 0,s\rangle_\iota} g(X_v,y) q(\dif v) .
  \]
  Testing against products $\prod_k h_k(X_{t_k})$ with $t_k \in \T_{\leq s}$ and
  invoking Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar} upgrades this from $\Filt^X$ to the
  filtration $({}^{*}\Filt^X_t)$ of Definition~\ref{def:markovMP}.  So
  $X \in \Msol(\XX^{(1)},\nu)$.
\end{proof}
