\begin{corollary}[Well-posedness]
  \label{cor:exdualitywellposed}
  Assume in addition that $\XX^{(1)}$ admits a shift system with $\ZZ^\circ$
  determining, and that Section~\ref{sec:duality} applies to the pair
  $(\XX^{(1)}, Y)$ --- for instance under the integrability hypothesis of
  Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless} for an atomless clock, or
  Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicdual} for an atomic one.  Then the martingale problem
  for $\XX^{(1)}$ is
  well posed for every $\nu \in \Prob(E_1)$, and its solution is the Markov
  process of Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Existence is Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality}.  For uniqueness let
  $\tilde X \in \Msol(\XX^{(1)},\nu)$.  The pair $(\tilde X, Y)$ satisfies the
  hypotheses of Section~\ref{sec:duality} with the same $H$ and the balance
  condition \ref{it:D1}, so the duality relation \eqref{eq:dualityrel} holds for
  $\tilde X$ as well; by \ref{it:D2} this determines the one-dimensional
  distributions of $\tilde X$, and Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq} concludes.  This is
  the closing of the circle: \emph{the same duality relation is used twice, once
  forwards to construct the process and once backwards to show it is the only
  one.}
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Riesz--Markov: a sufficient condition for \ref{it:D3}; \eqref{E3}]
  \label{prop:rieszmarkov}
  Suppose $E_1$ is compact metrizable, $H \in \Cb(E_1 \times E_2)$, and
  $\mathcal{H}_1 \coloneqq \operatorname{span}\{H(\cdot,y) : y \in E_2\}$ is a
  convergence determining algebra containing the constants.  If for each $t$ the
  map $H(\cdot,y) \mapsto (P_tH)(\cdot,y)$ is positive --- $\varphi \geq 0$
  implies $P_t\varphi \geq 0$ --- and $P_t \mathbf{1} = \mathbf{1}$, then the
  kernels of \ref{it:D3} exist for $\varphi = H$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  Fix $t$ and $x$.  By hypothesis $\mathcal{H}_1$ is a subalgebra of $C(E_1)$
  containing the constants and separating points --- being convergence
  determining --- so it is dense in $C(E_1)$ by Stone--Weierstrass
  (Fact~\ref{fact:stoneweierstrass}).  The map $\varphi \mapsto (P_t\varphi)(x,\cdot)$
  is linear, positive and unital on $\mathcal{H}_1$, hence bounded, hence extends
  to a positive unital linear functional on $C(E_1)$; the Riesz--Markov theorem
  provides $\mu_t(x,\cdot) \in \Prob(E_1)$ representing it.  Measurability in
  $(t,x)$ follows because $(t,x) \mapsto \mu_t(x,\cdot)$ is then continuous into
  $\Prob(E_1)$ with the weak topology, $\mathcal{H}_1$ being convergence
  determining; this is \DGP, Proposition~2.5.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What is new here, and what is \DGP's]
  \label{rem:exdualityscope}
  The architecture is \DGP, Theorem~2.1.  Three things are more general here, and
  in each case the source's hypothesis turns out to have been used for
  convenience rather than of necessity.
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \emph{The clock.}  \DGP{} have $\T = \Rp$ with $q = \lambda$.  The proofs
    above use the clock only through the additivity \eqref{eq:clockadd} and the
    local finiteness $q(\T_{\leq t}) < \infty$, so an arbitrary shift invariant
    clock is admissible --- in particular $\T = \N_0$ with counting measure, where
    Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality} becomes a statement about a Markov chain
    constructed from its dual, and $\iota = \mathrm{p}$ makes it the Doob
    decomposition of Remark~\ref{rem:jumpdiscrete}.
  \item \emph{The index --- and here the generalization has a sharp edge.}  The
    \emph{kernel layer} uses no linear order: Lemma~\ref{lem:dualsemigroup} needs
    \eqref{T4}, and Chapman--Kolmogorov needs commutativity of $+$, so the two-parameter
    semigroup $(\mu_t)$ exists on $\T = \Rp^2$.  But Step~1 of
    Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality} does \emph{not} survive: to apply Kolmogorov one
    must exhibit finite-dimensional distributions over all finite subsets of
    $\T$, and at an antichain --- $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$ in $\Rp^2$ --- the joint law
    is simply not determined by one-parameter kernels; nor is some consistent
    choice guaranteed to exist, the compatibility conditions at
    $(1,0),(0,1),(1,1)$ being a genuine constraint that $(\mu_t)$ does not
    resolve.  So Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality} is stated under \eqref{T2a}, and what
    survives a partial order is the semigroup layer
    (Lemma~\ref{lem:dualsemigroup}, Proposition~\ref{prop:dualCK}), not the
    process.  An earlier version of this manuscript claimed more; the corrected
    claim is the exact analogue of Remark~\ref{rem:chainonly}, with the chain
    obstruction now sitting in the construction instead of the induction.
  \item \emph{The state spaces.}  Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality} needs \eqref{E1} on $E_1$
    --- for the Kolmogorov extension in Step~1 --- and only \eqref{E0} on $E_2$.  The
    compactness of \DGP{} enters solely in
    Proposition~\ref{prop:rieszmarkov}, i.e.\ in \emph{checking} \ref{it:D3}, not
    in the theorem.
  \end{enumerate}

  What does \emph{not} generalize is the reach of the method.  The relation
  \eqref{eq:dualityrel} is a statement about one-dimensional distributions from a
  fixed initial state, so it presupposes exactly the Markov structure that
  Sections~\ref{ssec:absuniq} and \ref{ssec:localuniq} took pains to avoid, and it
  has nothing to say about the path-dependent problems of
  Examples~\ref{ex:volterra} and \ref{ex:pathdepsemi} or about
  Theorem~\ref{thm:pathjumpMP}.  The natural repair is \DGP's own outlook, their
  Section~5: dualize the \emph{historical} process, taking $E_1$ to be a path or
  genealogy space.  That changes $E_1$ from compact to not even locally compact
  and puts the weight back on \ref{it:D3}; it is a project, not a remark.
  Remark~\ref{rem:liftcollapse} explains why this is the one place where lifting
  to the path is worth doing: the content of Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality} sits in
  \ref{it:D3}, a statement about the dual on an unchanged space, and not in a
  Markov property that lifting would trivialize.

  \emph{The time-inhomogeneous version.}  Dropping shift invariance of $q$, or
  homogeneity of $Y$, replaces $(P_t)$ by a two-parameter family $(P_{r,t})$ and
  $Y$ by a shift system in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:shiftstable}; the
  dual then runs on the reflected clock, which by Remark~\ref{rem:haarrole} is the
  $q$-reflection $s \mapsto Q^{-1}(Q(t)-Q(s))$ and not $t-s$.
  Lemma~\ref{lem:dualsemigroup} becomes $P_{r,s}P_{s,t} = P_{r,t}$ and
  Proposition~\ref{prop:dualCK} the corresponding two-parameter
  Chapman--Kolmogorov relation, exactly as in
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov}.  Nothing in the proofs resists; it is not
  carried out here only to keep the notation readable.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{Towards a historical duality}

Remark~\ref{rem:exdualityscope} left one thing open: the duality relation
\eqref{eq:dualityrel} speaks about one-dimensional distributions from a fixed
initial state, and therefore does not reach the path-dependent problems of
Section~\ref{ssec:absuniq}.  \DGP's own proposal is to dualize the
\emph{historical} process.  This subsection sets out what that would require,
proves the one step of it that is not conditional, and names a test object.  It
is a programme, not a theorem, and is marked as such.

\begin{setting}[The historical variant; sketch]
  \label{set:historical}
  Let $E$ be a state space and let $E_1$ be the \emph{fibred} state space of
  Definition~\ref{def:Efibred} given by the path lift of
  Definition~\ref{def:pathlift},
  \[
    E_{1,t} \;=\; \hat{E}_t \;=\; E^{\T_{\leq t}}
    \quad\text{(or } D_E[0,t] \text{)} ,
    \qquad
    \rho_{r,t} : \hat{E}_t \to \hat{E}_r \ \text{ the restriction} .
  \]
  The duality function becomes a section on the total space,
  $H : \Sigma\hat{E} \times E_2 \to \R$, and Setting~\ref{set:dualdata} becomes
  its two-parameter form: kernels $\mu_{r,t}$ from $\hat{E}_r$ to $\hat{E}_t$,
  operators $(P_{r,t}\varphi)(\hat{x},y)$, and the Chapman--Kolmogorov relation
  $\mu_{r,s}\mu_{s,t} = \mu_{r,t}$ of Proposition~\ref{prop:dualCK} in its
  two-parameter shape.
\end{setting}

Three things change, and they change in the direction the manuscript has already
prepared for.  The state space is fibred, which is D45's reason for
Definition~\ref{def:Efibred}.  The problem is irreducibly time-inhomogeneous ---
the fibres grow --- so the two-parameter version sketched in
Remark~\ref{rem:exdualityscope} is not optional here but forced.  And the dual
typically runs \emph{backwards} from the horizon, so the reflection that appears
is the $q$-reflection of Remark~\ref{rem:haarrole}.

The step that is not conditional is the following, and it is worth isolating
because it answers a question left open in Section~\ref{ssec:localuniq}.

\begin{lemma}[Kernel representability delivers a restart kernel]
  \label{lem:histrestart}
  In Setting~\ref{set:historical}, let $(\mu_{r,t})$ satisfy the two-parameter
  form of \ref{it:D3}, and suppose that for $r \leq t$ there is a subfamily
  $E_2^{(r)} \subset E_2$ with
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \label{it:hist_past}
    $H_t(\hat{x}',y) = H_r(\rho_{r,t}\hat{x}',y)$ for all $\hat{x}' \in \hat{E}_t$
    and $y \in E_2^{(r)}$ --- these duality functions read only the past up to
    $r$;
  \item \label{it:hist_rest}
    $(P_{r,t}H)(\hat{x},y) = H_r(\hat{x},y)$ for $y \in E_2^{(r)}$ --- they are at
    rest under the dual dynamics;
  \item \label{it:hist_sep}
    $\operatorname{span}\{H_r(\cdot,y) : y \in E_2^{(r)}\}$ is separating on
    $\Prob(\hat{E}_r)$.
  \end{enumerate}
  Then
  \[
    \mu_{r,t}(\hat{x},\cdot) \circ \rho_{r,t}^{-1} \;=\; \delta_{\hat{x}} ,
    \qquad \hat{x} \in \hat{E}_r ,
  \]
  that is, \emph{the kernel keeps the past}.  Consequently $\mu_{r,t}$ satisfies
  \ref{it:R1k} of Definition~\ref{def:restartkernel} at the deterministic time
  $r$; and if Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality} applies in its two-parameter form, it
  satisfies \ref{it:R2k} as well, so it \emph{is} a restart kernel.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Fix $\hat{x} \in \hat{E}_r$ and $y \in E_2^{(r)}$.  By \ref{it:hist_past},
  \ref{it:D3} and \ref{it:hist_rest}, in that order,
  \[
    \int_{\hat{E}_t} H_r\bigl(\rho_{r,t}\hat{x}',y\bigr) \, \mu_{r,t}(\hat{x},\dif\hat{x}')
      \;=\; \int_{\hat{E}_t} H_t(\hat{x}',y) \, \mu_{r,t}(\hat{x},\dif\hat{x}')
      \;=\; (P_{r,t}H)(\hat{x},y)
      \;=\; H_r(\hat{x},y) .
  \]
  The left-hand side is $\int H_r(\cdot,y)$ against the image measure
  $\mu_{r,t}(\hat{x},\cdot) \circ \rho_{r,t}^{-1} \in \Prob(\hat{E}_r)$, and the
  right-hand side is $\int H_r(\cdot,y) \dif\delta_{\hat{x}}$.  As this holds for
  every $y \in E_2^{(r)}$, hypothesis \ref{it:hist_sep} identifies the two
  measures.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What Lemma~\ref{lem:histrestart} would buy]
  \label{rem:histbuys}
  Remark~\ref{rem:pastingassumed} records that the existence of a restart kernel
  is ``the one place in Section~\ref{sec:uniqueness} where something must be given
  from outside'', and Section~\ref{ssec:localuniq} has no example of one that is
  not Markovian: Corollary~\ref{cor:pastingmarkov} builds it from a shift system,
  which is exactly what a path-dependent problem does not have.
  Lemma~\ref{lem:histrestart} says that a historical duality would supply one ---
  from the \emph{dual}, and with no shift anywhere.

  The consequence is not small.  Theorem~\ref{thm:localuniqueness} would then
  give local uniqueness in the sense of \JS, Definition~III.2.37 for a
  path-dependent martingale problem constructed by duality, and
  Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop} would give uniqueness in law; both without
  a Markov structure, on a problem whose solution was produced by a Markovian
  device on an enlarged space.  That is the circle
  Sections~\ref{ssec:absuniq}, \ref{ssec:localuniq} and \ref{ssec:exduality} were
  built to close, and it is the reason the programme is worth carrying out.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[The obstruction, named]
  \label{rem:histobstruction}
  What blocks the programme is \ref{it:D3}, and specifically the route to it.
  Proposition~\ref{prop:rieszmarkov} obtains the kernels from positivity by
  Riesz--Markov, and needs $E_1$ compact.  A path space $\hat{E}_t$ is Polish but
  neither compact nor, for genealogy-valued states, locally compact, so that route
  is unavailable --- this is precisely the difficulty \DGP{} flag in their
  Section~5, and it is not a technicality that a better argument removes.

  The substitute is a \emph{transform} characterization: instead of
  representing a positive linear functional on $C(E_1)$ by a measure, one
  recognizes a given function of $y$ as a Laplace transform, or a Laplace
  functional, and reads off the law.  \DGP's Proposition~2.8 is the abstract
  version, and their Example~6 is the concrete one, where the check is that
  $y \mapsto Y^y_t$ is negative definite.  For a state space of point
  configurations the corresponding statement is the classical characterization of
  a point process by its Laplace functional, and it is available.  For a state
  space of genealogies it is not, and that is where the programme currently
  stops.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{Carrying it out: the Hawkes process}

The manuscript already contains a path-dependent process whose historical dual is
classical, \emph{deterministic}, and of exactly the shape
Setting~\ref{set:historical} asks for.  This subsection verifies the hypotheses.

\begin{setting}[The Hawkes duality data; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{set:hawkesdual}
  Let $N$ be the Hawkes process of Example~\ref{ex:hawkes}, with immigration rate
  $\mu_0 > 0$ and offspring kernel $\phi \in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Rp)$, $\phi \geq 0$.
  Put
  \begin{gather*}
    \hat{E}_t = \{\text{counting measures on } [0,t] \text{ with finitely many atoms}\} , \\
    E_2 = \{ f : \Rp \to [0,\infty) \text{ bounded measurable} \} , \\
    H_t(\hat{x},f) = \exp\Bigl\{ -\int_{[0,t]} f \dif\hat{x} \Bigr\} ,
    \qquad
    \Lambda_t(\hat{x}) = \mu_0 + \int_{[0,t)} \phi(t-s) \, \hat{x}(\dif s) .
  \end{gather*}
\end{setting}

\begin{lemma}[The dual flow; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{lem:hawkesflow}
  For every $t > 0$ and $f \in E_2$ the backward equation
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:hawkesdual}
    1 - w^{(t)}_f(s) \;=\; e^{-f(s)}
      \exp\Bigl\{ - \int_s^t \phi(u-s) \, w^{(t)}_f(u) \dif u \Bigr\} ,
    \qquad s \in [0,t] ,
  \end{equation}
  has a unique solution $w^{(t)}_f : [0,t] \to [0,1]$.  Put, for $r \leq t$,
  \[
    \Theta_{r,t}f(v) \;=\; f(v) + \int_r^t \phi(u-v) \, w^{(t)}_f(u) \dif u
      \quad (v \in [0,r]) ,
    \qquad
    V_{r,t}(f) \;=\; \exp\Bigl\{ -\mu_0 \int_r^t w^{(t)}_f \Bigr\} .
  \]
  Then for $r \leq s \leq t$
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:hawkesrestriction}
    w^{(t)}_f \;=\; w^{(s)}_{\Theta_{s,t}f} \quad\text{on } [0,s] ,
  \end{equation}
  and consequently
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:hawkescocycle}
    \Theta_{r,t} = \Theta_{r,s} \circ \Theta_{s,t} ,
    \qquad
    V_{r,t}(f) = V_{s,t}(f) \cdot V_{r,s}(\Theta_{s,t}f) .
  \end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  \emph{Existence and uniqueness.}  The right-hand side of \eqref{eq:hawkesdual}
  lies in $(0,1]$, so any solution takes values in $[0,1)$.  The map
  $w \mapsto 1 - e^{-f}\exp\{-\phi * w\}$ is monotone and maps $[0,1]$-valued
  functions to $[0,1]$-valued functions, so backward iteration from $w \equiv 0$
  increases to a solution; and on an interval of length $\delta$ with
  $\int_0^\delta \phi < 1$ it is a contraction in the supremum norm, which gives
  uniqueness there and then, stepping backwards, on all of $[0,t]$.

  \emph{\eqref{eq:hawkesrestriction}.}  For $s' \in [0,s]$ split the integral in
  \eqref{eq:hawkesdual} at $s$:
  \[
    1 - w^{(t)}_f(s')
      = e^{-f(s')} \exp\Bigl\{ -\int_s^t \phi(u-s') w^{(t)}_f(u)\dif u \Bigr\}
        \cdot \exp\Bigl\{ -\int_{s'}^{s} \phi(u-s') w^{(t)}_f(u)\dif u \Bigr\} .
  \]
  The first two factors are $e^{-\Theta_{s,t}f(s')}$ by the definition of
  $\Theta$.  So $w^{(t)}_f$ restricted to $[0,s]$ satisfies \eqref{eq:hawkesdual}
  with horizon $s$ and datum $\Theta_{s,t}f$, and uniqueness gives
  \eqref{eq:hawkesrestriction}.

  \emph{\eqref{eq:hawkescocycle}.}  Both assertions are \eqref{eq:hawkesrestriction}
  inserted into the definitions, the first after splitting
  $\int_r^t = \int_r^s + \int_s^t$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The conditional duality relation; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{prop:hawkesduality}
  Let $N$ be a Hawkes process as in Setting~\ref{set:hawkesdual} and let
  $\hat{N}$ be its path lift.  Then for $r \leq t$ and $f \in E_2$,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:hawkescond}
    E\bigl[ H_t(\hat{N}_t, f) \bigm| \hat{N}_r = \hat{x} \bigr]
      \;=\; H_r\bigl( \hat{x}, \Theta_{r,t}f \bigr) \cdot V_{r,t}(f) .
  \end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  By the Hawkes--Oakes cluster representation, conditionally on the past
  $\hat{x}$ the points in $(r,t]$ form a Poisson cluster process whose immigrant
  intensity at $u \in (r,t]$ is
  $\mu_0 + \int_{[0,r]} \phi(u-s)\hat{x}(\dif s)$ --- the constant immigration
  plus the offspring of the past --- each immigrant at $u$ generating an
  independent cluster with Laplace functional $1 - w^{(t)}_f(u)$ by
  \eqref{eq:hawkesdual}.  The Poisson superposition formula
  $E[\prod_{u \in \Pi}h(u)] = \exp\{-\int(1-h)\}$ therefore gives
  \[
    E\bigl[ e^{-\int_{(r,t]} f \dif N} \bigm| \hat{N}_r = \hat{x} \bigr]
      = \exp\Bigl\{ -\int_r^t
          \Bigl( \mu_0 + \int_{[0,r]}\phi(u-v)\hat{x}(\dif v) \Bigr)
          w^{(t)}_f(u) \dif u \Bigr\} ,
  \]
  and multiplying by the deterministic factor
  $e^{-\int_{[0,r]}f\dif\hat{x}}$ and using Fubini to rewrite the double integral
  as $\int_{[0,r]}(\Theta_{r,t}f - f)\dif\hat{x}$ gives \eqref{eq:hawkescond}.
\end{proof}

Equation~\eqref{eq:hawkescond} is \eqref{eq:dualityrel} in its two-parameter form,
with a \emph{deterministic} dual --- so $E^y[\cdot]$ is evaluation --- whose
dynamics is the flow $\Theta$ and whose Feynman--Kac factor is $V$.  It is the
memory-carrying analogue of \DGP's Example~6, where the dual solves
$\dot{Y} = -\Psi(Y)$ for the branching mechanism $\Psi$; here the branching
mechanism has become a Volterra kernel and the dual runs backwards.

\begin{remark}[This duality is classical, and under three names]
  \label{rem:hawkesknown}
  Nothing in Lemma~\ref{lem:hawkesflow} or
  Proposition~\ref{prop:hawkesduality} is new, and it is worth saying under which
  names it is already available, because they are not obviously the same name.

  \emph{As a cluster representation.}  \HO{} represent a self-exciting process as
  a Poisson cluster process; the Laplace functional of a Poisson cluster process
  is $\exp\{-\int\lambda(1-G_c)\}$ with $G_c$ that of a cluster, and for a
  self-exciting cluster $G_c$ satisfies a fixed-point equation.  That is
  \eqref{eq:hawkesdual} and the second half of \eqref{eq:hawkescond}, and it is
  textbook point-process theory; see \DVJ, the chapter on Poisson cluster
  processes.

  \emph{As an exponential-affine transform formula.}  Substituting
  $\chi(s) = \int_s^t \phi(u-s) w^{(t)}_f(u)\dif u$, so that
  $w^{(t)}_f = 1 - e^{-f-\chi}$, turns \eqref{eq:hawkesdual} into
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:volterrariccati}
    \chi(s) \;=\; \int_s^t \phi(u-s) \,
      \bigl( 1 - e^{-f(u)-\chi(u)} \bigr) \dif u ,
  \end{equation}
  a backward \emph{Volterra--Riccati} equation whose nonlinearity
  $x \mapsto 1-e^{-x}$ is the branching mechanism of Poisson offspring.  For the
  exponential kernel $\phi = \alpha\beta e^{-\beta\cdot}$ the pair
  $(N,\Lambda)$ is a finite-dimensional affine Markov process and
  \eqref{eq:volterrariccati} collapses to a Riccati ODE; this is \cite{EGG10}.
  For a general kernel it is the affine Volterra theory of \ALP, and the rough
  Heston analogue is \cite{ER19}.

  \emph{As a Markovian lift.}  \CT{} lift stochastic Volterra processes to
  Markov processes on a space of forward curves and characterize them by a
  generalized Feller semigroup.  That is Definition~\ref{def:pathlift} carried
  out properly --- the lift that \emph{keeps} the generator, in the sense of
  Remark~\ref{rem:liftform} --- and \eqref{eq:volterrariccati} is the transform
  formula of the lifted process.

  \emph{What is not classical} is only the packaging.  The verification below
  reads the transform formula as \ref{it:D1}--\ref{it:D3} of
  Setting~\ref{set:dualdata}, and Corollary~\ref{cor:hawkesrestart} reads it as a
  restart kernel in the sense of \JS, Definition~III.2.37.  Both are
  rearrangements of known facts, and neither is claimed as new mathematics; what
  they buy is recorded in Remark~\ref{rem:hawkesscope}.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[The remaining hypotheses; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{prop:hawkesDcheck}
  In Setting~\ref{set:hawkesdual}:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item \label{it:hd_balance}
    \emph{Balance, in integrated form.}  For every $\hat{x} \in \hat{E}_r$,
    $f \in E_2$ and $r \leq t$,
    \begin{equation}
      \label{eq:hawkesD1}
      H_r(\hat{x},\Theta_{r,t}f)\,V_{r,t}(f) \;-\; H_r(\hat{x},f)
      \;=\; \int_r^t
        E\bigl[ \Lambda_u(\hat{N}_u)\bigl(e^{-f(u)}-1\bigr) H_u(\hat{N}_u,f)
          \bigm| \hat{N}_r = \hat{x} \bigr] \dif u ,
    \end{equation}
    which is the two-parameter form of \ref{it:D1} with
    $g_u(\hat{x}',f) = \Lambda_u(\hat{x}')(e^{-f(u)}-1)H_u(\hat{x}',f)$ ---
    exactly $\mathcal{A}_u$ of \eqref{eq:pathgen} with
    $\mu(u,\hat{x}',\cdot) = \delta_{\hat{x}'(u-)+1}$ applied to
    $H_u(\cdot,f)$.  So the martingale problem \eqref{eq:dualMP} the duality
    produces is the Hawkes martingale problem of Example~\ref{ex:hawkes}.
    At a point $r$ at which $f$ is continuous and which is a Lebesgue point of
    $u \mapsto \Lambda_u(\hat{x})$ --- automatic for a.e.\ $r$, and for every $r$
    when $\phi$ is continuous and bounded --- dividing \eqref{eq:hawkesD1} by
    $t-r$ and letting $t \downarrow r$ recovers the generator relation
    \[
      \frac{\dif}{\dif t}\Big|_{t=r^+}
        H_r(\hat{x},\Theta_{r,t}f) \, V_{r,t}(f)
      \;=\; \Lambda_r(\hat{x}) \bigl( e^{-f(r)} - 1 \bigr) H_r(\hat{x},f) .
    \]
  \item \label{it:hd_sep}
    \emph{\ref{it:D2}.}  $\operatorname{span}\{H_r(\cdot,f) : f \in E_2\}$ is
    separating on $\Prob(\hat{E}_r)$, Laplace functionals determining the law of a
    point process.
  \item \label{it:hd_kernel}
    \emph{\ref{it:D3}.}  $1 - w^{(t)}_f$ is the Laplace functional of a point
    process on $[0,t]$, and consequently the right-hand side of
    \eqref{eq:hawkescond} is the Laplace functional of a point process on $[0,t]$
    extending $\hat{x}$.  The kernels $\mu_{r,t}(\hat{x},\cdot)$ of \ref{it:D3}
    are its laws.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  \ref{it:hd_balance}  \emph{The integrated identity comes from the process, not
  from the flow.}  By Proposition~\ref{prop:hawkesduality} the left-hand side of
  \eqref{eq:hawkesD1} is
  $E[H_t(\hat{N}_t,f) - H_r(\hat{N}_r,f) \mid \hat{N}_r = \hat{x}]$.  Now
  $M_t \coloneqq e^{-\int_{[0,t]}f\dif N}$ jumps by
  $M_{u-}(e^{-f(u)}-1)$ at each point $u$ of $N$ and is constant in between, so
  \[
    M_t - M_r \;=\; \int_{(r,t]} M_{u-}\bigl(e^{-f(u)}-1\bigr) \dif N_u ,
  \]
  an exact pathwise identity; and compensating the integral --- the same
  bracket-by-bracket computation as in Steps~1--2 of the proof of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:pathjumpMP}, with the bounded integrand
  $M_{u-}(e^{-f(u)}-1)$ in place of $f(Y_{n+1})-f(Y_n)$, and with
  $E[N_t] < \infty$ from Example~\ref{ex:hawkes} licensing the expectations ---
  gives
  \[
    E\bigl[ M_t - M_r \bigm| \Filt^N_r \bigr]
      \;=\; E\Bigl[ \int_r^t \Lambda_u(\hat{N}_u) M_{u}\bigl(e^{-f(u)}-1\bigr)
        \dif u \Bigm| \Filt^N_r \Bigr] ,
  \]
  where $M_{u-}$ may be replaced by $M_u$ under the $\dif u$-integral, the paths
  being piecewise constant with finitely many jumps.  This is
  \eqref{eq:hawkesD1}.

  \emph{The generator relation.}  Write
  $A(t) = \int_r^t \Lambda^{\hat{x}}_u w^{(t)}_f(u)\dif u$
  with $\Lambda^{\hat{x}}_u = \mu_0 + \int_{[0,r]}\phi(u-v)\hat{x}(\dif v)$, so
  that the left-hand side of the derivative claim is
  $H_r(\hat x,f) \cdot \frac{\dif}{\dif t}e^{-A(t)}$ at
  $t = r$.  As $t \downarrow r$, $w^{(t)}_f(u) \to 1 - e^{-f(u)}$ uniformly on
  $[r,t]$ --- from \eqref{eq:hawkesdual}, since
  $\int_u^t \phi(v-u)w(v)\dif v \leq \int_0^{t-r}\phi \to 0$ --- so
  \[
    \frac{A(t)}{t-r} \;=\; \frac{1}{t-r}\int_r^t \Lambda^{\hat{x}}_u
      \bigl(1 - e^{-f(u)}\bigr) \dif u + o(1)
    \;\longrightarrow\; \Lambda^{\hat{x}}_r \bigl(1 - e^{-f(r)}\bigr) ,
  \]
  the convergence of the average being exactly the Lebesgue-point hypothesis on
  $\Lambda^{\hat{x}}$ together with continuity of $f$ at $r$;
  note that $u \mapsto \Lambda^{\hat{x}}_u$ is locally integrable but, for
  $\phi$ merely locally integrable, not continuous, which is why the hypothesis
  cannot be dropped.  Also
  $\Lambda^{\hat{x}}_r = \Lambda_r(\hat{x})$, and the claim follows from
  $\frac{\dif}{\dif t}e^{-A(t)}\vert_{t=r} = -A'(r^+)$, since $A(r) = 0$.  That
  the integrand of \eqref{eq:hawkesD1} is $\mathcal{A}_uH_u(\cdot,f)$ is
  \eqref{eq:pathgen}: adding one point at $u$ multiplies
  $H_u(\hat{x}',f)$ by $e^{-f(u)}$.

  \ref{it:hd_sep}  Standard.

  \ref{it:hd_kernel}  Let $G_0(s) = e^{-f(s)}$ and
  $G_{n+1}(s) = e^{-f(s)}\exp\{-\int_s^t\phi(u-s)(1-G_n(u))\dif u\}$.  By
  induction $G_n$ is the Laplace functional of the cluster started at $s$ and
  truncated after $n$ generations: $G_0$ is that of the single point $\delta_s$,
  and the recursion superposes onto it a Poisson process of intensity
  $\phi(\cdot - s)$ of independent $n$-generation clusters, whose Laplace
  functional is the exponential factor.  The truncated clusters increase with
  $n$, so $G_n$ decreases, to $1 - w^{(t)}_f$ by uniqueness in
  Lemma~\ref{lem:hawkesflow}.  The limiting cluster has finitely many points a.s.,
  its expected size being $\sum_{n\geq 0}\lVert \phi^{*n}\rVert_{L^1[0,t]}$, which is
  finite because the Volterra resolvent of $\phi$ exists in
  $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for every $\phi \in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$.  So it is a point
  process and $1 - w^{(t)}_f$ is its Laplace functional; the second assertion
  follows by one more Poisson superposition, as in the proof of
  Proposition~\ref{prop:hawkesduality}.  \emph{Note that this argument does not
  presuppose the existence of $N$}: it constructs the cluster from the branching
  recursion alone.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The restart kernel]
  \label{cor:hawkesrestart}
  Take $E_2^{(r)} = \{f \in E_2 : f = 0 \text{ on } (r,\infty)\}$.  Then
  $w^{(t)}_f \equiv 0$ on $[r,t]$, hence $\Theta_{r,t}f = f$ and
  $V_{r,t}(f) = 1$; hypotheses \ref{it:hist_past}--\ref{it:hist_sep} of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:histrestart} hold, and therefore
  \[
    \mu_{r,t}(\hat{x},\cdot) \circ \rho_{r,t}^{-1} \;=\; \delta_{\hat{x}} :
  \]
  the kernels of Proposition~\ref{prop:hawkesDcheck}\ref{it:hd_kernel} form a
  restart kernel in the sense of Definition~\ref{def:restartkernel}.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  For $f$ vanishing on $(r,t]$, equation \eqref{eq:hawkesdual} on $[r,t]$ reads
  $1 - w(s) = \exp\{-\int_s^t\phi(u-s)w(u)\dif u\}$, which $w \equiv 0$ solves;
  uniqueness gives $w^{(t)}_f \equiv 0$ there, and the two displays follow.
  Hypothesis \ref{it:hist_past} holds because $H_t(\hat{x}',f)$ then depends on
  $\hat{x}'$ only through its restriction to $[0,r]$, \ref{it:hist_rest} is
  $\Theta_{r,t}f = f$ together with $V_{r,t}(f) = 1$, and \ref{it:hist_sep} is
  Proposition~\ref{prop:hawkesDcheck}\ref{it:hd_sep} applied on $\hat{E}_r$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What this settles, and what it does not]
  \label{rem:hawkesscope}
  \emph{Settled.}  Setting~\ref{set:historical} is not empty: there is a
  path-dependent martingale problem --- the Hawkes problem of
  Example~\ref{ex:hawkes}, whose rate is a functional of the whole past --- for
  which the historical duality data exist, the dual is deterministic, and
  \ref{it:D1}--\ref{it:D3} all hold.  The dual dynamics is a backward nonlinear
  Volterra flow, and the two-parameter structure of
  Remark~\ref{rem:exdualityscope} is not a convenience here but forced, the
  fibres $\hat{E}_t$ growing with $t$.

  \emph{The payoff is Corollary~\ref{cor:hawkesrestart}, not existence.}  The
  verification of \ref{it:D3} constructs the cluster, and assembling the Hawkes
  process from clusters is the Hawkes--Oakes construction; so on the existence
  side the duality route reproduces a known argument rather than replacing one.
  What is genuinely new is the restart kernel.
  Remark~\ref{rem:pastingassumed} records that its existence is ``the one place
  in Section~\ref{sec:uniqueness} where something must be given from outside'',
  and Section~\ref{ssec:localuniq} had no non-Markovian instance:
  Corollary~\ref{cor:pastingmarkov} builds one from a shift system, which a
  path-dependent problem does not have.  Corollary~\ref{cor:hawkesrestart} is
  such an instance, and with it Theorem~\ref{thm:localuniqueness} gives local
  uniqueness in the sense of \JS, Definition~III.2.37 for the Hawkes martingale
  problem, with no shift anywhere.

  \emph{Not settled.}  The general programme.  What made the verification
  possible is that the state space is one of point configurations, where Laplace
  functionals both separate (\ref{it:D2}) and characterize
  (\ref{it:D3}); Remark~\ref{rem:histobstruction} explains that for a genealogy
  space neither is available, and nothing here helps there.

  \emph{Novelty.}  None is claimed on the analytic side: see
  Remark~\ref{rem:hawkesknown}.  The duality is the exponential-affine transform
  formula for Hawkes processes, and what is done here is to check that it
  satisfies \ref{it:D1}--\ref{it:D3} and to extract
  Corollary~\ref{cor:hawkesrestart} from it.

  \emph{Numerics.}  Equation~\eqref{eq:hawkescond} and the cocycle
  \eqref{eq:hawkescocycle} were checked against a cluster simulation and against
  a discretization of \eqref{eq:hawkesdual}; the relative errors were $4 \cdot 10^{-4}$
  and $10^{-4}$, against a Monte Carlo standard error of $2 \cdot 10^{-3}$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Jump processes}
\label{ssec:jump}

Throughout this subsection $\T = \Rp$ with $q$ Lebesgue measure, so that the two
conventions of Definition~\ref{def:clock} agree and $\iota$ plays no r\^ole.  The
state space needs only \eqref{E0} for the construction and the martingale property;
\eqref{E2} enters only when one wants to say that the paths lie in $\DE$.

\begin{setting}[Jump data; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{set:jumpdata}
  Let $\lambda : E \to [0,\infty)$ be measurable (the \emph{rate}) and let $\mu$ be
  a transition kernel on $E$ (the \emph{jump distribution}).  Define
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:jumpgen}
    A f(x) \;=\; \lambda(x) \int_E \bigl( f(y) - f(x) \bigr) \, \mu(x,\dif y) ,
    \qquad f \in \Bdd(E) ,
  \end{equation}
  and $A = \{(f, Af) : f \in \Bdd(E)\}$.  If
  $\bar{\lambda} \coloneqq \sup_x \lambda(x) < \infty$ then $A$ maps $\Bdd(E)$ into
  itself with $\lVert Af \rVert \leq 2 \bar{\lambda} \lVert f \rVert$; in general
  $Af$ need not be bounded.
\end{setting}

\begin{definition}[The construction; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{def:jumpconstruction}
  Let $\nu \in \Prob(E)$.  On some probability space let $(Y_n)_{n \geq 0}$ be a
  Markov chain with initial distribution $\nu$ and transition kernel $\mu$, and
  let $\varepsilon_1, \varepsilon_2, \dots$ be i.i.d.\ exponential random
  variables of parameter $1$, independent of $(Y_n)$.  Put
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:jumptimes}
    \tau_0 = 0 , \qquad
    \tau_{n+1} = \tau_n + \frac{\varepsilon_{n+1}}{\lambda(Y_n)} ,
    \qquad \zeta = \lim_n \tau_n ,
  \end{equation}
  with the convention $1/0 = \infty$, and
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:jumpprocess}
    X_t = Y_n \quad \text{for } \tau_n \leq t < \tau_{n+1} , \qquad t < \zeta .
  \end{equation}
  We call $\zeta$ the \emph{explosion time} and $(\tau_n)$ the \emph{jump times}.
\end{definition}

Three observations before the theorem.  The paths of $X$ are piecewise constant
and right continuous by construction, hence lie in $\DE$ as soon as $E$ carries a
topology, i.e.\ under \eqref{E2}.  If $\lambda(Y_n) = 0$ then $\tau_{n+1} = \infty$ and
$X$ is absorbed at $Y_n$.  And each $\tau_n$ is a functional of the path --- the
time of its $n$-th jump --- which is what will make it a legitimate localizing
sequence in the sense of \ref{it:L1}.

\begin{lemma}[No explosion for bounded rates; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{lem:noexplosion}
  If $\bar{\lambda} < \infty$ then $\zeta = \infty$ almost surely.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  $\tau_{n+1} - \tau_n \geq \varepsilon_{n+1}/\bar{\lambda}$, and
  $\sum_n \varepsilon_{n+1}/\bar{\lambda} = \infty$ a.s.\ by the strong law of
  large numbers, the $\varepsilon_n$ being i.i.d.\ with mean $1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Jump processes solve their martingale problem; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{thm:jumpMP}
  Let $\bar{\lambda} < \infty$ and let $X$ be as in
  Definition~\ref{def:jumpconstruction}.  Then $X$ solves the martingale problem
  for $(A,\nu)$ of \eqref{eq:jumpgen}.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Fix $f \in \Bdd(E)$ and write $\mu f(x) = \int f(y)\mu(x,\dif y)$, so that
  $Af = \lambda \cdot (\mu f - f)$.  Let $\Gilt_t = \sigma(X_u : u \leq t)$ and
  \[
    M_t \;=\; f(X_t) - f(X_0) - \int_0^t Af(X_r) \dif r .
  \]

  \emph{Step 1: the paths make both terms explicit.}  Since $X$ is constant on
  $[\tau_n, \tau_{n+1})$,
  \begin{align*}
    \int_0^t Af(X_r)\dif r
      &= \sum_{n \geq 0} Af(Y_n) \bigl( (\tau_{n+1} \wedge t) - (\tau_n \wedge t) \bigr) , \\
    f(X_t) - f(X_0)
      &= \sum_{n \geq 1} \bigl( f(Y_n) - f(Y_{n-1}) \bigr) \one_{\{\tau_n \leq t\}} ,
  \end{align*}
  both sums being finite a.s.\ by Lemma~\ref{lem:noexplosion}.  Hence
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:jumpbrackets}
    M_t \;=\; \sum_{n \geq 0} D_n , \qquad
    D_n = \bigl( f(Y_{n+1}) - f(Y_n) \bigr) \one_{\{\tau_{n+1} \leq t\}}
        - Af(Y_n) \bigl( (\tau_{n+1} \wedge t) - (\tau_n \wedge t) \bigr) .
  \end{equation}

  \emph{Step 2: each bracket is a martingale difference.}  Let
  $\mathcal{H}_n = \sigma(Y_0,\dots,Y_n,\tau_1,\dots,\tau_n)$.  Conditionally on
  $\mathcal{H}_n$, and on the event $\{\tau_n \leq t\}$, put $x = Y_n$,
  $\lambda = \lambda(x)$ and $a = t - \tau_n \geq 0$.  By construction
  $\tau_{n+1} - \tau_n$ is exponential with parameter $\lambda$ and independent of
  $Y_{n+1} \sim \mu(x,\cdot)$, so
  \begin{align*}
    E\bigl[ (f(Y_{n+1}) - f(Y_n)) \one_{\{\tau_{n+1} \leq t\}} \bigm| \mathcal{H}_n \bigr]
      &= \bigl( \mu f(x) - f(x) \bigr) \bigl( 1 - e^{-\lambda a} \bigr) , \\
    E\bigl[ (\tau_{n+1} \wedge t) - \tau_n \bigm| \mathcal{H}_n \bigr]
      &= E\bigl[ \mathrm{Exp}(\lambda) \wedge a \bigr]
       = \frac{1 - e^{-\lambda a}}{\lambda} .
  \end{align*}
  Multiplying the second identity by
  $Af(x) = \lambda(\mu f(x) - f(x))$ gives exactly the first.  If $\lambda = 0$
  both displays are read as $0$ --- then $\tau_{n+1} = \infty$, so the first term
  of $D_n$ vanishes, and $Af(x) = 0$, so the second does too.  Hence
  $E[D_n \mid \mathcal{H}_n] = 0$ on $\{\tau_n \leq t\}$, and $D_n = 0$ on
  $\{\tau_n > t\}$, an event in $\mathcal{H}_n$.  \emph{This cancellation --- the
  factor $\lambda$ in \eqref{eq:jumpgen} against the mean $1/\lambda$ of the
  holding time --- is the whole content of the theorem.}

  \emph{Step 3: summation.}  The family $(D_n)$ is dominated:
  \[
    \sum_{n \geq 0} |D_n|
      \;\leq\; 2\lVert f\rVert N_t + \lVert Af\rVert \, t ,
    \qquad N_t \coloneqq \#\{n \geq 1 : \tau_n \leq t\} ,
  \]
  because the second terms telescope.  Since $\tau_{n+1}-\tau_n \geq
  \varepsilon_{n+1}/\bar\lambda$, the variable $N_t$ is stochastically dominated
  by a Poisson variable of parameter $\bar\lambda t$ and is in particular
  integrable.  So dominated convergence applies to \eqref{eq:jumpbrackets}, and
  Step~2 gives
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:jumpmeanzero}
    E[M_t] \;=\; \sum_{n \geq 0} E\bigl[ E[D_n \mid \mathcal{H}_n] \bigr] \;=\; 0
    \qquad \text{for every } t \geq 0
  \end{equation}
  and every initial law $\nu$.

  \emph{Step 4: the Markov property of the construction.}  Fix $s \geq 0$ and let
  $N_s$ be as above.  On $\{N_s = n\}$ the $\sigma$-field
  $\Gilt_s = \sigma(X_u : u \leq s)$ is generated by
  $Y_0,\dots,Y_n$, $\tau_1,\dots,\tau_n$ and the event $\{\tau_{n+1} > s\}$.
  Conditionally on these, the residual holding time $\tau_{n+1} - s$ is again
  exponential with parameter $\lambda(Y_n)$ --- memorylessness --- and is
  independent of $(Y_{n+1}, Y_{n+2}, \dots)$ and of
  $\varepsilon_{n+2}, \varepsilon_{n+3}, \dots$.  Hence, conditionally on
  $\Gilt_s$, the process $(X_{s+u})_{u \geq 0}$ is again a process of
  Definition~\ref{def:jumpconstruction}, with the same $\lambda$ and $\mu$ and
  with initial state $X_s$.

  \emph{Step 5: conclusion.}  For $s \leq t$,
  \[
    M_t - M_s \;=\; \bigl( f(X_t) - f(X_s) \bigr) - \int_s^t Af(X_r) \dif r
  \]
  is the variable \eqref{eq:jumpbrackets} at time $t-s$ for the restarted process
  of Step~4.  By Step~4 and \eqref{eq:jumpmeanzero} applied with $\nu = \delta_{X_s}$,
  \[
    E\bigl[ M_t - M_s \bigm| \Gilt_s \bigr] \;=\; 0 .
  \]
  As $|M_t| \leq 2\lVert f\rVert + 2\bar{\lambda}\lVert f\rVert t$ is bounded on
  bounded intervals, $M$ is a $(\Gilt_t)$-martingale, and since
  ${}^{*}\Filt^X_t = \Filt^X_t = \Gilt_t$ for the right continuous $X$
  (Section~\ref{ssec:notation}), the same holds for the filtration of
  Definition~\ref{def:markovMP}.
\end{proof}

The construction gives existence.  Uniqueness comes from
Section~\ref{sec:uniqueness}, and the input it needs is a Gronwall estimate.

\begin{proposition}[Well-posedness for bounded rates; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E2}]
  \label{prop:jumpwellposed}
  Let $\bar{\lambda} < \infty$.  Then the martingale problem for $(A,\nu)$ is well
  posed for every $\nu \in \Prob(E)$, every solution is a Markov process, and if
  $E$ satisfies \eqref{E2} it is strong Markov.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  Existence is Theorem~\ref{thm:jumpMP}.  For uniqueness it suffices, by
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}, to show that the one-dimensional distributions are
  determined by $\nu$, and since $\Bdd(E)$ is separating it suffices to determine
  $E[f(X_t)]$ for every $f \in \Bdd(E)$.  We show that they are given by the
  exponential of a \emph{bounded} operator.

  \emph{Step 1: $A$ is bounded on $\Bdd(E)$.}  For $f \in \Bdd(E)$,
  $\lVert Af \rVert \leq 2\bar\lambda \lVert f \rVert$ by Setting~\ref{set:jumpdata},
  so $A^k f \in \Bdd(E)$ is defined for every $k \geq 0$ with
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:Akbound}
    \lVert A^k f \rVert \;\leq\; (2\bar\lambda)^k \lVert f \rVert .
  \end{equation}
  This is the only place where $\bar\lambda < \infty$ is used, and it is what
  makes the iteration below converge.

  \emph{Step 2: one step of the iteration.}  Let $X$ be any solution of the
  martingale problem for $(A,\nu)$ and let $h \in \Bdd(E)$.  Applying the
  martingale property to the pair $(h,Ah) \in A$ between $0$ and $t$ and taking
  expectations gives
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:jumpiter}
    E[h(X_t)] \;=\; E[h(X_0)] + \int_0^t E\bigl[ Ah(X_r) \bigr] \dif r ,
  \end{equation}
  Fubini being legitimate because $Ah$ is bounded.  Note that
  \eqref{eq:jumpiter} is available for \emph{every} $h \in \Bdd(E)$, which is what
  allows it to be iterated; a martingale problem posed on a smaller domain would
  not.

  \emph{Step 3: the iteration.}  Insert \eqref{eq:jumpiter} into itself, starting
  from $h = f$ and using it at each stage for $h = A^k f$:
  \[
    E[f(X_t)] \;=\; \sum_{k=0}^{n} \frac{t^k}{k!} \, E\bigl[ A^k f(X_0) \bigr]
      \;+\; R_n , \qquad
    R_n = \int_{\Delta_{n+1}(t)} E\bigl[ A^{n+1}f(X_{r_{n+1}}) \bigr] \dif r ,
  \]
  where $\Delta_{n+1}(t) = \{0 \leq r_{n+1} \leq \dots \leq r_1 \leq t\}$ is the
  simplex of volume $t^{n+1}/(n+1)!$.  By \eqref{eq:Akbound},
  \[
    |R_n| \;\leq\; \frac{t^{n+1}}{(n+1)!} \, (2\bar\lambda)^{n+1} \lVert f\rVert
      \;\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\; 0 .
  \]
  Hence
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:jumpexp}
    E[f(X_t)] \;=\; \sum_{k \geq 0} \frac{t^k}{k!} \int_E A^k f \dif\nu
      \;=\; \int_E \bigl( e^{tA} f \bigr) \dif\nu ,
  \end{equation}
  the series converging absolutely in $\Bdd(E)$ by \eqref{eq:Akbound}.  The right
  hand side depends only on $A$ and $\nu$, so the one-dimensional distributions
  are determined and Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq} applies.

  The strong Markov property is Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov}, whose
  hypotheses hold because the paths are piecewise constant --- so the shift system
  is measurable --- and $M$ has increments bounded on bounded intervals.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Which contraction, and where Gronwall really sits]
  \label{rem:jumpgronwall}
  The proof above is Picard iteration for the linear equation $\dot v = Av$ in the
  Banach space $\Bdd(E)$, and \eqref{eq:Akbound} is the estimate that makes the
  Picard operator a contraction on every bounded time interval; Gronwall's lemma
  would serve equally well in place of the explicit remainder $R_n$.  It is worth
  contrasting it with the \emph{first-jump equation}
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:firstjump}
    v(t,x) \;=\; e^{-\lambda(x)t} f(x)
      + \int_0^t \lambda(x) e^{-\lambda(x)s}
        \bigl( \mu\, v(t-s,\cdot) \bigr)(x) \dif s ,
  \end{equation}
  which one is tempted to use instead, and which yields the same conclusion by
  Gronwall.  The temptation should be resisted at this point: \eqref{eq:firstjump}
  presupposes that the solution \emph{is} a jump process with the prescribed first
  jump law, and that is a statement about the path structure which the martingale
  problem does not hand over --- Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar} characterizes
  finite-dimensional distributions, not jump times.  Equation \eqref{eq:firstjump}
  is a valid identity for the constructed process of
  Definition~\ref{def:jumpconstruction}, where it follows by conditioning on
  $\tau_1$; but as an argument for \emph{uniqueness} it would be circular.  The
  iteration of Step~3 avoids the issue because it uses nothing but the martingale
  property.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Explosion, and why localization is not academic]
  \label{rem:jumpexplosion}
  Drop the assumption $\bar\lambda < \infty$.  Then $\zeta$ may be finite with
  positive probability --- take $E = \N$, $\mu(n,\cdot) = \delta_{n+1}$ and
  $\lambda(n) = 2^n$, so that $E[\zeta] = \sum_n 2^{-n} < \infty$ --- and
  \eqref{eq:jumpprocess} defines $X$ only on $[0,\zeta)$.  There is then no
  solution of the \emph{global} martingale problem for $A$ on $\Rp$, and the
  honest statement is the local one:
  \begin{itemize}
  \item Step~2 of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:jumpMP} is a finite computation
    and is unaffected, so $M^{\tau_n}$ is a martingale for every $n$;
  \item $\tau_n$ is a functional of the path, the same for every solution, and
    $\tau_n \uparrow \zeta$;
  \item hence $(\tau_n)$ is a localizing system in the sense of
    Definition~\ref{def:localizing}, and \ref{it:L1} holds by construction rather
    than by assumption --- exactly as Lemma~\ref{lem:L1auto} predicts, since the
    stopped test processes are bounded.
  \end{itemize}
  So $X$ solves the local martingale problem for $A$ on $[0,\zeta)$, and
  Section~\ref{ssec:localmp} applies verbatim.  This is the example that
  Definition~\ref{def:localizing} was written for; up to this point it had none.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Discrete time, and the two conventions]
  \label{rem:jumpdiscrete}
  Deleting the holding times from Definition~\ref{def:jumpconstruction} leaves the
  chain $(Y_n)$ itself, which is the case $\T = \N_0$ with $q$ counting measure.
  There $A = \mu - I$ under the predictable convention $\iota = \mathrm{p}$, so
  that $M_n = f(Y_n) - f(Y_0) - \sum_{k<n}(\mu f - f)(Y_k)$ is the Doob
  decomposition; under $\iota = \mathrm{o}$ one would obtain the condition
  $\mu f - f = \mu(\mu f - f)$ instead, which is not what one wants.  This is the
  concrete form of Remark~\ref{rem:convention}, and it is also the case in which
  Corollary~\ref{cor:dualdiscrete} lives.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[What this example is for]
  \label{rem:jumppoint}
  Besides being the only explicit solution in the manuscript, the construction
  exercises three of its abstractions and is the reason they were made.  The state
  space needs only \eqref{E0}: $\lambda$ measurable and $\mu$ a kernel, no topology, no
  metric, no Polish structure --- so jump processes on a standard Borel $E$, for
  instance on a space of measures or of distributions, are covered by
  Theorem~\ref{thm:jumpMP} as it stands.  The localizing system of
  Remark~\ref{rem:jumpexplosion} is a genuine instance of \ref{it:L1}--\ref{it:L3}.
  And the discrete case of Remark~\ref{rem:jumpdiscrete} is a genuine instance of a
  clock with atoms.
\end{remark}

\subsection{From stochastic differential equations}
\label{ssec:exsde}

For $E = \R^d$ and continuous paths there is a route that is more important than
all the others put together, and we state it without proofs.  Let
$\sigma : \R^d \to \R^{d \times m}$ and $b : \R^d \to \R^d$ be measurable, put
$a = \sigma\sigma^{\top}$, and let
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:sdegen}
  A f(x) \;=\; \tfrac{1}{2} a^{ij}(x) \partial_{ij} f(x) + b^i(x) \partial_i f(x) ,
  \qquad f \in C^\infty_c(\R^d) .
\end{equation}
A \emph{weak solution} of the equation $(\sigma,b)$ is a pair $(X,B)$ on some
filtered probability space with $B$ an $m$-dimensional Brownian motion and
$X_t = X_0 + \int_0^t \sigma(X_s)\dif B_s + \int_0^t b(X_s)\dif s$.

\begin{fact}[Picard--Lindel\"of for SDEs]
  \label{fact:picard}
  If $\sigma$ and $b$ are Lipschitz, then for every $x$ the equation
  $(\sigma,b)$ has a strong solution with $X_0 = x$, and pathwise uniqueness
  holds.  The proof is Picard iteration in $L^2$, with the It\^o isometry in place
  of the triangle inequality.
\end{fact}

\begin{fact}[Yamada--Watanabe]
  \label{fact:yamadawatanabe}
  Pathwise uniqueness together with weak existence implies uniqueness in law.
\end{fact}

\begin{fact}[Stroock--Varadhan; \KA, Theorem 32.7]
  \label{fact:strookvaradhan}
  For progressive $\sigma, b$ and $P \in \Prob(C_{\Rp,\R^d})$ the following are
  equivalent: $P$ is the law of a weak solution of $(\sigma,b)$; and $P$ solves
  the local martingale problem for $A$ of \eqref{eq:sdegen}.
\end{fact}

\begin{corollary}[Well-posedness for Lipschitz coefficients; \eqref{T3}, $E = \R^d$]
  \label{cor:sdewellposed}
  If $\sigma$ and $b$ are Lipschitz, the local martingale problem for $A$ of
  \eqref{eq:sdegen} is well posed for every initial distribution, and its solution
  is a strong Markov process.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Facts~\ref{fact:picard} and \ref{fact:yamadawatanabe} give weak existence and
  uniqueness in law; Fact~\ref{fact:strookvaradhan} transfers both to the local
  martingale problem.  The Markov properties are
  Theorems~\ref{thm:localuniq} and \ref{thm:absstrongmarkov}, applied with the
  localizing system of Remark~\ref{rem:localhyp} --- which is where
  \ref{it:L1}--\ref{it:L3} were verified for exactly this operator --- or
  directly \KA, Theorem~32.11.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What a proof would cost]
  \label{rem:sdecost}
  Section~\ref{ssec:ch2} contains nothing about stochastic integration, and this
  is the only place in the manuscript that reaches outside \EK, Chapters~1--3.  A
  self-contained treatment of Corollary~\ref{cor:sdewellposed} would need the
  It\^o integral against a continuous local martingale, quadratic variation and
  the Kunita--Watanabe inequality, It\^o's formula, and L\'evy's characterization
  of Brownian motion --- the last being what turns a solution of the martingale
  problem back into a solution of the equation in Fact~\ref{fact:strookvaradhan}.
  That is a chapter, not a section, and it is deliberately not attempted here.

  For the formalization the situation is less bad than it looks: the
  \texttt{brownian-motion} development of Remark~\ref{rem:bmrepo} is building
  stochastic integration in Lean, so the dependency may become available rather
  than having to be created.  Until then, Section~\ref{ssec:jump} is the route
  that can actually be carried out.
\end{remark}

\subsection{By convergence: the Ethier--Kurtz version}
\label{ssec:EKconv}

The remaining route obtains solutions as weak limits of solutions of approximating
martingale problems.  We state the classical result first, and then the abstract
theorem of which it is a corollary; its proof below is a forward reference, and
deliberately so --- the point of the arrangement is that
Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv} has no proof of its own.


\begin{lemma}[\EK, Lemma 4.5.1; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{lem:EKconv}
  Assume \eqref{E3}, let $A \subset \Cb(E) \times \Cb(E)$ and
  $A_n \subset \Bdd(E) \times \Bdd(E)$ for $n \in \N$.  Suppose that for each
  $(f,g) \in A$ there exist $(f_n,g_n) \in A_n$ with
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:approxA}
    \lim_{n \to \infty} \lVert f_n - f \rVert = 0,
    \qquad
    \lim_{n \to \infty} \lVert g_n - g \rVert = 0 .
  \end{equation}
  If for each $n$ the process $X_n$ is a solution of the martingale problem for
  $A_n$ with sample paths in $\DE$, and if $X_n \wto X$ in $\DE$, then $X$ is a
  solution of the martingale problem for $A$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  We verify \ref{it:C1}--\ref{it:C3} of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}, with
  $F = \DE$ --- Polish by \eqref{E3} and Theorem~\ref{thm:DEpolish} --- and
  $\XX = \XX_A(X)$, whose canonical version is
  $Y^\circ_t(\omega) = f(\omega(t)) - \int_0^t g(\omega(u))\dif u$ for
  $(f,g) \in A$.  Fix $(f,g) \in A$ and an approximating sequence $(f_n,g_n)$ as
  in \eqref{eq:approxA}, and put
  \[
    D \;=\; \{ u \geq 0 : P\{X(u) = X(u-)\} = 1 \} ,
  \]
  which has countable complement by Fact~\ref{fact:Dcountable} and is therefore
  dense; $\T = \Rp$ has no greatest element, so the proviso in
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} on $D$ is vacuous.

  \emph{\ref{it:C1} and \ref{it:C2}.}  The first is the assumed weak convergence
  $X_n \wto X$ on $\DE$.  For the second take $\ZZ^\circ$ to be the determining
  set of Example~\ref{ex:determining} built from $D$, i.e.\ the products
  $Z^\circ_s = \prod_{i \leq k} h_i(\pi_{t_i})$ with $h_i \in \Cb(E)$ and
  $t_i \in D \cap \T_{\leq s}$.

  \emph{\ref{it:C3a}.}  For $t \in D$ the evaluation $\omega \mapsto \omega(t)$ is
  $J_1$-continuous at every $\omega$ with $\omega(t) = \omega(t-)$, hence
  $P X^{-1}$-a.s.  The integral term is continuous at \emph{every} $\omega$: if
  $\omega_m \to \omega$ in $\DE$, then $\omega_m(u) \to \omega(u)$ for every $u$
  outside the at most countable set of discontinuities of $\omega$, hence Lebesgue
  almost everywhere, so $g(\omega_m(u)) \to g(\omega(u))$ by continuity of $g$ and
  bounded convergence gives
  $\int_0^t g(\omega_m(u))\dif u \to \int_0^t g(\omega(u))\dif u$.  So $Y^\circ_t$
  is $P$-continuous at $X$, and so is $Y^\circ_t Z^\circ_s$, the factor
  $Z^\circ_s$ being a product of evaluations at times in $D$.

  \emph{\ref{it:C3b}.}  $f$ and $g$ are bounded, so
  $|Y^\circ_r| \leq \lVert f\rVert + t\lVert g\rVert$ for $r \leq t$, and a
  uniformly bounded family is uniformly integrable.

  \emph{\ref{it:C3c}.}  Write
  $Y^{\circ,n}_t(\omega) = f_n(\omega(t)) - \int_0^t g_n(\omega(u))\dif u$.  Since
  $X_n$ solves the martingale problem for $A_n$ and $(f_n,g_n) \in A_n$,
  Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar} gives
  \[
    E^{P^n}\bigl[ \bigl( Y^{\circ,n}_t(X_n) - Y^{\circ,n}_s(X_n) \bigr)
      Z^\circ_s(X_n) \bigr] \;=\; 0
  \]
  for all $s \leq t$ and $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$.  Moreover
  \[
    \bigl| Y^\circ_r(\omega) - Y^{\circ,n}_r(\omega) \bigr|
      \;\leq\; \lVert f_n - f \rVert + r \lVert g_n - g \rVert
    \qquad \text{for every } \omega \in \DE ,
  \]
  a bound uniform in $\omega$, so with
  $C = \sup_i \lVert h_i\rVert^k$,
  \[
    \Bigl| E^{P^n}\bigl[ (Y^\circ_t(X_n) - Y^\circ_s(X_n)) Z^\circ_s(X_n) \bigr] \Bigr|
      \;\leq\; 2C \bigl( \lVert f_n-f\rVert + t \lVert g_n-g\rVert \bigr)
      \;\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\; 0 ,
  \]
  which is \ref{it:C3c}.  \emph{This is the only place where
  \eqref{eq:approxA} is used, and it is used only as a uniform bound} --- which is
  why Theorem~\ref{thm:CPSconv} can replace it by the far weaker
  \eqref{eq:cps2}--\eqref{eq:cps3}.

  \emph{Conclusion.}  The paths of $X$ lie in $\DE$, so every $Y \in \XX$ is
  right continuous, and $\T = \Rp$ satisfies \eqref{T2b} with $D$ dense.
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} therefore gives $P \in \Msol(\XX)$, i.e.\ $X$ solves
  the martingale problem for $A$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Why this is a corollary and not a theorem]
  \label{rem:EKconvcor}
  \EK{} prove Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv} directly, and an earlier version of this
  manuscript reproduced that proof.  It is worth being explicit that nothing is
  lost by not doing so, because the two arguments are the same argument:
  \EK's ``let $D(X)$ be the continuity times, take limits there, extend by right
  continuity'' is precisely Steps~1 and~3 of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}, and their
  appeal to bounded convergence is its Steps~0 and~2 in the special case where
  \ref{it:C3b} holds because $f$ and $g$ are bounded.  Reproducing it would
  duplicate the abstract proof in a notation that hides which hypothesis does
  what.

  What the derivation makes visible is the division of labour that the direct
  proof conceals.  Of the four hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv}, weak
  convergence is \ref{it:C1}; $A \subset \Cb \times \Cb$ is what buys
  \ref{it:C3a}; boundedness of $f$ and $g$ is what buys \ref{it:C3b}; and the
  uniform approximation \eqref{eq:approxA} is what buys \ref{it:C3c} --- one
  hypothesis per condition, with no overlap.  Each can then be weakened
  separately, and Theorem~\ref{thm:CPSconv} weakens the last two while
  Section~\ref{ssec:weakstrong} weakens \ref{it:C3a}.
\end{remark}

\begin{corollary}[Approximation along the paths]
  \label{cor:EKconvpath}
  Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv} remains true if \eqref{eq:approxA} is replaced by the
  requirement that for each $(f,g) \in A$ there be $(f_n,g_n) \in A_n$ with
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:approxpath}
    \lim_{n \to \infty} E^{P^n}\bigl[ \lvert (f_n-f)(X_n(t)) \rvert \bigr] = 0
    \quad\text{and}\quad
    \lim_{n \to \infty} E^{P^n}\Bigl[ \int_0^t \lvert (g_n-g)(X_n(u)) \rvert \dif u \Bigr] = 0
  \end{equation}
  for every $t \geq 0$.  In particular the conclusion holds if
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item \label{it:ap_bdd} $\sup_{n \in \N} ( \lVert f_n \rVert + \lVert g_n \rVert ) < \infty$;
  \item \label{it:ap_ucc} $f_n \to f$ and $g_n \to g$ uniformly on compact subsets of $E$;
  \item \label{it:ap_cc} $\{X_n\}$ satisfies the compact containment condition \eqref{eq:ccn}.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
  Only \ref{it:C3c} used \eqref{eq:approxA} in the proof of
  Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv}; \ref{it:C3a} and \ref{it:C3b} concern $f$ and $g$ alone
  and are untouched.  Keeping the notation there, and writing
  $\Delta^n_r = Y^\circ_r - Y^{\circ,n}_r$, one has for $s \leq t$
  \[
    \Delta^n_t(\omega) - \Delta^n_s(\omega)
      \;=\; (f-f_n)(\omega(t)) - (f-f_n)(\omega(s))
        - \int_s^t (g-g_n)(\omega(u)) \dif u ,
  \]
  so with $C = \sup_i \lVert h_i \rVert^k \geq \lVert Z^\circ_s \rVert$ and
  $E^{P^n}[(Y^{\circ,n}_t(X_n) - Y^{\circ,n}_s(X_n)) Z^\circ_s(X_n)] = 0$ as before,
  \begin{align*}
    \Bigl| E^{P^n}\bigl[ (Y^\circ_t(X_n) - Y^\circ_s(X_n)) Z^\circ_s(X_n) \bigr] \Bigr|
      \;\leq\;\ & C \sum_{r \in \{s,t\}} E^{P^n}\bigl[ \lvert (f_n-f)(X_n(r)) \rvert \bigr] \\
      &{}+ C\, E^{P^n}\Bigl[ \int_0^t \lvert (g_n-g)(X_n(u)) \rvert \dif u \Bigr] ,
  \end{align*}
  which tends to $0$ by \eqref{eq:approxpath}.  Note that no uniform bound on
  $f_n$ or $g_n$ is needed: \ref{it:C3b} is bought by boundedness of $f$ and $g$,
  not of $f_n$ and $g_n$.

  For the sufficient condition, let $t > 0$ and $\eta > 0$, put
  $M = \sup_n ( \lVert f_n \rVert + \lVert g_n \rVert ) + \lVert f \rVert + \lVert g \rVert$,
  which is finite by \ref{it:ap_bdd} and $A \subset \Cb \times \Cb$, and use
  \ref{it:ap_cc} to choose a compact $\Gamma \subset E$ with
  $\inf_n P\{ X_n(u) \in \Gamma \text{ for all } u \leq t \} \geq 1 - \eta$.  On
  that event both integrands are at most $\sup_\Gamma |f_n - f|$ resp.\
  $\sup_\Gamma |g_n - g|$, and off it at most $M$; hence
  \[
    \limsup_{n \to \infty} E^{P^n}\bigl[ \lvert (f_n-f)(X_n(t)) \rvert \bigr] \leq M \eta ,
    \qquad
    \limsup_{n \to \infty} E^{P^n}\Bigl[ \int_0^t \lvert (g_n-g)(X_n(u)) \rvert \dif u \Bigr]
      \leq t M \eta ,
  \]
  using \ref{it:ap_ucc} for the terms on $\Gamma$.  Let $\eta \downarrow 0$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Three rungs of approximation]
  \label{rem:approxladder}
  Corollary~\ref{cor:EKconvpath} sits strictly between \EK{} and \CPS:
  \[
    \eqref{eq:approxA} \;\Longrightarrow\; \eqref{eq:approxpath}
      \;\Longrightarrow\; \eqref{eq:cps2}\text{--}\eqref{eq:cps3}
      \ \text{for } \xi^n = f_n(X^n), \ \varphi^n = g_n(X^n) ,
  \]
  the second implication because the test variables $\prod_i h_i(X^n_{t_i})$ are
  bounded and \eqref{eq:approxpath} controls absolute values.  The three rungs
  differ in what they are conditions \emph{on}:
  \eqref{eq:approxA} is a condition on $E$, \eqref{eq:approxpath} a condition on
  the occupation measures $\int_0^{\cdot} P\{X_n(u) \in \dif x\} \dif u$ and the
  one-dimensional marginals, and \eqref{eq:cps2}--\eqref{eq:cps3} a condition on
  the joint laws which moreover permits $\xi^n, \varphi^n$ that are not of the
  form $h(X^n)$ at all.  The middle rung is the one that costs nothing: its proof
  is the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv} with the estimate taken under $E$
  instead of over $\omega \in \DE$, and it is what one wants in practice, since
  approximating operators are typically obtained by localization and then
  converge only locally uniformly.  It is also free in situ: \ref{it:ap_cc} is
  the hypothesis one is assuming anyway in Remark~\ref{rem:EKrelcompact} in order
  to have convergence at all.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[\EK, Remark 4.5.2]
  \label{rem:EKrelcompact}
  Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv} presupposes convergence.  Suppose $(E,r)$ is Polish and
  $\dom(A)$ contains an algebra that separates points and vanishes nowhere; by
  Fact~\ref{fact:stoneweierstrass} such an algebra is separating, and it is dense
  in $\Cb(E)$ in the topology of uniform convergence on compact sets.  Then
  $\{X_n\}$ is relatively compact provided \eqref{eq:approxA} holds for each
  $(f,g) \in A$ and the compact containment condition \eqref{eq:ccn} holds.  This
  is Facts~\ref{fact:relcompact}, \ref{fact:relcompact2},
  \ref{fact:fddconv} and \ref{fact:prohorov}.  Combining with
  Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv}: every limit point of $\{X_n\}$ solves the martingale
  problem for $A$; in particular the martingale problem for $A$ has a solution
  with sample paths in $\DE$.  If in addition uniqueness holds (e.g.\ by
  Theorem~\ref{thm:uniqueness} or Corollary~\ref{cor:uniqviadual}), then
  $X_n \wto X$ for the unique solution $X$.
\end{remark}

\subsection{By convergence: the abstract theorem}
\label{ssec:absconv}

Theorem~\ref{thm:CPSconv} below is an instance of one
statement, which uses neither an operator, nor c\`adl\`ag paths, nor the
Skorokhod topology, nor any structure on the time index beyond a dense subset ---
and none at all if that subset is all of $\T$.  It is \CPS, Theorem~3.14 and
Corollary~3.17, and it is the cheapest genuine theorem in this manuscript; we
give it with its proof, since it is what the formalization should target
(step~\ref{it:F5a} of Section~\ref{sec:formalization}).  Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv}
is a special case of it directly, as its proof shows, and a fortiori of
Theorem~\ref{thm:CPSconv}; so all of Section~\ref{sec:convergence} rests on
Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}.

\begin{definition}[$P$-continuity at $X$; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E3}]
  \label{def:Pcont}
  A Borel map $\psi : F \to \R$ is \emph{$P$-continuous at $X$} if there is
  $C \in \Bor(F)$ with $P\{X \in C\} = 1$ such that $\psi(\alpha_m) \to \psi(\alpha)$
  whenever $\alpha_m \to \alpha$ in $F$ with $\alpha \in C$.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Abstract convergence; \CPS, Theorem 3.14 and Corollary 3.17; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E3}]
  \label{thm:absconv}
  In Setting~\ref{set:abstract}, let $\XX$ be canonical for $X$, let
  $D \subset \T$, and for each $n$ let $B^n = (\Omega^n,\Filt^n,\mathbb{F}^n,P^n)$
  be a filtered probability space carrying a process $X^n$ with paths in $F$.
  Assume:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(C\arabic*)]
  \item \label{it:C1} $X^n \wto X$ weakly on $F$;
  \item \label{it:C2} $\XX$ admits a determining set $\ZZ^\circ$
    (Definition~\ref{def:canonical});
  \item \label{it:C3} for every $Y \in \XX$ there is a canonical version
    $(Y^\circ_t)$ such that for all $t \in D$, $s \in D \cap \T_{\leq t}$ and
    $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$:
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
    \item \label{it:C3a} $Y^\circ_t$ and $Y^\circ_t Z^\circ_s$ are $P$-continuous
      at $X$;
    \item \label{it:C3b} $\{ Y^\circ_r(X^n) : r \in D \cap \T_{\leq t},\ n \in \N \}$
      is uniformly integrable;
    \item \label{it:C3c}
      $\displaystyle \lim_{n \to \infty}
        E^{P^n}\bigl[ \bigl( Y^\circ_t(X^n) - Y^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr) Z^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr]
        = 0$.
    \end{enumerate}
  \end{enumerate}
  Then $E^P[Y_t \mid \Filt_s] = Y_s$ for all $s \leq t$ in $D$ and all
  $Y \in \XX$.  If moreover $D = \T$, then $P \in \Msol(\XX)$.  The same
  conclusion holds for a proper $D \subsetneq \T$ provided $\T$ satisfies \eqref{T2b}
  with $D$ its countable dense subset, $D$ contains the greatest element of $\T$
  if there is one, and every $Y \in \XX$ is right continuous.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Fix $Y \in \XX$ with canonical version $Y^\circ$, and $t \in D$.

  \emph{Step 0: integrability.}  For $r \in D \cap \T_{\leq t}$ the map
  $|Y^\circ_r|$ is $P$-continuous at $X$ by \ref{it:C3a}, so
  $|Y^\circ_r(X^n)| \wto |Y^\circ_r(X)|$ by \ref{it:C1} and
  Fact~\ref{fact:cmt}; the family is uniformly integrable by \ref{it:C3b}, so
  Fact~\ref{fact:ui} gives
  $E^{P^n}|Y^\circ_r(X^n)| \to E^P|Y^\circ_r(X)| < \infty$.  In particular
  $Y_r = Y^\circ_r(X) \in L^1(P)$.

  \emph{Step 1: the martingale identity on $D$.}
  Let $s \in D \cap \T_{\leq t}$ and $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$, and put
  $\psi = (Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s) Z^\circ_s : F \to \R$.  Both $Y^\circ_t Z^\circ_s$
  and $Y^\circ_s Z^\circ_s$ are $P$-continuous at $X$ by \ref{it:C3a} --- the
  second by applying \ref{it:C3a} with $t$ replaced by $s$, legitimate since
  $s \in D$ and $s \in D \cap \T_{\leq s}$ --- and
  Definition~\ref{def:Pcont} is stable under differences, the exceptional sets
  being intersected; so $\psi$ is $P$-continuous at $X$.
  Hence $\psi(X^n) \wto \psi(X)$ by \ref{it:C1} and Fact~\ref{fact:cmt}.  The
  family $\{\psi(X^n)\}_n$ is uniformly integrable, being dominated by
  $\lVert Z^\circ_s \rVert ( |Y^\circ_t(X^n)| + |Y^\circ_s(X^n)| )$ and
  $Z^\circ_s$ bounded by Definition~\ref{def:canonical}(i).  So
  Fact~\ref{fact:ui} applies and, with \ref{it:C3c},
  \[
    E^P\bigl[ (Y_t - Y_s) Z^\circ_s(X) \bigr]
      \;=\; E^P\bigl[ \psi(X) \bigr]
      \;=\; \lim_{n \to \infty} E^{P^n}\bigl[ \psi(X^n) \bigr]
      \;=\; 0 ,
  \]
  the first equality because $\XX$ is canonical.  As $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$
  was arbitrary and $Y_s, Y_t \in L^1(P)$ by Step~0, part~(ii) of
  Definition~\ref{def:canonical} yields $E^P[Y_t \mid \Filt_s] = Y_s$.  If
  $D = \T$ this is already the assertion.

  \emph{Step 2: uniform integrability under $P$.}
  Let $\varphi_N(x) = |x| - |x| \wedge N$.  For $r \in D \cap \T_{\leq t}$ the map
  $\varphi_N \circ Y^\circ_r$ is $P$-continuous at $X$, and
  $\{\varphi_N(Y^\circ_r(X^n))\}_n$ is uniformly integrable because it is
  dominated by $|Y^\circ_r(X^n)|$.  So Fact~\ref{fact:ui} applies twice and gives
  \[
    E^P\bigl[ \varphi_N(Y_r) \bigr]
      \;=\; \lim_{n \to \infty} E^{P^n}\bigl[ \varphi_N(Y^\circ_r(X^n)) \bigr]
      \;\leq\; \sup_{n \in \N} E^{P^n}\bigl[ \varphi_N(Y^\circ_r(X^n)) \bigr] .
  \]
  Taking the supremum over $r \in D \cap \T_{\leq t}$ and letting $N \to \infty$,
  the right-hand side tends to $0$ by \ref{it:C3b} and the second
  characterization of uniform integrability in Fact~\ref{fact:ui}.  Hence
  $\{ Y_r : r \in D \cap \T_{\leq t} \}$ is uniformly integrable under $P$.

  \emph{Step 3: from $D$ to $\T$.}
  Assume \eqref{T2b} and $Y$ right continuous, and let $s < t$ in $\T$ be arbitrary; the
  case $s = t$ is trivial.  Choose $t_m$ as follows: if $t$ is the greatest
  element of $\T$, put $t_m = t$, which lies in $D$ by hypothesis; otherwise
  \eqref{T2b} provides $t_m \downarrow t$ in $D$ with $t_m > t$.  Since $s < t$, the same
  argument gives $s_m \downarrow s$ in $D$ with $s \leq s_m < t_m$ for all large
  $m$.  Let $G \in \Filt_s$.  Then $G \in \Filt_{s_m}$ because $s \leq s_m$, so
  Step~1 gives $E^P[Y_{t_m} \one_G] = E^P[Y_{s_m} \one_G]$.  By right continuity
  $Y_{t_m} \to Y_t$ and $Y_{s_m} \to Y_s$ $P$-a.s.  All the indices involved lie
  in $D \cap \T_{\leq t_1}$, so both families are uniformly integrable by Step~2
  applied at $t_1$; Fact~\ref{fact:ui} applied to $Y_{t_m}\one_G$ and
  $Y_{s_m}\one_G$ yields $E^P[Y_t \one_G] = E^P[Y_s \one_G]$.  As
  $G \in \Filt_s$ was arbitrary, $E^P[Y_t \mid \Filt_s] = Y_s$, and
  $P \in \Msol(\XX)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[\ref{it:C1} and \ref{it:C3a} are one hypothesis, and it is not topological]
  \label{rem:absconvtopfree}
  The proof invoked \ref{it:C1} and \ref{it:C3a} only through
  Fact~\ref{fact:cmt}, and only in order to know that certain \emph{real} random
  variables converge in distribution.  Collecting them: for $t \in D$,
  $s, r \in D \cap \T_{\leq t}$ and $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$, what Steps~0--2
  used is
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:C1prime}
    Y^\circ_r(X^n) \wto Y^\circ_r(X)
    \qquad\text{and}\qquad
    \bigl( Y^\circ_t(X^n) - Y^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr) Z^\circ_s(X^n)
      \;\wto\; \bigl( Y^\circ_t(X) - Y^\circ_s(X) \bigr) Z^\circ_s(X) ,
  \end{equation}
  and nothing else --- Step~2 needs only $\varphi_N \circ Y^\circ_r$, a bounded
  continuous function of the first.  Replacing \ref{it:C1} and \ref{it:C3a} by
  \eqref{eq:C1prime} therefore leaves the theorem and its proof standing verbatim.

  In that form the path space $F$ carries no topology at all: it is the
  measurable path space of Setting~\ref{set:abstract} and nothing more is asked
  of it.  This is worth isolating for two reasons.  First, it locates the only
  topological hypothesis in Section~\ref{sec:convergence}: \ref{it:C1} presupposes
  a topology on $F$ and Fact~\ref{fact:cmt} presupposes it separable and metric,
  whereas \eqref{eq:C1prime} presupposes neither, so a formalization can carry
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} on a bare measurable space and pay for a topology
  only where one verifies \eqref{eq:C1prime}.  Second, \eqref{eq:C1prime} is what
  one is reduced to verifying whenever the topology on $F$ is too weak for the
  coordinates $\pi_t$ to be continuous --- which is the situation of
  Section~\ref{ssec:weakstrong}, where they fail at the atoms of the clock, and
  the situation of Section~\ref{ssec:measureconv}, where they fail everywhere.
\end{remark}


\begin{remark}[Where the index enters]
  \label{rem:absconvindex}
  Steps~0--2 use no structure on $\T$ whatsoever beyond the preorder: $D$ is any
  subset, and $\T_{\leq t}$ is a down-set.  The only place where more is needed is
  Step~3, and it is needed only to pass from $D$ to its complement.  With
  $D = \T$ --- in particular for $\T = \N_0$, where every point is isolated from
  the right and no approximation is available or necessary --- Step~3 is empty and
  the theorem holds under \eqref{T0}.  This is why step~\ref{it:F5a} of
  Section~\ref{sec:formalization} can be done immediately after \ref{it:F2},
  before any of the Skorokhod theory.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[What has to be checked, and what does not]
  \label{rem:absconvcheck}
  Of the three hypotheses, \ref{it:C1} is the input, \ref{it:C2} is a property of
  the path space and is supplied once and for all by
  Example~\ref{ex:determining} or by Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar}, and
  \ref{it:C3} is the work.  Within \ref{it:C3}, \ref{it:C3a} is where the topology
  of $F$ enters --- and it is the only place, which is what
  Lemma~\ref{lem:contuse} makes precise and Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug}
  exploits; for $F = \DE$ it is the reason for the ``countable complement''
  hypothesis of Theorem~\ref{thm:CPSconv}, since
  $\alpha \mapsto \alpha(t)$ is $J_1$-continuous exactly at paths not jumping at
  $t$.  Condition \ref{it:C3c} is where the approximating martingales are used,
  and \ref{it:C3b} is what makes the two limits interchangeable.
\end{remark}

\subsection{By convergence: the general version}
\label{ssec:CPSconv}

The following theorem is \CPS, Theorem~4.1; it is the specialization of the
abstract convergence theorem \CPS, Corollary~3.17, to the Markovian martingale
problem, and it generalizes Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv} --- which
Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} has already delivered --- in three independent
directions:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
\item the approximating objects are \emph{not} required to solve a martingale
  problem for an operator; they are arbitrary pairs $(\xi^n,\varphi^n)$ of
  progressively measurable processes such that
  $\xi^n - \int_0^\cdot \varphi^n_s \dif s$ is a martingale;
\item the approximation \eqref{eq:approxA} in the uniform norm is replaced by
  the much weaker \emph{asymptotic} conditions \eqref{eq:cps2} and
  \eqref{eq:cps3}, which are conditions on the laws only;
\item uniform boundedness is replaced by uniform integrability
  \eqref{eq:cps1}.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{theorem}[\CPS, Theorem 4.1; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{thm:CPSconv}
  Assume \eqref{E3}.  Let $B = (\Omega,\Filt,\mathbb{F},P)$ and
  $B^n = (\Omega^n,\Filt^n,\mathbb{F}^n,P^n)$ be filtered probability spaces
  carrying $E$-valued c\`adl\`ag adapted processes $X$ and $X^n$, and assume that
  $\mathbb{F}$ is generated by $X$.  Let $A \subset \Cb(E) \times \Cb(E)$ and let
  $\XX = \XX_A(X)$ be as in \eqref{eq:XXA}.  For each $n$, let $\XX^n$ be a set
  of pairs $(\xi,\varphi)$ of $\K$-valued progressively measurable processes on
  $B^n$ such that $\sup_{s \leq T} E^{P^n}[|\xi_s| + |\varphi_s|] < \infty$ for
  all $T>0$ and such that $\xi - \int_0^{\cdot} \varphi_s \dif s$ is a martingale
  on $B^n$.

  Suppose that $X^n \wto X$ weakly on $\DE$ with the Skorokhod $J_1$ topology,
  and that there is a set $\Gamma \subset \Rp$ with countable complement such
  that for each $(f,g) \in A$ and $T > 0$ there is a sequence
  $(\xi^n, \varphi^n) \in \XX^n$ with
  \begin{align}
    \label{eq:cps1}
    \sup_{n \in \N} \sup_{s \leq T} E^{P^n}\bigl[ |\xi^n_s| + |\varphi^n_s| \bigr]
      &< \infty , \\
    \label{eq:cps2}
    \lim_{n \to \infty}
      E^{P^n}\Bigl[ \bigl( \xi^n_t - f(X^n_t) \bigr)
        \prod_{i=1}^k h_i(X^n_{t_i}) \Bigr] &= 0 , \\
    \label{eq:cps3}
    \lim_{n \to \infty}
      E^{P^n}\Bigl[ \int_s^t \bigl( \varphi^n_u - g(X^n_u) \bigr) \dif u
        \prod_{i=1}^k h_i(X^n_{t_i}) \Bigr] &= 0 ,
  \end{align}
  for all $k \in \N$, $t_1,\dots,t_k \in \Gamma \cap [0,t]$,
  $t \in \Gamma \cap [0,T]$, $h_1,\dots,h_k \in \Cb(E)$.
  Then $P \in \Msol(\XX)$, i.e.\ $X$ solves the martingale problem for $A$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  We verify \ref{it:C1}--\ref{it:C3} of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}.

  \emph{The set $D$, and \ref{it:C2}.}
  Set $D = \{ t \in \Gamma : P\{X_t \neq X_{t-}\} = 0 \}$.  By
  Fact~\ref{fact:Dcountable} and the hypothesis on $\Gamma$, $D^c$ is countable,
  so $D$ is dense.  Let $\ZZ^\circ$ be the determining set of
  Example~\ref{ex:determining} built from $D$; this is \ref{it:C2}.
  Hypothesis \ref{it:C1} is the assumed weak convergence $X^n \wto X$ on $\DE$.

  \emph{\ref{it:C3b}.}
  Let $Y = f(X) - \int_0^\cdot g(X_s)\dif s \in \XX$ with canonical version
  $Y^\circ_t(\omega) = f(\omega(t)) - \int_0^t g(\omega(s))\dif s$.  Since $f,g$
  are bounded, $Y^\circ_r$ is bounded on $r \in [0,t]$, so
  $\{Y^\circ_r(X^n) : r \in D \cap [0,t],\, n \in \N\}$ is uniformly integrable.

  \emph{\ref{it:C3a}.}
  The evaluation $\omega \mapsto \omega(t)$ is $J_1$-continuous at every $\omega$
  with $\omega(t) = \omega(t-)$, so the definition of $D$ makes $Y^\circ_t$ and
  $Y^\circ_t Z^\circ_s$ $P$-continuous at $X$ in the sense of
  Definition~\ref{def:Pcont}, for $s,t \in D$.  This is the only point at which
  the Skorokhod topology is used, and it is what the hypothesis on $\Gamma$ is
  for.

  \emph{\ref{it:C3c}.}
  It remains to verify, for $s,t \in D$ with $s \leq t$ and
  $Z^\circ_s = \prod_i h_i(\cdot(t_i))$,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:cpskey}
    \lim_{n \to \infty}
      E^{P^n}\bigl[ \bigl( Y^\circ_t(X^n) - Y^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr) Z^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr]
      \;=\; 0 .
  \end{equation}
  Put $Y^n = \xi^n - \int_0^{\cdot} \varphi^n_u \dif u$, a martingale on $B^n$.
  Because $Z^\circ_s(X^n)$ is $\Filt^n_s$-measurable,
  $E^{P^n}[(Y^n_t - Y^n_s) Z^\circ_s(X^n)] = 0$, so
  \[
    E^{P^n}\bigl[ ( Y^\circ_t(X^n) - Y^\circ_s(X^n) ) Z^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr]
    = E^{P^n}\bigl[ ( Y^\circ_t(X^n) - Y^n_t + Y^n_s - Y^\circ_s(X^n) )
      Z^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr] ,
  \]
  and the right-hand side tends to $0$ by \eqref{eq:cps2} and \eqref{eq:cps3},
  since
  $Y^\circ_t(X^n) - Y^n_t = (f(X^n_t) - \xi^n_t)
    + \int_0^t (\varphi^n_u - g(X^n_u)) \dif u$.
  Thus \eqref{eq:cpskey} holds, which is \ref{it:C3c}.

  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} now gives $E^P[Y_t \mid \Filt_s] = Y_s$ for
  $s \leq t$ in $D$, and its Step~3 --- which uses the uniform integrability
  established in its Step~2 from \ref{it:C3b}, together with the right continuity
  of $Y$ --- extends this to arbitrary $s < t$.  Hypothesis \eqref{eq:cps1} is
  what makes all the expectations in \eqref{eq:cps2} and \eqref{eq:cps3} finite,
  uniformly in $n$; it is not what supplies \ref{it:C3b}, which here comes for
  free from the boundedness of $f$ and $g$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:convlocal}
  Theorem~\ref{thm:CPSconv} concludes that $P$ solves the \emph{global}
  martingale problem.  To obtain a solution of a \emph{local} martingale problem
  in the limit, apply the theorem to a localized family of test processes, i.e.\
  to $\XX^{(m)} = \{ Y^{\tau_m} : Y \in \XX \}$ for a suitable exhausting
  sequence of stopping times $\tau_m$, and let $m \to \infty$.  This is how
  \CPS{} obtain their semimartingale results (their Section 4.3), and it is the
  same reduction as in Remark~\ref{rem:uniquelocal}.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Path spaces other than $\DE$]
  \label{rem:CPSabstract}
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} is stated for an arbitrary Polish path space
  $F \subset E^{\T}$, and Theorem~\ref{thm:CPSconv} is the instance $F = \DE$.
  Nothing in the abstract theorem refers to c\`adl\`ag paths, to a Markov
  structure, or to the Skorokhod topology; $F$ may for instance be
  $L^p_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Rp,\R^d)$, which is the state space \CPS{} use for
  solutions of Volterra equations (their Example 3.13), or a product of such
  spaces.  What changes from one $F$ to another is only which maps are
  $P$-continuous at $X$, i.e.\ hypothesis \ref{it:C3a} --- see
  Remark~\ref{rem:absconvcheck}.  \CPS{} also give a version in which the
  uniform integrability of \ref{it:C3b} is dropped (their Corollary 3.21) and one
  with control variables and weak-strong convergence (their Theorem 3.14).  The
  part of the latter that concerns different clocks is
  Section~\ref{ssec:diffclocks}, the part that concerns weak-strong convergence is
  Section~\ref{ssec:weakstrong}.  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug} is a third
  instance of the same freedom in $F$ --- there the enlargement is by finitely or
  countably many coordinates rather than by a change of topology --- and it is the
  one that does the work here.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Approximation on different clocks}
\label{ssec:diffclocks}

Nothing so far requires the approximating processes to run on the same clock as
the limit, and this subsection makes that precise.  The observation to begin with
is negative and structural: \emph{Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} does not mention the
approximating martingale problems at all}.  Its hypotheses \ref{it:C1}--\ref{it:C3}
speak only of the canonical version $Y^\circ$ of the \emph{limit} test process,
evaluated along $X^n$.  Where the approximating martingales come from, on which
clock they are compensated, and with which convention, is not part of the theorem
--- it is part of the verification of \ref{it:C3c}.  So different clocks are not a
difficulty for the theorem; they are a difficulty for one of its hypotheses, and
the following isolates it.

\begin{setting}[Approximants with their own clocks; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E3}]
  \label{set:diffclocks}
  In the situation of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}, let the limit test process be
  \[
    Y^\circ_t \;=\; f(\pi_t) - \int_{\langle 0,t \rangle_\iota} g(u,\pi_u) \, q(\dif u) ,
  \]
  and for each $n$ let $q^n$ be a clock on $\T$, $\iota_n$ a convention, and
  $\xi^n, \psi^n$ progressively measurable processes on $B^n$ such that
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:approxclock}
    Y^n_t \;=\; \xi^n_t - \int_{\langle 0,t \rangle_{\iota_n}} \psi^n_u \, q^n(\dif u)
  \end{equation}
  is a $P^n$-martingale.  Write, for $s \leq t$,
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:clockdiscrepancy}
    \Delta^n_{s,t} \;\coloneqq\;
      \int_{\langle s,t \rangle_{\iota_n}} \psi^n_u \, q^n(\dif u)
      - \int_{\langle s,t \rangle_{\iota}} \psi^n_u \, q(\dif u)
  \end{equation}
  for the \emph{clock discrepancy} along the integrands.
\end{setting}

\begin{theorem}[Clock change; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E3}]
  \label{thm:clockchange}
  Assume \ref{it:C1}, \ref{it:C2}, \ref{it:C3a} and \ref{it:C3b} of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}, and suppose that for all $s \leq t$ in $D$ and all
  $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(K\arabic*)]
  \item \label{it:K1}
    $\displaystyle \sup_{n} \sup_{r \leq T}
      E^{P^n}\bigl[ |\xi^n_r| + |\psi^n_r| \bigr] < \infty$ for every $T$;
  \item \label{it:K2}
    $\displaystyle \lim_n E^{P^n}\bigl[ (\xi^n_t - f(X^n_t)) \, Z^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr] = 0$;
  \item \label{it:K3}
    $\displaystyle \lim_n E^{P^n}\Bigl[
      \int_{\langle s,t \rangle_\iota} \bigl( \psi^n_u - g(u,X^n_u) \bigr) q(\dif u)
      \; Z^\circ_s(X^n) \Bigr] = 0$;
  \item \label{it:K4}
    $\displaystyle \lim_n E^{P^n}\bigl[ \Delta^n_{s,t} \, Z^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr] = 0$.
  \end{enumerate}
  Then \ref{it:C3c} holds, and consequently $P \in \Msol(\XX)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Fix $s \leq t$ in $D$ and $Z^\circ_s \in \ZZ^\circ_s$.  Since $Y^n$ is a
  $P^n$-martingale and $Z^\circ_s(X^n)$ is $\Filt^n_s$-measurable,
  $E^{P^n}[(Y^n_t - Y^n_s) Z^\circ_s(X^n)] = 0$, so
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:clockchangesplit}
    E^{P^n}\bigl[ (Y^\circ_t(X^n) - Y^\circ_s(X^n)) Z^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr]
      = E^{P^n}\bigl[ \bigl( R^n_t - R^n_s \bigr) Z^\circ_s(X^n) \bigr] ,
    \qquad R^n_r \coloneqq Y^\circ_r(X^n) - Y^n_r .
  \end{equation}
  By \eqref{eq:approxclock} and the definition of $Y^\circ$,
  \[
    R^n_r = \bigl( f(X^n_r) - \xi^n_r \bigr)
      + \int_{\langle 0,r \rangle_{\iota_n}} \psi^n_u \, q^n(\dif u)
      - \int_{\langle 0,r \rangle_{\iota}} g(u,X^n_u) \, q(\dif u) ,
  \]
  Inserting $\pm \int_{\langle 0,r \rangle_\iota} \psi^n_u \, q(\dif u)$ splits the
  last two terms into $\Delta^n_{0,r}$ and
  \[
