    \int_{\langle 0,r \rangle_\iota} \bigl( \psi^n_u - g(u,X^n_u) \bigr) q(\dif u) .
  \]
  Taking the difference at $r=t$ and $r=s$, and using the additivity
  \eqref{eq:clockadd} of both interval families,
  \[
    R^n_t - R^n_s = \bigl( f(X^n_t) - \xi^n_t \bigr) - \bigl( f(X^n_s) - \xi^n_s \bigr)
      + \Delta^n_{s,t}
      + \int_{\langle s,t \rangle_\iota} \bigl( \psi^n_u - g(u,X^n_u) \bigr) q(\dif u) .
  \]
  Each of the four terms has vanishing limit against $Z^\circ_s(X^n)$, by
  \ref{it:K2} twice, \ref{it:K4} and \ref{it:K3}; \ref{it:K1} makes all the
  expectations finite.  So the left-hand side of \eqref{eq:clockchangesplit}
  tends to $0$, which is \ref{it:C3c}, and Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} applies.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:K4isnotweak}
  Condition \ref{it:K4} is \emph{not} weak convergence $q^n \Rightarrow q$.  It is
  convergence of the clocks \emph{paired with the integrands}, and it does not
  follow from $q^n \Rightarrow q$ unless the family $\{\psi^n\}$ is equicontinuous
  in a suitable sense --- which for the piecewise constant $\psi^n$ of
  Example~\ref{ex:invariance} it is not.  This is why \CPS, Assumption~5.3 states
  it as a hypothesis, their condition (5.10), rather than deriving it; and
  Theorem~\ref{thm:clockchange} is their Theorem~5.4, stripped of the control
  variables.  Note also that \ref{it:K4} absorbs the difference between $\iota_n$
  and $\iota$: the two conventions for one clock differ by a boundary term, and
  \eqref{eq:clockdiscrepancy} does not care which of the two discrepancies it is
  measuring.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Rescaled Markov chains; the invariance principle]
  \label{ex:invariance}
  Let $E = \R^d$, $F = \DE$, $q = \lambda$ for the limit, and for the $n$-th
  approximant
  \[
    q^n = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{k \geq 1} \delta_{k/n} , \qquad \iota_n = \mathrm{o} .
  \]
  Let $(\Xi^n_k)_{k \geq 0}$ be a Markov chain with one-step kernel $P_n$ and put
  $X^n_t = \Xi^n_{\lfloor nt \rfloor}$, a piecewise constant element of $\DE$.
  With $\xi^n = f(X^n)$ and the \emph{predictable} integrand
  $\psi^n_u = n\,(P_n f - f)(X^n_{u-})$ we get, since
  $X^n_{k/n-} = \Xi^n_{k-1}$,
  \[
    \int_{(0,t]} \psi^n_u \, q^n(\dif u)
      = \frac{1}{n} \sum_{1 \leq k \leq nt} \psi^n_{k/n}
      = \sum_{j < \lfloor nt \rfloor} (P_n f - f)(\Xi^n_j) ,
  \]
  so that \eqref{eq:approxclock} reads
  $Y^n_t = f(\Xi^n_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}) - \sum_{j<\lfloor nt\rfloor}(P_nf-f)(\Xi^n_j)$.
  This is the Doob decomposition of $f(\Xi^n)$ read along the embedded grid, it is
  constant on each $[k/n,(k+1)/n)$, and it is a genuine
  $(\Filt^{X^n}_t)_{t \geq 0}$-martingale.

  Then \ref{it:K2} holds trivially, $\xi^n - f(X^n)$ being identically zero;
  \ref{it:K3} is the classical requirement that the rescaled generators
  $n(P_nf - f)$ converge to $Af$ --- the discrepancy between $X^n_{u-}$ and
  $X^n_u$ is immaterial there, the two differing only on the Lebesgue null set
  $\frac1n\N$; and \ref{it:K4} holds because $\psi^n$ is constant on each interval
  $(\tfrac{k-1}{n},\tfrac{k}{n}]$, on which the atomic and the Lebesgue integral
  give the same value $(P_nf-f)(\Xi^n_{k-1})$, so that the two integrals agree
  except on the two boundary intervals and
  $|\Delta^n_{s,t}| \leq \tfrac{2}{n} \sup_u |\psi^n_u| = 2\lVert P_nf - f\rVert$,
  which tends to $0$ as soon as $n(P_nf-f)$ is bounded.

  So ``a rescaled Markov chain converges to a diffusion'' is, in this formalism,
  a statement about clocks: $q^n \to q$ along the integrands, with the chain's
  clock atomic and the limit's atomless.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}[Why the convention flips on embedding]
  \label{rem:embedflip}
  Remark~\ref{rem:jumpdiscrete} says that on $\T = \N_0$ with counting measure the
  Doob decomposition is the \emph{predictable} convention $\iota = \mathrm{p}$,
  and Example~\ref{ex:invariance} uses $\iota_n = \mathrm{o}$.  There is no
  contradiction, and the point is worth isolating because it is easy to get wrong
  --- an earlier version of this manuscript did.

  On $\T = \N_0$ there is nothing between two time points, and
  $f(\Xi_m) - \sum_{k<m}(P_nf-f)(\Xi_k)$ is a martingale for the only filtration
  there is.  Embed the grid into $\Rp$, however, and the same formula with
  $\iota_n = \mathrm{p}$ and $\psi^n_u = n(P_nf-f)(X^n_u)$ gives
  \[
    \int_{[0,t)} \psi^n_u \, q^n(\dif u) \;=\; \sum_{k < nt} (P_nf-f)(\Xi^n_k) ,
  \]
  whose value for $t \in (\tfrac{m}{n},\tfrac{m+1}{n})$ is
  $\sum_{k \leq m}(P_nf-f)(\Xi^n_k)$, one summand \emph{more} than at $t = m/n$.
  So $Y^n$ drops by $(P_nf-f)(\Xi^n_m)$ immediately after $m/n$ while
  $\Filt^{X^n}$ does not change, and $Y^n$ is a martingale along the grid but
  \emph{not} in continuous time.  The mismatch is exactly the one
  Section~\ref{sec:cadlag} warns about: with $\iota = \mathrm{p}$ an atom of the
  clock at $u$ is compensated \emph{after} $u$, whereas $f(X^n)$ jumps \emph{at}
  $u$.

  The repair is the one the semimartingale literature makes by reflex: take
  $\iota_n = \mathrm{o}$ and evaluate the integrand at $u-$.  Then atom and jump
  occur at the same instant and cancel.  This is the sharpest argument in the
  manuscript for carrying both conventions: the same object needs $\mathrm{p}$
  intrinsically and $\mathrm{o}$ after embedding, and a manuscript committed to
  one of them could not state Example~\ref{ex:invariance} at all.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[What this example costs, and what paid for it]
  \label{rem:invariancepay}
  Three earlier decisions are needed to write Example~\ref{ex:invariance} down at
  all, and none of them was made with it in view.
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \emph{Atoms} (D7 of the progress log, Theorem~\ref{thm:absreg}).  Without
    a clock that may have atoms, the chain's martingale problem is not a
    martingale problem; one would have to interpolate the chain into continuous
    time, which is precisely the manoeuvre the clock makes unnecessary.
  \item \emph{Clocks as measures on $\T$} (Definition~\ref{def:clock}).  Different
    clocks on one $\T$ subsume different time indices, as long as the latter
    embed: $\frac1n\N_0 \subset \Rp$ carries $q^n$, and no separate index set is
    needed.
  \item \emph{Both conventions} (Remark~\ref{rem:convention}).  The chain needs
    $\iota = \mathrm{p}$ intrinsically and $\iota = \mathrm{o}$ once its grid is
    embedded in $\Rp$ (Remark~\ref{rem:embedflip}); the limit does not care.  Had
    one convention been fixed, Example~\ref{ex:invariance} could not be stated as
    it is.  That the two differ by
    $\psi^n_t \, q^n(\{t\}) = (P_nf-f)(X^n_{t-}) = O(1/n)$, and so
    \emph{wash out in the limit}, is what makes the flip harmless; it is a
    retrospective argument for carrying both rather than choosing.
  \end{enumerate}
  What is \emph{not} supplied here is tightness: Theorem~\ref{thm:clockchange}
  assumes \ref{it:C1}, and establishing $X^n \wto X$ on $\DE$ is the Skorokhod
  half of the theory, cited in Remark~\ref{rem:EKrelcompact} and not proved.
\end{remark}

% =====================================================================
\subsection{Relaxing the continuity hypothesis}
\label{ssec:weakstrong}

Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} has two hypotheses that can fail in practice, and this
manuscript now has a section for each.  Section~\ref{ssec:diffclocks} relaxes
\ref{it:C3c}, the approximation condition.  This one relaxes \ref{it:C3a}, the
continuity condition --- and it has to, because \ref{it:C3a} fails outright in
precisely the situation that the clock of Definition~\ref{def:clock} was widened
to admit.

There are two repairs, and they are not of the same weight.  The first, given
here in full, enlarges the path space by the values at the atoms of the clock and
needs nothing beyond the continuous mapping theorem; it settles every case that
arises in this manuscript.  The second is the weak-strong convergence of \CPS,
which is what the literature states and what is needed when the times at which
continuity fails are \emph{random}.  Remark~\ref{rem:augvsws} sets the two side
by side.

\begin{example}[Why \ref{it:C3a} is not a technicality; \CPS, Section 4.3]
  \label{ex:atomicdiscontinuity}
  Take $\T = \Rp$, $E = \R$, $F = D_\R[0,\infty)$, $g(x) = x$ and the atomic clock
  $q = \delta_1$.  The compensator of Definition~\ref{def:markovMP} is then the
  functional
  \[
    F_t(\omega) \;=\; \int_{\langle 0,t \rangle_{\iota}} \omega(s) \, q(\dif s)
      \;=\; \omega(1) \, \one_{\{t > 1\}}
      \quad (\iota = \mathrm{p}) ,
      \qquad
      \omega(1) \, \one_{\{t \geq 1\}} \quad (\iota = \mathrm{o}) ,
  \]
  since $[0,t)$ contains the atom $1$ exactly when $t > 1$, and $(0,t]$ exactly
  when $t \geq 1$.  Either way $F_t$ is, for every $t$ beyond the atom, the
  evaluation $\omega \mapsto \omega(1)$, and \emph{that} map is $J_1$-discontinuous
  at every path jumping at $1$: with
  \[
    \omega_n = \one_{[1+1/n,\infty)} \;\longrightarrow\; \omega = \one_{[1,\infty)}
    \quad\text{in } J_1 ,
  \]
  one has $F_t(\omega_n) = \omega_n(1) = 0 \not\to 1 = \omega(1) = F_t(\omega)$.
  For $t$ before the atom $F_t \equiv 0$ is continuous, so the times at which
  $F_t$ is continuous at $\omega$ form the bounded set $\T_{\leq 1}$.

  Consequently, if the limit law $P$ charges paths jumping at the atom ---
  $P\{X_1 \neq X_{1-}\} > 0$, which is the generic situation when the clock has an
  atom at $1$ --- then hypothesis \ref{it:C3a} of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} fails
  for \emph{every} $t > 1$, a set of times of full measure.  This is a different
  failure from the one Theorem~\ref{thm:CPSconv} handles: there the bad times form
  a countable set, namely the fixed discontinuities of $X$ itself, and a dense
  $\Gamma$ avoids them; here the bad \emph{index} $t$ ranges over a half-line
  because the discontinuity of the functional sits at the atom of the clock and
  not at $t$.  No choice of $\Gamma$ helps.  The cause is exactly the atom of $q$,
  and hence exactly the generality that Remark~\ref{rem:convention} and
  Section~\ref{ssec:jump} insist on.  Processes with fixed times of discontinuity
  are not a curiosity here; they are the case this manuscript admits and \EK{} do
  not.
\end{example}

\subsubsection*{An elementary repair: enlarge the path space by the atoms}

Before importing machinery it is worth asking how much of it the failure in
Example~\ref{ex:atomicdiscontinuity} actually calls for.  The answer is: none.
The discontinuity there sits at a \emph{deterministic} place --- an atom of the
clock --- and a functional that is discontinuous only because it evaluates the
path at finitely or countably many prescribed times becomes continuous as soon as
those values are carried along as separate coordinates.  That is the whole idea,
and it costs one lemma.

\begin{lemma}[Where continuity is used in Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}]
  \label{lem:contuse}
  In the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}, hypotheses \ref{it:C1} and
  \ref{it:C3a} are used only in the following way: to conclude, from a Borel
  $\psi : F \to \R$ which is $P$-continuous at $X$, that
  $\psi(X^n) \wto \psi(X)$.  They are used for
  $\psi \in \{ |Y^\circ_r| ,\ \varphi_N \circ Y^\circ_r ,\
  (Y^\circ_t - Y^\circ_s)Z^\circ_s \}$ and for nothing else.  In particular
  neither hypothesis enters the filtration, the determining set, or the
  conclusion, all of which are statements about $F$ and $P$ alone.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
  Inspection.  Step~0 applies Fact~\ref{fact:cmt} to $|Y^\circ_r|$ and then
  Fact~\ref{fact:ui}; Step~1 does the same for
  $(Y^\circ_t-Y^\circ_s)Z^\circ_s$; Step~2 for $\varphi_N \circ Y^\circ_r$.
  Step~3 uses right continuity and Step~2, and no continuity on $F$ at all.  The
  conclusion $E^P[Y_t \mid \Filt_s] = Y_s$ and the hypothesis \ref{it:C2} refer
  only to $F$.
\end{proof}

Lemma~\ref{lem:contuse} says that the continuity may be demanded on \emph{any}
space through which the relevant functionals factor, provided the convergence
\ref{it:C1} is available there.  So we enlarge.

\begin{theorem}[Abstract convergence, augmented form; \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E3}]
  \label{thm:absconvaug}
  In the situation of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}, let $G$ be Polish, let
  $\gamma : F \to G$ be Borel, and put
  \[
    \hat{F} = F \times G , \qquad
    \hat\gamma : F \to \hat{F}, \ \ \omega \mapsto (\omega,\gamma(\omega)) ,
    \qquad \hat{X}^n = \hat\gamma(X^n) , \quad \hat{X} = \hat\gamma(X) .
  \]
  Replace \ref{it:C1} and \ref{it:C3a} by
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(C\arabic*$'$),start=1]
  \item \label{it:C1aug}
    $\hat{X}^n \wto \hat{X}$ weakly on $\hat{F}$;
  \item[\textup{(C3a$'$)}] \label{it:C3aaug}
    there are Borel $\hat{Y}^\circ_t : \hat{F} \to \R$ with
    $\hat{Y}^\circ_t \circ \hat\gamma = Y^\circ_t$ such that $\hat{Y}^\circ_t$ and
    $\hat{Y}^\circ_t \, (Z^\circ_s \circ \mathrm{pr}_F)$ are $\hat{P}$-continuous
    at $\hat{X}$, where $\hat{P} = P \circ \hat{X}^{-1}$.
  \end{enumerate}
  Keep \ref{it:C2}, \ref{it:C3b} and \ref{it:C3c} unchanged.  Then the conclusion
  of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} holds verbatim.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  By Lemma~\ref{lem:contuse} it suffices to produce
  $\psi(X^n) \wto \psi(X)$ for the three functionals listed there.  Each factors
  through $\hat\gamma$: $\psi = \hat\psi \circ \hat\gamma$ with
  $\hat\psi \in \{ |\hat{Y}^\circ_r| ,\ \varphi_N \circ \hat{Y}^\circ_r ,\
  (\hat{Y}^\circ_t - \hat{Y}^\circ_s)(Z^\circ_s \circ \mathrm{pr}_F) \}$, by the
  defining property $\hat{Y}^\circ_t \circ \hat\gamma = Y^\circ_t$ and because
  $\mathrm{pr}_F \circ \hat\gamma = \mathrm{id}_F$.  Each $\hat\psi$ is
  $\hat{P}$-continuous at $\hat{X}$ --- for the first and second because
  $x \mapsto |x|$ and $\varphi_N$ are continuous, for the third by
  \textup{(C3a$'$)} and stability of Definition~\ref{def:Pcont} under
  differences.  So $\hat\psi(\hat{X}^n) \wto \hat\psi(\hat{X})$ by
  \ref{it:C1aug} and Fact~\ref{fact:cmt}, and
  $\hat\psi(\hat{X}^n) = \psi(X^n)$, $\hat\psi(\hat{X}) = \psi(X)$.  Every other
  step of the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} is untouched.
\end{proof}

Note that \ref{it:C1aug} implies \ref{it:C1}, by projection; the augmented
statement is a genuine strengthening of the input and a genuine weakening of the
work.  The point is what $G$ has to be for an atomic clock.

\begin{proposition}[Atomic clocks need only the values at the atoms; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E2}]
  \label{prop:atomaug}
  Let $F = \DE$, let $q = q_{\mathrm{a}} + q_{\mathrm{c}}$ with
  $q_{\mathrm{a}} = \sum_{a \in A} m_a \delta_a$ for a countable $A \subset \T$ and
  $q_{\mathrm{c}}$ atomless, and let
  \[
    Y^\circ_t(\omega) \;=\; f(\omega(t))
      - \int_{\langle 0,t\rangle_\iota} g(u,\omega(u)) \, q(\dif u)
  \]
  with $f \in \Cb(E)$, $g$ bounded, $g(u,\cdot) \in \Cb(E)$ for each $u$ and
  $u \mapsto g(u,x)$ measurable.  Put $G = E^A$ with the product topology,
  $\gamma(\omega) = (\omega(a))_{a \in A}$, and
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:Yhat}
    \hat{Y}^\circ_t(\omega,\xi) \;=\; f(\omega(t))
      - \int_{\langle 0,t\rangle_\iota} g(u,\omega(u)) \, q_{\mathrm{c}}(\dif u)
      - \sum_{a \in A \cap \langle 0,t\rangle_\iota} m_a \, g(a,\xi_a) .
  \end{equation}
  Then $\hat{Y}^\circ_t \circ \hat\gamma = Y^\circ_t$, and \textup{(C3a$'$)} holds
  for every $t$ outside the countable set $\{t : P\{X_t \neq X_{t-}\} > 0\}$ ---
  the same exceptional set as in the atomless case, and in particular independent
  of $A$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
  The identity $\hat{Y}^\circ_t \circ \hat\gamma = Y^\circ_t$ is the splitting
  $q = q_{\mathrm{a}} + q_{\mathrm{c}}$, the atomic integral being
  $\sum_{a \in A \cap \langle 0,t\rangle_\iota} m_a g(a,\omega(a))$; note that the
  index set $A \cap \langle 0,t\rangle_\iota$ is \emph{deterministic}, which is
  the whole point.  We check continuity of the three summands of
  \eqref{eq:Yhat} on $\hat{F} = \DE \times E^A$.

  \emph{The atomic sum.}  Each $(\omega,\xi) \mapsto g(a,\xi_a)$ is continuous, the
  coordinate projections of a product being continuous and $g(a,\cdot)$ being
  continuous.  The series converges uniformly on $\hat{F}$, since
  $\sum_{a \in A \cap \langle 0,t\rangle_\iota} m_a \leq q(\T_{\leq t}) < \infty$ by
  Definition~\ref{def:clock} and $|g| \leq \lVert g\rVert$.  A uniform limit of
  continuous functions is continuous, so this summand is continuous
  \emph{everywhere} on $\hat{F}$ --- no exceptional set at all.  \emph{This is
  where the augmentation does its work}: on $\DE$ alone the same sum is
  discontinuous at every path jumping at an atom, which is
  Example~\ref{ex:atomicdiscontinuity}.

  \emph{The diffuse integral.}  Let $\omega_m \to \omega$ in $J_1$.  Then
  $\omega_m(u) \to \omega(u)$ for every $u$ outside the at most countable set of
  discontinuities of $\omega$, hence $q_{\mathrm{c}}$-almost everywhere,
  $q_{\mathrm{c}}$ being atomless; by continuity of $g(u,\cdot)$ and bounded
  convergence the integral converges.  So this summand too is continuous
  everywhere, for \emph{every} $\omega$, and it does not use the second
  coordinate.

  \emph{The evaluation $f(\omega(t))$.}  This is the classical term: it is
  $J_1$-continuous at every $\omega$ with $\omega(t) = \omega(t-)$, so it is
  $\hat{P}$-continuous at $\hat{X}$ as soon as $P\{X_t = X_{t-}\} = 1$, and by
  Fact~\ref{fact:Dcountable} the set of $t$ failing this is countable.

  Finally $Z^\circ_s \circ \mathrm{pr}_F$ is bounded and, for $s$ in the same
  exceptional-free set, continuous at $\hat{X}$ $\hat{P}$-a.s.\ by
  Example~\ref{ex:determining}; products of $\hat{P}$-continuous functions are
  $\hat{P}$-continuous.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What \ref{it:C1aug} asks, concretely]
  \label{rem:C1aug}
  For $G = E^A$ the hypothesis \ref{it:C1aug} reads: \emph{$X^n \wto X$ in $J_1$,
  jointly with convergence of the values at the atoms of the clock}.  This is
  ordinary weak convergence on a product of Polish spaces, so Prohorov
  (Fact~\ref{fact:prohorov}) applies and tightness is the tightness of
  $\{X^n\}$ in $\DE$ together with that of the finitely or countably many
  $E$-valued families $\{X^n_a\}$, which for $E$ Polish is automatic once
  $\{X^n\}$ is tight in $\DE$.  What is \emph{not} automatic is the
  identification of the limit: one must know that $X^n_a \wto X_a$ and not, say,
  $X^n_a \wto X_{a-}$.  That is exactly the content of
  Example~\ref{ex:atomicdiscontinuity}, and it is a condition on the
  approximating scheme --- it holds whenever the $X^n$ jump \emph{at} the atoms
  rather than near them, which is the case for schemes whose grid contains $A$,
  and in particular in Example~\ref{ex:invariance}.

  It is worth being explicit that \ref{it:C1aug} is strictly stronger than
  \ref{it:C1}: the sequence $\omega_n = \one_{[1+1/n,\infty)}$ of
  Example~\ref{ex:atomicdiscontinuity} converges in $J_1$ but not in
  $\DE \times E^{\{1\}}$.  Something has to be strengthened, and the question is
  only what.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Augmentation versus weak-strong convergence]
  \label{rem:augvsws}
  Proposition~\ref{prop:atomaug} settles Example~\ref{ex:atomicdiscontinuity}
  with the continuous mapping theorem and Vitali's theorem, both of which the
  manuscript already has (Facts~\ref{fact:cmt} and \ref{fact:ui}).  Set against
  the alternative below, the ledger reads:
  \begin{center}
  \begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.44\textwidth}p{0.44\textwidth}@{}}
  \toprule
  \textbf{Augmentation} (Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug}) &
  \textbf{Weak-strong} (Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvws}) \\
  \midrule
  one Borel map $\gamma : F \to G$ &
  a control space $U$, Definitions~\ref{def:weakstrong} and \ref{def:PnPcont} \\
  ordinary weak convergence on $F \times G$ &
  a new mode of convergence \\
  Facts~\ref{fact:cmt}, \ref{fact:ui} &
  Fact~\ref{fact:jacodmemin} (Jacod--M\'emin), cited unproved \\
  discontinuity at \emph{deterministic} times &
  discontinuity at \emph{random} times \\
  \bottomrule
  \end{tabular}
  \end{center}
  The last line is the real division, and it is what decides which to use.  The
  atoms of a clock are fixed in advance, so the bad set of paths is
  $\{\omega : \omega(a) \neq \omega(a-) \text{ for some } a \in A\}$ with $A$
  deterministic, and carrying the finitely or countably many numbers
  $(\omega(a))_{a \in A}$ removes it.  \CPS{} need more because in their
  Section~5.3 the times at which continuity fails are the jump times of the
  approximating processes, which are random and different for each $n$; no fixed
  countable family of coordinates captures them, and the exceptional set must be
  allowed to depend on a control variable.

  For everything in this manuscript the deterministic case is the case that
  occurs.  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug} is therefore the tool to reach for, and
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvws} is recorded for completeness and because it is what
  the literature states.
\end{remark}

\subsubsection*{The general repair of \CPS}

The repair of \CPS{} is to weaken not the continuity but the \emph{space on which
it is demanded}: a continuity that holds along random sections is enough, provided
the sections are indexed by a variable that converges in a stronger sense than
weakly.  This is the setting we have so far silently avoided by taking the control
space to be a singleton.

\begin{definition}[Weak-strong convergence; \CPS, Definition 2.1]
  \label{def:weakstrong}
  Let $(U,\mathcal{U})$ be a measurable space, let $F$ be Polish, and put
  $S = U \times F$ with $\mathcal{S} = \mathcal{U} \otimes \Bor(F)$.  Let $C_S$ be
  the set of bounded $\mathcal{S}$-measurable $f : S \to \R$ such that
  $\omega \mapsto f(\alpha,\omega)$ is continuous for \emph{every} $\alpha \in U$.
  Probability measures $P^n$ on $S$ converge to $P$ in the \emph{weak-strong}
  sense, $P^n \to_{\mathrm{ws}} P$, if $E^{P^n}[f] \to E^P[f]$ for every
  $f \in C_S$.
\end{definition}

Continuity is thus required in the second variable only; in the first, measurability
suffices.  If $U$ is a singleton this is ordinary weak convergence
(\CPS, Remark~2.2(i)), which is why nothing of it has appeared until now.

\begin{definition}[$(P^n,P)$-continuity; \CPS, Definition 2.7]
  \label{def:PnPcont}
  An $\mathcal{S}$-measurable $g : S \to \R$ is \emph{$(P^n,P)$-continuous} if
  there is $A \in \mathcal{S}$ with $P^n(A) \to 1$ and $P(A) = 1$ such that
  \[
    \bigl\{ (\alpha,\omega) \in A \;:\;
      A_\alpha \ni \zeta \mapsto g(\alpha,\zeta) \text{ is discontinuous at } \omega \bigr\}
  \]
  is $P$-null, where $A_\alpha = \{\zeta : (\alpha,\zeta) \in A\}$.
\end{definition}

Two relaxations at once: continuity is asked for only along the sections
$A_\alpha$, and only on a set of asymptotically full measure.  For a singleton
$U$ and $A = U \times C$ this is Definition~\ref{def:Pcont}.

\begin{fact}[Continuous mapping, Jacod--M\'emin; \CPS, Theorem 2.9]
  \label{fact:jacodmemin}
  If $P^n \to_{\mathrm{ws}} P$ and $g : S \to \R$ is $(P^n,P)$-continuous with
  $\sup_n E^{P^n}[|g| \one_{\{|g|>a\}}] \to 0$ as $a \to \infty$, then
  $E^{P^n}[g] \to E^P[g]$.
\end{fact}

\begin{theorem}[Abstract convergence with control variables; \CPS, Theorem 3.14]
  \label{thm:absconvws}
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} holds with $X^n, X$ replaced by pairs
  $(L^n,X^n), (L,X)$ taking values in $S = U \times F$, with \ref{it:C1} replaced
  by $(L^n,X^n) \to_{\mathrm{ws}} (L,X)$ and the $P$-continuity of \ref{it:C3a}
  replaced by $(Q^n,Q)$-continuity, where $Q^n$ and $Q$ are the laws of
  $(L^n,X^n)$ and $(L,X)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof, or rather: what has to be changed]
  Nothing, beyond two citations.  Steps~0, 1 and 2 of the proof of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} each consist of applying the continuous mapping
  theorem (Fact~\ref{fact:cmt}) to obtain weak convergence and then
  Fact~\ref{fact:ui} to upgrade it to convergence of expectations.
  Fact~\ref{fact:jacodmemin} performs both steps at once under the weaker
  hypotheses, and is the only place where the difference between weak and
  weak-strong convergence is felt.  Step~3 uses no continuity at all.  All the
  work is therefore in Fact~\ref{fact:jacodmemin}, which we do not prove.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What the control variable does]
  \label{rem:controlvars}
  The point of $U$ is that the exceptional set may depend on $\alpha$.  In
  \CPS, Section~5.3 the control variables $L^n$ are c\`adl\`ag processes chosen so
  that, with probability tending to one, \emph{the jump times of $X^n$ are
  dominated by those of $L^n$} (their Assumption 5.1).  On the resulting sections
  the Skorokhod $J_1$ and the local uniform topology coincide (their
  Proposition~2.12), and the functional of
  Example~\ref{ex:atomicdiscontinuity} --- discontinuous in $J_1$ at every
  $t \geq 1$ --- becomes continuous.  One may then ``treat the control variables as
  deterministic'', which is the practical content of
  Definition~\ref{def:weakstrong}.

  So the answer to why weak-strong convergence has been absent is that
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} is \CPS, Corollary~3.17, the case of a singleton
  control space, and that in that case \ref{it:C3a} is available whenever the
  limit has no fixed times of discontinuity.  Atoms in the clock destroy that,
  but --- and this is the conclusion of Remark~\ref{rem:augvsws} --- they destroy
  it at deterministic times, which Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug} handles with the
  continuous mapping theorem.  So the price atoms exact is \emph{not} a new mode
  of convergence but a joint convergence hypothesis, \ref{it:C1aug}.  Weak-strong
  convergence is needed for a different reason: random times of discontinuity,
  which no fixed family of coordinates captures.  In Section~\ref{sec:duality}
  atoms do cost something, though not the conclusion: they obstruct the
  time change of Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless}, so that the atomic case has to be
  treated by a separate argument (Remark~\ref{rem:atomsnotchange} and
  Proposition~\ref{prop:atomicdual}).
\end{remark}

\subsection{Convergence in measure}
\label{ssec:measureconv}

Every convergence statement so far has been made for the Skorokhod $J_1$
topology, and $J_1$ asks for a great deal: the approximating processes must place
their jumps at nearly the right times and of nearly the right sizes.  A sequence
can fail it for reasons that have nothing to do with the martingale problem --- two
jumps of $X_n$ merging into one jump of $X$ is the standard example, and $J_1$
also fails when the jump times are perturbed by a vanishing but non-uniform
amount.  What survives such perturbations is convergence \emph{in measure} of the
paths.  \MZ{} showed that this weaker mode has, for quasimartingales, a
compactness theory that $J_1$ does not have, and Remark~\ref{rem:absconvtopfree}
makes it cheap to record that the abstract theorem transfers to it.  The point of
this subsection is that the transfer costs one page and no new machinery: it is
the last of the three relaxations of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} --- \ref{it:C3c}
in Section~\ref{ssec:diffclocks}, \ref{it:C3a} in Section~\ref{ssec:weakstrong},
and now \ref{it:C1} itself.

\begin{fact}[Pseudo-paths; \MZ, Section~1 and Lemma~1]
  \label{fact:pseudopath}
  Let $\lambda(\dif u) = e^{-u} \dif u$ on $\Rp$ and let $E$ be Polish.  The
  \emph{pseudo-path} of a Borel map $w : \Rp \to E$ is the image of $\lambda$
  under $u \mapsto (u, w(u))$, a probability measure on $[0,\infty] \times
  \hat{E}$ for a compact metrizable $\hat E \supset E$.  This assignment
  identifies two paths exactly when they agree $\lambda$-a.e., so it is injective
  on $\DE$, and the topology it induces on $\DE$ --- the \emph{pseudo-path
  topology} --- is
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
  \item \label{it:pp_meas} exactly the topology of convergence in $\lambda$-measure,
    hence in Lebesgue measure on compact time intervals;
  \item \label{it:pp_sep} metrizable and separable, being induced from the weak
    topology on the laws on a compact metric space; but \emph{not} Polish
    (B.~V.~Rao; \MZ, Appendix), $\DE$ being merely Borel in that compact space;
  \item \label{it:pp_sigma} of the same Borel $\sigma$-field on $\DE$ as $J_1$,
    namely $\sigma(\pi_u : u \in \Rp)$ (\MZ, footnote~2).
  \end{enumerate}
  The coordinates $\pi_u$ are, by contrast, discontinuous at every point of $\DE$.
\end{fact}

\begin{fact}[Tightness; \MZ, Theorem~4, and \Ku]
  \label{fact:MZtight}
  For a c\`adl\`ag adapted $X$ with $X_u \in L^1$ and the convention
  $X_\infty = 0$, let
  \[
    V(X) \;=\; \sup_\tau\; E\Bigl[ \sum_i \bigl| E[ X_{t_{i+1}} - X_{t_i} \mid \Filt_{t_i} ] \bigr| \Bigr]
    \qquad (\tau = \{ 0 = t_0 < t_1 < \dots < t_n < \infty \})
  \]
  be its \emph{conditional variation}, so that $V(X) \geq \sup_u E|X_u|$, and call
  $X$ a quasimartingale if $V(X) < \infty$.  If $P^n$ are laws on $\DE$ under
  which the coordinate process is a quasimartingale with $\sup_n V < \infty$, then
  a subsequence of $(P^n)$ converges weakly on $\DE$ in the pseudo-path topology
  to a law $P$ under which the coordinate process is again a quasimartingale ---
  in particular is c\`adl\`ag.  \Ku{} extends the criterion to a separable metric
  state space and identifies the resulting convergence as $J_1$-convergence of
  time-changed processes.
\end{fact}

The point of Fact~\ref{fact:MZtight} is what it does \emph{not} require: no
modulus of continuity, no Aldous condition, no compact containment
\eqref{eq:ccn}.  A single first-moment bound on the drift, uniform in $n$,
replaces the whole apparatus behind Remark~\ref{rem:EKrelcompact}.  This is the
reason to want the following.

\begin{theorem}[Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv} for convergence in measure; \eqref{T3}]
  \label{thm:MZconv}
  Assume \eqref{E3}, let $A \subset \Cb(E) \times \Cb(E)$ and
  $A_n \subset \Bdd(E) \times \Bdd(E)$, and suppose that for each $(f,g) \in A$
  there are $(f_n,g_n) \in A_n$ satisfying \eqref{eq:approxA}, or more generally
  \eqref{eq:approxpath}.  Let $X_n$ solve the martingale problem for $A_n$ with
  sample paths in $\DE$, let $X$ have sample paths in $\DE$, and suppose
  \[
    X_n \;\wto\; X \quad\text{on } \DE \text{ in the pseudo-path topology.}
  \]
  Then $X$ is a solution of the martingale problem for $A$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  The conclusion is a property of the law of $X$ alone, and each hypothesis on
  $X_n$ is a property of the law of $X_n$ alone --- for the martingale property
  this is Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar}.  We may therefore replace the sequence
  by any subsequence, and may realize all the processes on whatever space is
  convenient.  Fix $(f,g) \in A$ with approximants $(f_n,g_n)$, and let
  $Y^\circ_t(\omega) = f(\omega(t)) - \int_0^t g(\omega(u))\dif u$ and
  $Y^{\circ,n}$ be as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv}.

  \emph{Step 1: an almost sure realization.}  By
  Fact~\ref{fact:pseudopath}\ref{it:pp_sep} the pseudo-path topology on $\DE$ is
  separable metric, so Fact~\ref{fact:PSpolish} provides $\DE$-valued random
  variables $\tilde X_n, \tilde X$ on one probability space, with the laws of
  $X_n$ and $X$ --- the same laws on the same $\sigma$-field, by
  Fact~\ref{fact:pseudopath}\ref{it:pp_sigma} --- and $\tilde X_n \to \tilde X$
  a.s.\ in the pseudo-path topology.  Being c\`adl\`ag and measurable in $\omega$
  for each $u$, each $\tilde X_n$ is jointly measurable in $(\omega,u)$.

  \emph{Step 2: from convergence in measure to convergence a.e.}  Write $\rho$ for
  a metric on $E$ bounded by $1$.  By
  Fact~\ref{fact:pseudopath}\ref{it:pp_meas}, for a.e.\ $\omega$ the paths
  $\tilde X_n(\omega,\cdot)$ converge to $\tilde X(\omega,\cdot)$ in
  $\lambda$-measure, so $\int \rho(\tilde X_n, \tilde X) \dif\lambda \to 0$ a.s.;
  the integrals being bounded by $1$, dominated convergence gives
  $E \int \rho(\tilde X_n,\tilde X)\dif\lambda \to 0$, i.e.\ $\tilde X_n \to
  \tilde X$ in $P \otimes \lambda$-measure.  Pass to a subsequence along which the
  convergence is $P \otimes \lambda$-a.e.  Fubini now gives both halves of what is
  needed:
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item \label{it:mz_fdd} there is $I \subset \Rp$ of full Lebesgue measure ---
    $\lambda$ and Lebesgue measure being equivalent --- such that
    $\tilde X_n(r) \to \tilde X(r)$ a.s.\ for every $r \in I$;
  \item \label{it:mz_int} for a.e.\ $\omega$ the paths converge Lebesgue-a.e.,
    that is $\tilde X_n(\omega,u) \to \tilde X(\omega,u)$ for a.e.\ $u$; since $g$
    is bounded and continuous, dominated convergence then gives
    $\int_0^t |g(\tilde X_n(\omega,u)) - g(\tilde X(\omega,u))| \dif u \to 0$
    for \emph{every} $t$.
  \end{enumerate}
  Let $D \subset I$ be countable and dense; such a set exists because $I$ has full
  measure, hence is dense, and $\Rp$ is second countable.

  \emph{Step 3: the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}.}  We verify
  \eqref{eq:C1prime} of Remark~\ref{rem:absconvtopfree} together with
  \ref{it:C2}, \ref{it:C3b} and \ref{it:C3c}, on the set $D$.

  For \ref{it:C2} take the determining set of Example~\ref{ex:determining},
  built from $D$.  This is legitimate by part~\ref{it:pp_sigma} of
  Fact~\ref{fact:pseudopath}, which says that the $\sigma$-field in play is the
  one that example is about.  Its
  elements are $Z^\circ_s = \prod_{i \leq k} h_i(\pi_{t_i})$ with $t_i \in D \cap
  [0,s]$ and $h_i \in \Cb(E)$.

  For \eqref{eq:C1prime}, let $r \in D$.  By \ref{it:mz_fdd} and continuity of
  $f$, $f(\tilde X_n(r)) \to f(\tilde X(r))$ a.s., and by \ref{it:mz_int} the
  integral term converges a.s.\ as well, so $Y^\circ_r(\tilde X_n) \to
  Y^\circ_r(\tilde X)$ a.s.  Likewise $Z^\circ_s(\tilde X_n) \to Z^\circ_s(\tilde
  X)$ a.s., since every $t_i$ lies in $D \subset I$.  Products of a.s.\ convergent
  sequences converge a.s., hence in distribution, which is \eqref{eq:C1prime}.
  Note that this is where the argument differs from every earlier one in this
  section: the coordinates are nowhere continuous, so \ref{it:C3a} is unavailable
  and \emph{only} the weakened form \eqref{eq:C1prime} can be verified --- and it
  is verified not by a continuous mapping theorem but by exhibiting the
  convergence on a common space.

  \ref{it:C3b} holds because $|Y^\circ_r| \leq \lVert f \rVert + t \lVert g\rVert$
  for $r \leq t$, and \ref{it:C3c} is verified exactly as in
  Corollary~\ref{cor:EKconvpath}, an estimate that involves no topology.

  \emph{Conclusion.}  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}, as amended by
  Remark~\ref{rem:absconvtopfree}, gives the martingale identity on $D$.  Now $\T
  = \Rp$ satisfies \eqref{T2b} with the countable dense set $D$ and has no
  greatest element, and $Y$ is right continuous because $X$ has paths in $\DE$; so
  the last clause of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} upgrades the identity to all of
  $\T$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What convergence in measure costs]
  \label{rem:MZcost}
  Four things, of which only the last is serious.

  \emph{The dense set is not explicit.}  Under $J_1$ one takes $D$ to be the
  continuity set of the limit and knows by Fact~\ref{fact:Dcountable} that its
  complement is countable.  Here $D$ is an unspecified countable subset of an
  unspecified Lebesgue-full set, and there is no analogue of
  Fact~\ref{fact:Dcountable}: \MZ, Theorem~5 gives full measure and no more.  This
  costs nothing because Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} was written for an arbitrary
  dense $D$ from the start, and the passage from $D$ to $\T$ is its Step~3 --- the
  same regularization that Section~\ref{sec:cadlag} performs in general.  Note the
  order of business: one does \emph{not} first prove a limit theorem at all times
  and then regularize; one proves it a.e.\ and lets right continuity do the rest.

  \emph{The path space is not Polish.}  It is separable metric, which is all that
  Facts~\ref{fact:cmt} and~\ref{fact:PSpolish} were stated for, and this
  manuscript never used more.  It does mean that a formalization must have the
  continuous mapping theorem and the Skorokhod representation at that generality
  and not only for Polish spaces (Section~\ref{ssec:available}).

  \emph{C\`adl\`ag paths of the limit must be known separately.}  In the
  pseudo-path topology $\DE$ is not closed, and the limit of pseudo-paths of
  c\`adl\`ag functions need not be one; this is why it is a hypothesis of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:MZconv} and a conclusion of Fact~\ref{fact:MZtight}, which
  supplies it from the quasimartingale bound.

  \emph{Nothing about jumps passes to the limit.}  Convergence in measure is blind
  to any functional that is not an $\dif u$-average or an a.e.-$u$ evaluation:
  $\sup_{u \leq t} \rho(X_n(u), x_0)$, the jump measure, the number of jumps in
  $[0,t]$, the value at a prescribed time.  So the mode is exactly right for
  identifying a limit through a martingale problem --- which is an a.e.-$u$
  statement, as this proof makes visible --- and wrong for anything that reads the
  paths pointwise.  Section~\ref{ssec:weakstrong} is the sharp illustration: the
  functional of Example~\ref{ex:atomicdiscontinuity} is the evaluation at the atom
  of the clock, so a clock with atoms is precisely a martingale problem that
  convergence in measure cannot see, and no augmentation repairs it, since
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug} augments by coordinates and coordinates are what
  fail.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Three topologies, and what a formalization should do]
  \label{rem:threetopologies}
  Between $J_1$ and convergence in measure sits Jakubowski's $S$-topology
  \cite{Jak97}, which retains finite-dimensional convergence outside a
  \emph{countable} set --- so Fact~\ref{fact:Dcountable} survives --- and for
  which both Prohorov theorems hold, at the price of not being metrizable, only
  sequential.  For the present purpose the ordering is clear.
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} in the form of Remark~\ref{rem:absconvtopfree} asks
  for no topology at all, so the choice is not a choice about the theorem but
  about which convergence one can verify in a given application; and a
  non-metrizable topology is a poor thing to formalize, since
  Facts~\ref{fact:cmt} and \ref{fact:PSpolish} would both have to be replaced.
  The recommendation is therefore to formalize
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} with \eqref{eq:C1prime} in place of \ref{it:C1} and
  \ref{it:C3a} --- costing nothing, since its proof is unchanged --- and to treat
  $J_1$, weak-strong convergence and the pseudo-path topology as three ways of
  discharging one hypothesis, none of which the theorem needs to know about.
  \Ku{} makes the relation between the outer two precise: convergence in the
  pseudo-path topology is $J_1$-convergence after a random time change, which is
  the same move as the clock change of Section~\ref{ssec:diffclocks} with the
  clock allowed to depend on the path.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Hawkes processes and their Volterra limit}
\label{ssec:hawkes}

This last subsection is a single example, and it is here because it is the first
place where every strand of the manuscript is used at once: the explicit
construction of Section~\ref{ssec:jump}, the localization of
Section~\ref{ssec:localmp}, the \emph{non-Markovian} uniqueness of
Section~\ref{ssec:absuniq}, and the convergence theorem of
Section~\ref{ssec:absconv}.  Throughout, $\T = \Rp$ and $q$ is Lebesgue measure.

\subsubsection*{Jump processes with a path-dependent rate}

Section~\ref{ssec:jump} constructed jump processes from a rate $\lambda(x)$ and a
kernel $\mu(x,\cdot)$ depending on the current state.  Nothing in that
construction needs the dependence to be Markovian, and one gain is worth the
change of notation: the holding time ceases to be exponential, and the
computation that carried Theorem~\ref{thm:jumpMP} survives intact.

\begin{setting}[Path-dependent jump data; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{set:pathjump}
  Let $F$ be the set of piecewise constant right continuous paths $\T \to E$ with
  finitely many jumps on bounded intervals, and let
  \[
    \Lambda : \T \times F \to [0,\infty) ,
    \qquad
    \mu : \T \times F \to \Prob(E)
  \]
  be \emph{predictable}: $\Lambda(t,\omega)$ and $\mu(t,\omega,\cdot)$ depend on
  $\omega$ only through $\omega|_{[0,t)}$, and $t \mapsto \Lambda(t,\omega)$ is
  locally integrable for every $\omega$.  Put
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:pathgen}
    \mathcal{A}_t f(\omega)
      \;=\; \Lambda(t,\omega) \int_E
        \bigl( f(y) - f(\omega(t-)) \bigr) \, \mu(t,\omega,\dif y) ,
    \qquad f \in \Bdd(E) ,
  \end{equation}
  and let $\XX^\circ$ consist of the processes
  $f(\pi_t) - \int_0^t \mathcal{A}_s f \dif s$, $f \in \Bdd(E)$.
\end{setting}

\begin{definition}[The construction; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{def:pathjumpconstruction}
  Let $\varepsilon_1,\varepsilon_2,\dots$ be i.i.d.\ exponential of parameter $1$.
  Set $\tau_0 = 0$, let $Y_0 \sim \nu$, and given $(\tau_n, Y_n)$ let
  $\omega^{(n)}$ be the path built so far, continued constantly at $Y_n$; put
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:pathjumptime}
    \tau_{n+1} \;=\; \inf\Bigl\{ t > \tau_n \;:\;
      \int_{\tau_n}^{t} \Lambda(u,\omega^{(n)}) \dif u > \varepsilon_{n+1} \Bigr\} ,
  \end{equation}
  and draw $Y_{n+1} \sim \mu(\tau_{n+1},\omega^{(n)},\cdot)$.  Define $X$ by
  \eqref{eq:jumpprocess} and $\zeta = \lim_n \tau_n$ as before.
\end{definition}

Writing $A_n(u) = \int_{\tau_n}^{u}\Lambda(v,\omega^{(n)})\dif v$, the construction
gives, conditionally on $\mathcal{H}_n = \sigma(Y_0,\dots,Y_n,\tau_1,\dots,\tau_n)$,
the survival function $P(\tau_{n+1}>u \mid \mathcal{H}_n) = e^{-A_n(u)}$ and the
jump-time density $\Lambda(u,\omega^{(n)})e^{-A_n(u)}$.  For $\Lambda$ constant
this is the exponential law of Definition~\ref{def:jumpconstruction}.

\begin{theorem}[Path-dependent jump processes; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E0}]
  \label{thm:pathjumpMP}
  Let $X$ be as in Definition~\ref{def:pathjumpconstruction} and let
  $N_t = \#\{n \geq 1 : \tau_n \leq t\}$ be its jump counter.
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item \label{it:pj_loc}
    $X$ solves the \emph{local} martingale problem for $\XX^\circ$ of
    Setting~\ref{set:pathjump} on $[0,\zeta)$, with initial law $\nu$ and with
    $(\tau_n)$ a localizing system in the sense of
    Definition~\ref{def:localizing}.  No hypothesis beyond
    Setting~\ref{set:pathjump} is needed.
  \item \label{it:pj_glob}
    If $E[N_t] < \infty$ for every $t$ --- equivalently, by \ref{it:pj_loc},
    $E\bigl[\int_0^t \Lambda(u,X)\dif u\bigr] < \infty$ --- then $\zeta = \infty$
    a.s.\ and $X$ solves the \emph{global} martingale problem for $\XX^\circ$.
  \end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[Why the local statement is the primary one]
  \label{rem:pathjumpprimary}
  In Setting~\ref{set:jumpdata} the bound $\bar\lambda < \infty$ gave
  $E[N_t] \leq \bar\lambda t$ for free, and the global statement was the natural
  one.  With a path-dependent rate this is no longer so, and the failure is not
  about explosion: even when $\zeta = \infty$ a.s., the compensator
  $\int_0^t \Lambda(u,X)\dif u$ need not be integrable, so
  $f(X_t) - \int_0^t \mathcal{A}_sf \dif s$ need not lie in $L^1$ and the phrase
  ``is a martingale'' has no content.  Stopping at $\tau_n$ repairs this
  \emph{unconditionally}, because
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:compensatorexp}
    \int_{\tau_k}^{\tau_{k+1}} \Lambda(u,\omega^{(k)}) \dif u \;=\; \varepsilon_{k+1}
    \qquad \text{on } \{\tau_{k+1} < \infty\}
  \end{equation}
  by the very definition \eqref{eq:pathjumptime} of $\tau_{k+1}$, so that
  $\int_0^{t \wedge \tau_n} \Lambda \dif u \leq \varepsilon_1 + \dots + \varepsilon_n$
  is integrable.  This is a clean instance of the phenomenon
  Definition~\ref{def:localizing} exists for, and unlike
  Remark~\ref{rem:jumpexplosion} it is not about paths leaving every compact set.
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}
  The decomposition of Step~1 in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:jumpMP} is
  unchanged, so the first task is to show $E[D_n \mid \mathcal{H}_n] = 0$ on
  $\{\tau_n \leq t\}$ for
  \[
    D_n = \bigl( f(Y_{n+1}) - f(Y_n) \bigr)\one_{\{\tau_{n+1}\leq t\}}
        - \int_{\tau_n \wedge t}^{\tau_{n+1}\wedge t} \mathcal{A}_s f \dif s .
  \]
  Write $\Lambda_u = \Lambda(u,\omega^{(n)})$ and
  $\mu_u f = \int f \dif\mu(u,\omega^{(n)},\cdot)$, and note that on
  $\{\tau_{n+1} > s\}$ the path agrees with $\omega^{(n)}$, so that
  $\mathcal{A}_s f = \Lambda_s(\mu_s f - f(Y_n))$ there.  Then
  \begin{align*}
    E\bigl[ (f(Y_{n+1}) - f(Y_n))\one_{\{\tau_{n+1}\leq t\}} \bigm| \mathcal{H}_n \bigr]
      &= \int_{\tau_n}^{t} \Lambda_u e^{-A_n(u)}
         \bigl( \mu_u f - f(Y_n) \bigr) \dif u , \\
    E\Bigl[ \int_{\tau_n}^{\tau_{n+1}\wedge t} \mathcal{A}_s f \dif s
      \Bigm| \mathcal{H}_n \Bigr]
      &= \int_{\tau_n}^{t} \Lambda_s \bigl( \mu_s f - f(Y_n) \bigr)
         \, P(\tau_{n+1} > s \mid \mathcal{H}_n) \dif s ,
  \end{align*}
  the first because the jump time has density $\Lambda_u e^{-A_n(u)}$ and the jump
  distribution at time $u$ is $\mu(u,\omega^{(n)},\cdot)$, the second by Fubini.
  Since $P(\tau_{n+1}>s\mid\mathcal{H}_n) = e^{-A_n(s)}$, the two expressions
  \emph{coincide}, and $D_n = 0$ on $\{\tau_n > t\}$.  Both displays are finite
  because $|\mu_uf - f(Y_n)| \leq 2\lVert f\rVert$ and
  $\int_{\tau_n}^{t}\Lambda_u e^{-A_n(u)}\dif u \leq 1$.

  \emph{\ref{it:pj_loc}.}  Stop at $\tau_m$.  Then
  \eqref{eq:jumpbrackets} has only the $m$ summands $D_0,\dots,D_{m-1}$, each
  integrable by the previous paragraph, so no domination argument is needed and
  $E[M_{t\wedge\tau_m}] = 0$ for every $t$.  For the increment from $s$ to $t$,
  observe that conditionally on $\Filt^\circ_s$ and on $\{N_s = n\}$ the residual
  jump time satisfies
  \[
    P\bigl( \tau_{n+1} > u \bigm| \Filt^\circ_s \bigr)
      \;=\; \exp\Bigl\{ -\int_s^u \Lambda(v,\omega^{(n)}) \dif v \Bigr\} ,
    \qquad u \geq s ,
  \]
  by \eqref{eq:pathjumptime} and the lack of memory of $\varepsilon_{n+1}$ ---
  which here concerns the \emph{compensator} $A_n$, not the time; and that the
  subsequent construction is \eqref{eq:pathjumptime} again, with $\tau_n$ replaced
  by $s$ and with the past $\omega|_{[0,s]}$ carried along.  The computation above
  then applies with $\tau_n$ replaced by $s$ and $\mathcal{H}_n$ by
  $\Filt^\circ_s$, and gives $E[M_{t\wedge\tau_m} - M_{s\wedge\tau_m} \mid
  \Filt^\circ_s] = 0$.  Note that the restart carries the whole past, not merely
  $X_s$: this is Lemma~\ref{lem:restartmemory}, and it is the reason
  Theorem~\ref{thm:pathjumpMP} needs no shift system.  Since
  $\tau_m \uparrow \zeta$ and each $\tau_m$ is a functional of the path ---
  indeed a \emph{strict} stopping time, being the $m$-th jump time of a right
  continuous piecewise constant path --- $(\tau_m)$ is a localizing system, with
  \ref{it:L1} holding by construction, \ref{it:L2} because the $m$-th jump time
  after $r$ is a jump time, and \ref{it:L3} by \eqref{eq:compensatorexp}.

  \emph{\ref{it:pj_glob}.}  Assume $E[N_t] < \infty$.  Then
  $\tau_m \uparrow \infty$ a.s., so $\zeta = \infty$.  Moreover
  \[
    \sum_{n \geq 0} |D_n| \;\leq\; 2\lVert f\rVert N_t
      + 2\lVert f\rVert \int_0^t \Lambda(u,X) \dif u ,
  \]
  and by \eqref{eq:compensatorexp} the last integral equals
  $\sum_{k < N_t}\varepsilon_{k+1} + \int_{\tau_{N_t}}^{t}\Lambda \dif u
   \leq \sum_{k \leq N_t}\varepsilon_{k+1}$, whose expectation is
  $E[N_t] + 1 < \infty$ by Wald's identity, the $\varepsilon_k$ being i.i.d.\ of
  mean $1$ and $N_t + 1$ a stopping time for their natural filtration.  So
  dominated convergence applies to \eqref{eq:jumpbrackets}, and letting
  $m \to \infty$ in \ref{it:pj_loc} turns the local statement into the global one.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
  \label{rem:pathjumpsplit}
  The cancellation is the same one as in Theorem~\ref{thm:jumpMP}: the rate in the
  generator against the survival function of the holding time.  What has gone is
  only the Markov structure --- $\Lambda$ and $\mu$ may depend on the whole past
  --- and with it the shift system.  So Theorem~\ref{thm:pathjumpMP} produces
  solutions to which Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop} applies and
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq} does not, which is the situation
  Remark~\ref{rem:markovaudit} describes and which has so far been illustrated only
  by the cited Examples~\ref{ex:volterra} and \ref{ex:pathdepsemi}.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}[Hawkes processes]
  \label{ex:hawkes}
  Take $E = \N_0$, $\mu(t,\omega,\cdot) = \delta_{\omega(t-)+1}$ and
  \begin{equation}
    \label{eq:hawkesrate}
    \Lambda(t,\omega) \;=\; \mu_0 + \int_{[0,t)} \phi(t-s) \, \dif\omega_s ,
    \qquad \mu_0 > 0 , \ \phi : \Rp \to [0,\infty) ,
  \end{equation}
  with $\phi$ locally integrable.  Then $X = N$ is a \emph{Hawkes process}, and
  Theorem~\ref{thm:pathjumpMP} says that
  $f(N_t) - \int_0^t \Lambda_s (f(N_s+1) - f(N_s))\dif s$ is a martingale for every
  bounded $f$; taking $f = \mathrm{id}$ on bounded sets recovers the defining
  property that $N - \int_0^\cdot \Lambda_s \dif s$ is a martingale.

  \emph{A linear Hawkes process never explodes.}  The mean intensity
  $m(t) = E[\Lambda_t]$ satisfies the renewal equation
  $m = \mu_0 + \phi * m$, and a Volterra kernel $\phi \in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ has
  a resolvent in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ whatever its mass: on an interval short
  enough that $\int_0^\delta \phi < 1$ the Neumann series converges, and one steps
  forward.  So $E[N_t] = \int_0^t m < \infty$ for \emph{every} $t$ and every
  $\phi \geq 0$, and Theorem~\ref{thm:pathjumpMP}\ref{it:pj_glob} always applies.
  An earlier version of this manuscript asserted the opposite for
  $\lVert\phi\rVert_1 \geq 1$; that was a confusion of exponential growth with
  explosion.

  What $\lVert\phi\rVert_1 = 1$ separates is not existence but \emph{stability}:
  for $\lVert\phi\rVert_1 < 1$ the bound is uniform in $t$,
  $\sup_t E[\Lambda_t] \leq \mu_0/(1-\lVert\phi\rVert_1)$, and for
  $\lVert\phi\rVert_1 > 1$ it is not, $m$ growing exponentially.  The scaling
  limit below lives at $\lVert\phi\rVert_1 \uparrow 1$, where the two regimes
  meet.
\end{example}

\subsubsection*{The Volterra limit}

The observation that makes the limit theorem an application rather than an
analogy is that \eqref{eq:hawkesrate} \emph{is} a Volterra equation.

\begin{remark}[A Hawkes process is a Volterra SDE]
  \label{rem:hawkesisvolterra}
  Put $Z = N$ and $X = \Lambda$.  Then \eqref{eq:hawkesrate} reads
  \[
    X_t \;=\; g_0(t) + \int_{[0,t)} K_{t-s} \, \dif Z_s ,
    \qquad g_0 \equiv \mu_0 , \quad K = \phi ,
  \]
  which is verbatim the constraint of Example~\ref{ex:volterra}, and $Z$ is a
  semimartingale whose characteristics are driven by $X$: $a = 0$ and
  $\nu(x,\dif y) = x \, \delta_1(\dif y)$.  A Hawkes process is therefore a
  solution of a Volterra stochastic differential equation with pure-jump noise,
  and the pair $(\Lambda,N)$ lies in the path space
  $L^p_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Rp,\R) \times D_\R[0,\infty)$ of
  Example~\ref{ex:volterra}.  This identification is standard: Hawkes processes
  are the pure-jump members of the affine Volterra class of \ALP, and
  Section~\ref{ssec:exduality} uses their transform formula
  (Remark~\ref{rem:hawkesknown}).  Approximants and limit below are thus elements of
  one and the same solution set, for two triplets of the same shape --- one purely
  discontinuous, one continuous.
\end{remark}

\begin{theorem}[Hawkes to Volterra, conditionally on tightness; \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E3}]
  \label{thm:hawkesvolterra}
  For each $n$ let $(\Lambda^n, N^n)$ be a Hawkes process with kernel $\phi^n$ and
  baseline $\mu_0^n$, rescaled in time, and let $(X^n,Z^n)$ be the corresponding
  pair of Remark~\ref{rem:hawkesisvolterra}.  Let $\XX^\circ$ be the Volterra
  family of Example~\ref{ex:volterra} for a kernel $K$, a triplet $(b,a,\nu)$ and
  $g_0$.  Suppose
  \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
  \item \label{it:hv_tight}
    $(X^n,Z^n) \wto (X,Z)$ weakly on $L^p_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Rp,\R) \times D_\R[0,\infty)$;
  \item \label{it:hv_gen}
    the rescaled generators converge in the sense of \ref{it:C3c}, i.e.\ for
    $f \in C^2_b$ and $s \leq t$ in a dense $D$,
    $\lim_n E\bigl[ \bigl( \int_s^t (\mathcal{A}^n_u f - Lf(X^n_u,Z^n_u)) \dif u \bigr)
      Z^\circ_s(X^n,Z^n) \bigr] = 0$;
  \item \label{it:hv_ui}
    $\{ f(Z^n_r) - \int_0^r Lf(X^n_u,Z^n_u)\dif u : r \in D \cap [0,t],\, n \in \N \}$
    is uniformly integrable.
  \end{enumerate}
  Then $(X,Z)$ solves the Volterra martingale problem $\XX^\circ$.  If moreover
  that problem propagates agreement in the sense of
  Definition~\ref{def:propagation} --- uniqueness in law from any prescribed past
  --- then
  $(X,Z)$ is determined, and the whole sequence converges to it.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
  Hypotheses \ref{it:hv_tight}--\ref{it:hv_ui} are \ref{it:C1}, \ref{it:C3c} and
  \ref{it:C3b} of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}; \ref{it:C2} is
  Example~\ref{ex:determining} on the second coordinate together with the
  integrated form of the Volterra constraint, and \ref{it:C3a} holds because the
  first coordinate enters only through Lebesgue integrals --- which is why
  Example~\ref{ex:volterra} states the constraint in integrated form --- and the
  second through evaluations at times where the limit does not jump.  The
  approximating martingales required by \ref{it:C3c} are those of
  Theorem~\ref{thm:pathjumpMP}.  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} then gives
  $(X,Z) \in \Msol(\XX^\circ)$.  The final assertion is
  Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop}, whose hypothesis \eqref{eq:propagation} is
  uniqueness in law of the Volterra equation restarted from a prescribed past;
  it upgrades to uniqueness of the whole law with no Markov structure --- which is
  essential, since a Volterra process has none.  See Example~\ref{ex:volterra}
  and Remark~\ref{rem:whyZ}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What is proved here and what is cited]
  \label{rem:hawkescaveat}
  Hypothesis \ref{it:hv_tight} is tightness, and it is not supplied --- here as
  everywhere in this section (Remark~\ref{rem:invariancepay}).  For the Hawkes
  scaling it is the substance of the theorem: the interesting regime is
  $\lVert\phi^n\rVert_1 \uparrow 1$ with a heavy-tailed $\phi$, the kernel rescales
  to $K(t) = t^{\alpha-1}/\Gamma(\alpha)$, and the limit is a rough stochastic
  Volterra equation.  That is the content of \JR, and it is cited, not proved; the
  present theorem is the identification step, and it is the part the abstract
  machinery does supply.

  Three remarks on scope.  First, \emph{neither weak-strong convergence nor the
  augmentation of Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug} is needed}: the clock is Lebesgue,
  so there are no atoms, the jumps of $N^n$ have height one and the limit is
  continuous; the limit therefore has no fixed times of discontinuity and
  \ref{it:C3a} holds on a dense set with countable complement.
  Section~\ref{ssec:weakstrong} stays out of it entirely.  Second,
  \emph{the clock does not change} if one rescales the continuous-time Hawkes
  process, so Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} suffices and
  Section~\ref{ssec:diffclocks} is not used; it would be, if one approximated
  instead by the discrete branching representation, whose clock is atomic.
  Third, and this is the point of the example: \emph{no result of
  Section~\ref{ssec:markovlayer} is used anywhere}.  Neither the approximants nor
  the limit has a shift system, and none is wanted.
\end{remark}

% =====================================================================
\section{Notes for the formalization}
\label{sec:formalization}
% =====================================================================

\subsection{What is available and what has to be built}
\label{ssec:available}

Section~\ref{sec:notation} lists the prerequisites; the following is a stocktaking
of which of them Mathlib already supplies.

\emph{Available.}  Filtrations, adapted and progressively measurable processes,
stopping times and $\Filt_\tau$ (Definition~\ref{def:process},
Fact~\ref{fact:stoppingtimes}); martingales and the optional stopping theorem
(Definition~\ref{def:martingale}, Fact~\ref{fact:optsampl}) --- and note that
\texttt{MeasureTheory.Martingale} is stated for values in an arbitrary real
Banach space, so it covers $\K = \C$ with nothing to do; Doob's inequalities
(Fact~\ref{fact:doob}); Polish spaces; weak convergence of measures with the
portmanteau theorem, the L\'evy--Prokhorov metric and Prohorov's theorem
(Facts~\ref{fact:portmanteau}, \ref{fact:PSpolish}, \ref{fact:prohorov});
uniform integrability (Fact~\ref{fact:ui}); monotone class theorems
(Fact~\ref{fact:monotoneclass}); the Riesz--Markov--Kakutani representation of a
positive linear functional on compactly supported continuous functions, in the
form \texttt{integral\_rieszMeasure}, which is what
Proposition~\ref{prop:rieszmarkov} needs; and part of what
Fact~\ref{fact:kolmogorov} needs --- see the list there.

\emph{To be built.}
\begin{itemize}
\item the Skorokhod space with the $J_1$ metric, and the facts that it is
  Polish, that its Borel field is generated by the coordinates, and the
  characterization of its compact sets.  These are no longer cited but built:
  Section~\ref{sec:skorokhod}, and Remark~\ref{rem:skorokhodform} there is the
  stocktaking for them;
\item regularization of submartingales along a dense set
  (Facts~\ref{fact:cadlagext}, \ref{fact:submgreg}) --- but see
  Remark~\ref{rem:bmrepo}: this exists already, outside Mathlib;
\item local martingales in the \EK{} sense
  (Definition~\ref{def:localmartingale}, Fact~\ref{fact:stoppedlocalmg});
\item separating and convergence determining classes and their Stone--Weierstrass
  theory (Definition~\ref{def:separating},
  Facts~\ref{fact:convdet}, \ref{fact:stoneweierstrass}, \ref{fact:fdd},
  \ref{fact:sepcond}) --- the separating half of \ref{fact:fdd} only, its path
  space half being Theorem~\ref{thm:fdd};
\item bounded pointwise convergence and bp-closures
  (Definition~\ref{def:bp}, Fact~\ref{fact:bp}) --- but only for
  Corollary~\ref{cor:bpclosure}, which is itself optional: the working statement
  is Lemma~\ref{lem:closure}, and that is dominated convergence;
\item the continuous mapping theorem in the form of Fact~\ref{fact:cmt} and the
  Skorokhod representation (Fact~\ref{fact:PSpolish});
\item convergence of finite-dimensional distributions in $\DE$ and the relative
  compactness criteria (Facts~\ref{fact:fddconv}, \ref{fact:relcompact},
  \ref{fact:relcompact2});
\item dissipative operators and the full generator of a measurable contraction
  semigroup (Definitions~\ref{def:dissipative}, \ref{def:fullgenerator},
  Fact~\ref{fact:fullgenerator}); these are needed only for
  Proposition~\ref{prop:dissipative} and Remark~\ref{rem:fullgenerator}, which
  are themselves optional context;
\item the Kolmogorov extension theorem for an uncountable index
  (Fact~\ref{fact:kolmogorov}).  Mathlib has the scaffolding and the sequential
  case but not the theorem; this is the one external prerequisite of
  Section~\ref{ssec:exduality}, and for $\T = \N_0$ it is already there.
\end{itemize}

\begin{remark}[Prior art: the \texttt{brownian-motion} project]
  \label{rem:bmrepo}
  The c\`adl\`ag half of \ref{it:F4} is already formalized, not in Mathlib but in
  the Lean development accompanying \BM, in the repository
  \texttt{RemyDegenne/}\allowbreak\texttt{brownian-motion} on GitHub.  Its file
  \texttt{Cadlag.lean} defines \texttt{IsRightContinuous} for a preorder and
  \texttt{IsCadlag} as a structure, and develops jump sets, the discreteness of
  large jumps, local boundedness and the usual closure properties, with no
  remaining gaps.  Its \texttt{CadlagModification.lean} then constructs the
  c\`adl\`ag modification; the blueprint chapter proves that a submartingale has a
  c\`adl\`ag modification if and only if $t \mapsto E[X_t]$ is right continuous,
  and that a martingale always has one.  These are precisely
  Facts~\ref{fact:cadlagext} and \ref{fact:submgreg}, and they are proved for
  \emph{quasimartingales}, which is more general than what
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absreg} needs.

  Three consequences for the present plan.  First, \ref{it:F4} loses its most
  expensive prerequisite.  Second, the index there is a preorder with an order
  topology, which agrees with the bundle \eqref{T2b} of Definition~\ref{def:bundles} ---
  the two developments make the same choice independently.  Third, what is
  \emph{not} there is the Skorokhod space: no $J_1$ metric, no
  $\Bor(\DE) = \sigma(\pi_t)$, no compactness criterion.  So \ref{it:F5b} is
  untouched there, and Theorems~\ref{thm:DEpolish} and \ref{thm:DEcompact} are
  proved here instead.
\end{remark}

This dictates a natural order of work.  Each item names the bundle of
Definition~\ref{def:bundles} it targets; the point of doing so is that
\ref{it:F1}, \ref{it:F5a} and half of \ref{it:F2} live under
\texttt{[Preorder \(\iota\)]}, which is how Mathlib already indexes
\texttt{Filtration} and \texttt{Martingale}, and can therefore be stated without
any bespoke index theory.

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{(F\arabic*)}]
\item \label{it:F1}
  \emph{Targets \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E0} + clock.}
  Definitions only: Definition~\ref{def:absMP}, Definition~\ref{def:markovMP},
  the filtration \eqref{eq:starfilt}, Definition~\ref{def:cc} (which needs
  \eqref{T2b}), and Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar}.  The clock is a
  \texttt{MeasureTheory.Measure} on $\T$ together with the hypothesis that every
  down-set is measurable and of finite mass; the interval algebra
  \eqref{eq:clockinterval} is then definitional.  These need nothing beyond what Mathlib has and
  should be done first; note that Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar} is what turns
  every subsequent statement into a statement about finite-dimensional
  distributions.
\item \label{it:F0}
  \emph{Targets \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E0}.}  The jump processes of Section~\ref{ssec:jump}.  Theorem~\ref{thm:jumpMP} needs a Markov chain,
  i.i.d.\ exponential variables and the computation of Step~2, and nothing else;
  Proposition~\ref{prop:jumpwellposed} adds the Picard iteration
  \eqref{eq:jumpexp} for the bounded operator $A$, which in Lean is the
  exponential series of a bounded linear map and needs no analysis beyond
  \texttt{NormedSpace}.  It is by a wide margin the cheapest theorem with real
  content in this manuscript, it is the only one that exhibits a solution, and it
  supplies the missing example for Definition~\ref{def:localizing}.  It is placed
  here, before any of the theory, as a way of testing the definitions of
  \ref{it:F1} against something concrete.
\item \label{it:F2}
  \emph{Targets \eqref{T0} + \eqref{T4} + \eqref{E0}; \eqref{T2a} for the uniqueness half, \eqref{E1} for
  Lemma~\ref{lem:disint}.}
  Lemma~\ref{lem:mixture}, Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} and
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}, then Theorem~\ref{thm:uniqueness} and
  Corollary~\ref{cor:DEuniqueness} as corollaries.  Modulo \eqref{eq:pathsigma}
  this is pure measure theory, and Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq} needs no path
  regularity at all, so it is the best entry point after \ref{it:F1}.  Almost all
  of the work sits in Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}, which is four lines; the two
  theorems that follow are bookkeeping.  Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov}
  additionally needs Fact~\ref{fact:optsampl} and c\`adl\`ag paths and should be
  deferred until after \ref{it:F4}, and so should the local theory of
  Section~\ref{ssec:localmp} --- Definition~\ref{def:localizing} is about
  stopping times.  Note that
  Lemma~\ref{lem:localmix}\ref{it:lm_conv} and Lemma~\ref{lem:L1auto} are the
  only genuinely new proofs there; Lemma~\ref{lem:localrestart} is
  Lemma~\ref{lem:restart} plus the one identity \eqref{eq:shiftedstopped}, and
  Theorem~\ref{thm:localuniq} is bookkeeping.
\item \label{it:F5a}
  \emph{Targets \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E3}, with $D = \T$.}
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv}, the abstract convergence theorem.  It needs an
  abstract Polish path space, a determining set, $P$-continuity at $X$, uniform
  integrability and Vitali's theorem --- and \emph{no} Skorokhod topology, no
  c\`adl\`ag paths and no Markov structure.  It is placed here, immediately after
  \ref{it:F2}, and not with the Skorokhod theory of \ref{it:F5b} where the
  ordering of the literature would put it: it is one of the cheapest genuine
  theorems in this list, not part of the most expensive one.  Take
  Lemma~\ref{lem:contuse} and Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug} with it; they are two
  lines each once Theorem~\ref{thm:absconv} is in place.
\item \label{it:F3}
  \emph{Targets \eqref{T0} + \eqref{E0} for Lemma~\ref{lem:chain}, \eqref{T2a} + $\iota = \mathrm{p}$
  for Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless} (which needs \eqref{T3} and an atomless clock),
  \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E2} for the rest.}
  Lemma~\ref{lem:chain} --- an exact telescoping identity, and the cheapest item
  in this list --- then Proposition~\ref{prop:haar}, Corollary~\ref{cor:atomless},
  Corollary~\ref{cor:dualdiscrete}, and finally Lemma~\ref{lem:calculus},
  Theorem~\ref{thm:duality} and their corollaries.  Self-contained: Fubini,
  absolute continuity, dominated convergence.  No Skorokhod space is needed.
  Note that Corollary~\ref{cor:dualdiscrete} needs none of the analysis and can
  be formalized directly after Lemma~\ref{lem:chain}.  Together with \ref{it:F2} this already yields
  Corollary~\ref{cor:uniqviadual}, a complete uniqueness theory.
\item \label{it:F3b}
  \emph{Targets \eqref{T0} + \eqref{T4} + \eqref{E1} + a shift invariant clock.}
  Section~\ref{ssec:exduality}: Lemma~\ref{lem:dualsemigroup},
  Proposition~\ref{prop:dualCK} and Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality}.  Everything in
  the proofs is the Markov property, Fubini and \eqref{eq:clockadd}.  The one
  external input is Fact~\ref{fact:kolmogorov}, and it is a real one: Mathlib has
  the scaffolding and the sequential Ionescu--Tulcea case but not the Kolmogorov
  extension theorem for an uncountable index.  So this item splits: for
  $\T = \N_0$ it can be done at once, for $\T = \Rp$ it waits on that theorem.
  It uses \ref{it:F2} for Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq} and \ref{it:F3} for
  Corollary~\ref{cor:exdualitywellposed}, but Theorem~\ref{thm:exduality} itself
  needs neither.  Proposition~\ref{prop:rieszmarkov} is separate; Mathlib's
  \texttt{integral\_rieszMeasure} should carry it, but the theorem is stated with
  \ref{it:D3} as a hypothesis so that it can be skipped.
\item \label{it:F4}
  \emph{Targets \eqref{T2b} + \eqref{E2}, $\iota = \mathrm{o}$.}
  Fact~\ref{fact:cadlagext} and Fact~\ref{fact:submgreg}, then
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absreg} and, as a one-line corollary,
  Theorem~\ref{thm:cadlag}.  This requires the notion of a c\`adl\`ag path but
  \emph{not} the Skorokhod topology.  Formalize the abstract theorem, not the
  Markovian one: Definition~\ref{def:regclass} is three hypotheses on a pair of
  processes, whereas $\dom(A)$ drags in the operator, its domain and the
  filtration \eqref{eq:starfilt}, none of which the proof uses.
\item \label{it:F5b}
  \emph{Targets \eqref{T3} + \eqref{E3}.}
  The Skorokhod space and its topology, then Lemma~\ref{lem:EKconv},
  Remark~\ref{rem:EKrelcompact} and Theorem~\ref{thm:CPSconv} as instances of
  \ref{it:F5a}.  This is by far the largest item.
  Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug} belongs with \ref{it:F5a}, not here: it is
  Lemma~\ref{lem:contuse} plus a change of the ambient space, costs nothing once
  \ref{it:F5a} is done, and needs no Skorokhod theory.  Only
  Proposition~\ref{prop:atomaug}, which instantiates it for $F = \DE$, does.
  Section~\ref{ssec:weakstrong} beyond that --- Definitions~\ref{def:weakstrong}
  and \ref{def:PnPcont} and Fact~\ref{fact:jacodmemin} --- should be skipped:
  nothing in this manuscript needs it, by Remark~\ref{rem:augvsws}.
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{Design decisions}

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
\item \emph{Operators as relations.}  $A$ is a subset of
  $\Meas(E) \times \Meas(E)$, not a function.  In Lean this is
  \texttt{A : Set ((E \textrightarrow{} R) \texttimes{} (E \textrightarrow{} R))} together with measurability side
  conditions, or a structure bundling the pair with its measurability.  Keeping
  $A$ multivalued costs nothing and is needed for
  Remark~\ref{rem:fddconsequences}(b).
\item \emph{Two notions of solution.}  Definition~\ref{def:markovMP} defines
  ``solution with respect to $(\Gilt_t)$'' as the primitive and ``solution''
  as the special case $\Gilt_t = {}^{*}\Filt^X_t$.  Doing it the other way round
  duplicates every statement.
\item \emph{Compact containment as a predicate.}  Definition~\ref{def:cc} occurs
  in Theorem~\ref{thm:cadlag}, in Remark~\ref{rem:EKrelcompact} and implicitly in
  Remarks~\ref{rem:uniquelocal} and \ref{rem:convlocal}.  It should be one
  definition with the two variants \eqref{eq:cc} (over $D$, single process) and
  \eqref{eq:ccn} (over $[0,T]$, family) related by a lemma for right continuous
  processes.
\item \emph{Local martingale problems: localize the test processes, not the
  operator.}  Define the local martingale problem at the level of
  Definition~\ref{def:absMP} and localize by stopping the elements of $\XX^\circ$,
  as Definition~\ref{def:localizing} does.  The alternative --- replacing $A$ by
  $A^{(m)} = \{(f\one_{K_m}, g\one_{K_m})\}$ and passing to the limit, which is
  \EK, Section~4.6 and Remark~\ref{rem:uniquelocal} --- should \emph{not} be the
  primitive: in the abstract setting $\XX^\circ$ is primitive and $A$ is not, so
  the operator route has nothing to modify.  See Remark~\ref{rem:localsummary}.
  Concretely, the reusable object in Lean is the structure
  \ref{it:L1}--\ref{it:L3} of Definition~\ref{def:localizing}, and the reusable
  lemma is Lemma~\ref{lem:L1auto}, which \emph{constructs} \ref{it:L1} rather than
  assuming it.  Note that the times in $\Sigma$ must be \emph{strict} stopping
  times; Remark~\ref{rem:strictdebut} is the trap, and in Lean it is the
  difference between \texttt{IsStoppingTime} for the raw and for the right
  continuous filtration.
\item \emph{Separating classes.}  Facts~\ref{fact:sepcond} and \ref{fact:fdd}
  should be developed independently of martingale problems; they are of general
  interest, and within Sections~\ref{sec:cadlag} and \ref{sec:uniqueness} they are
  the only place where Polishness of $E$ is really used.  (In
  Section~\ref{sec:convergence} it is used throughout, and irreducibly: \eqref{E3}
  is what makes Prohorov's theorem available.)
\item \emph{No semigroup theory.}  The manuscript rests on Chapters~2 and~3 of
  \EK{} alone, as envisaged in the project plan; of Chapter~1 only the notions of
  a dissipative operator and of the full generator are borrowed, and only for the
  optional Proposition~\ref{prop:dissipative} and Remark~\ref{rem:fullgenerator}.
  See Remark~\ref{rem:noch1}.  Section~\ref{ssec:exduality} does not disturb
  this: the family $(P_t)$ there is a semigroup of \emph{kernels}, and the
  balance condition \ref{it:D1} is assumed in integrated form, so no strong
  continuity, no generator and no domain theory are needed --- which is one
  reason for stating it that way rather than as \DGP{} do.
\item \emph{Both conventions, carried and not chosen.}  $\iota \in \{\mathrm{o},\mathrm{p}\}$
  should be a parameter of the compensator, not a fixed choice.  The evidence is
  in Remark~\ref{rem:embedflip}: the same Markov chain needs $\mathrm{p}$ on
  $\T = \N_0$ and $\mathrm{o}$ once its grid is embedded in $\Rp$.  Everything
  except Section~\ref{sec:cadlag} is convention-agnostic, so the parameter costs a
  hypothesis in one section and nothing anywhere else.
\item \emph{The scalar field is \texttt{RCLike}.}  Test processes take values in
  $\K \in \{\R,\C\}$ (Remark~\ref{rem:complexvalued}), and the Mathlib class for
  this is \texttt{RCLike} --- \texttt{[RCLike \(\mathbb{K}\)]}, with
  \texttt{re}, \texttt{im} and the \texttt{star} operation for the conjugation
  that Fact~\ref{fact:stoneweierstrass} needs.  Two points are worth separating.
  The \emph{martingale} property needs nothing: Mathlib's
  \texttt{MeasureTheory.Martingale} is already stated for an arbitrary real
  Banach space, which is more general than \texttt{RCLike}, and Mathlib's
  conditional expectation API is itself \texttt{RCLike}-parametrized.  What
  \emph{does} want a field is everything that \emph{multiplies} test processes:
  Definition~\ref{def:canonical}, where $Y^\circ_t$ is paired with $Z^\circ_s$;
  Proposition~\ref{prop:fddchar}, with its products $\prod_k h_k$; and
  Section~\ref{sec:duality}, where the chain identity is scalar.  So
  \texttt{RCLike} is the right class for the \emph{test functions} and a Banach
  space would do for the \emph{martingales}; formalize the first with
  \texttt{RCLike} and inherit the second.  The places needing $\K = \R$ are
  listed in Remark~\ref{rem:complexvalued}, and the design point is that they are
  \emph{prerequisites}, not statements: the retreat to $\Re$ and $\Im$ happens in
  exactly one lemma, Corollary~\ref{cor:regK}, so in Lean exactly one proof
  destructs the scalar field and everything above it is uniformly
  \texttt{RCLike}.
\item \emph{The state space is fibred.}  Take
  \texttt{E : T \textrightarrow{} Type*} with
  \texttt{[$\forall$ t, MeasurableSpace (E t)]} and the path space inside
  \texttt{$\Pi$ t, E t}, not a fixed \texttt{E}.  The justification is
  Remark~\ref{rem:fibredrecommend}; the practical points are that the pairs
  $(f,g)$ become sections \eqref{eq:sectiontest}, that
  Sections~\ref{sec:cadlag} and \ref{sec:convergence} should be stated for a
  \emph{constant} fibre rather than carrying an index they immediately freeze,
  and that Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov} needs the total space
  $\Sigma E = \Sigma$ \texttt{t, E t} because $X(\tau)$ is a dependent value.
  Nothing in the abstract layer changes at all, since it never mentions the state
  space.
\item \emph{Augmentation before weak-strong convergence.}  Where a functional
  fails to be continuous at deterministic times --- the atoms of the clock ---
  enlarge the path space (Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug}) rather than weaken the
  mode of convergence.  This keeps Section~\ref{sec:convergence} inside the
  continuous mapping theorem and Vitali's theorem, and avoids formalizing
  Fact~\ref{fact:jacodmemin}.  See Remark~\ref{rem:augvsws}.
\end{enumerate}

% =====================================================================
\section{What this manuscript adds to its sources}
\label{sec:added}
% =====================================================================

This section is an audit, not an advertisement.  The criterion throughout is
narrow: an item is listed only if the statement here is strictly stronger than,
or absent from, \EK, \CPS, \JS, \KA{} and \DGP, and each entry says who uses it.
That last column is the discipline that matters.  A generalization nobody
consumes is not thereby worthless --- it may be exactly what a later application
needs --- but it should not be mistaken for a result, and
Section~\ref{ssec:addednothing} lists four that were.

\subsection{Results that are not in the sources}
\label{ssec:addednew}

The three characterization results of \EK, Chapter~4 are proved here in a
setting with no operator in it, and \CPS{} --- who work in that setting --- prove
none of them.  For the c\`adl\`ag theorem the omission in \CPS{} is structural
rather than accidental: their standing setting presupposes a Polish path space
and right continuous test processes, which is what a regularization theorem has
to produce.

\medskip
\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.30\textwidth}p{0.30\textwidth}p{0.33\textwidth}@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Here} & \textbf{Nearest in the sources} & \textbf{What is new} \\
\midrule
Definition~\ref{def:regclass}, Theorem~\ref{thm:absreg}
  & \EK{} 4.3.6, for an operator
  & the hypothesis is isolated as three conditions on a pair of processes; the
    compensator may be path dependent and may have atoms \\
Definition~\ref{def:propagation}, Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop}
  & \EK{} 4.4.2, for an operator
  & uniqueness is proved for an arbitrary family of measures, with no shift, no
    operator and no Markov structure \\
Lemma~\ref{lem:restart}, Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}
  & \EK{} 4.4.2
  & four constructions in \EK{} become one lemma applied four times; the shifted
    problem is carried as a family, so time inhomogeneity is free \\
Lemma~\ref{lem:chain}
  & \EK{} 4.4.10, on $\Rp$ with Lebesgue measure
  & the analytic core of duality as an exact telescoping over an arbitrary
    staircase, on a preorder, with no probability in it \\
Lemma~\ref{lem:optsamplafter}
  & \EK{} 2.2.13, at bounded times
  & optional sampling at $\tau+s$ for an a.s.\ finite $\tau$, from
    integrability of the increment over the \emph{random} window alone \\
Lemma~\ref{lem:semiring}
  & ---
  & one-sided determination: nonnegativity on a generating semiring containing
    $\Omega$ \\
Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug}, Proposition~\ref{prop:atomaug}
  & \CPS{} 3.14, with control variables
  & for discontinuities at \emph{deterministic} times, enlarging the path space
    replaces weak-strong convergence entirely \\
Lemma~\ref{lem:histrestart}, Corollary~\ref{cor:hawkesrestart}
  & \DGP, Section~5 (outlook)
  & kernel representability yields a \emph{restart kernel}, hence local
    uniqueness for a path-dependent problem with no shift system \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\medskip
\noindent
Two of these have a consumer inside the manuscript and the rest are terminal.
Lemma~\ref{lem:optsamplafter} carries Theorem~\ref{thm:absstrongmarkov};
Lemma~\ref{lem:semiring} carries Remark~\ref{rem:inequalitystable}.
Theorem~\ref{thm:absconvaug} is used by nothing here and is nevertheless the
item this manuscript would most defend, because it removes a whole apparatus
from the one place where the apparatus looked unavoidable.

\subsection{The split that organizes everything: uniqueness is not Markovian}
\label{ssec:addedsplit}

If one thing here is worth carrying elsewhere it is this.  \EK, Theorem~4.4.2
proves uniqueness \emph{and} the Markov property from one hypothesis, and reads
as though the two were the same fact.  They are not.

\medskip
\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.46\textwidth}p{0.30\textwidth}l@{}}
\toprule
& \textbf{needs} & \textbf{Markovian?} \\
\midrule
Uniqueness (Proposition~\ref{prop:uniqfromprop})
  & \eqref{eq:propagation} & \textbf{no} \\
Restarting (Lemma~\ref{lem:restartmemory})
  & the tower property & \textbf{no} \\
\eqref{eq:propagation} from one-dimensional laws
  (Lemma~\ref{lem:propagation})
  & a shift system & yes \\
Markov property (Theorem~\ref{thm:absuniq}\ref{it:absuniq_a})
  & a shift system & yes \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}

\medskip
\noindent
Two consequences that the classical formulation hides.  First, \emph{restarting
a solution is free} (Lemma~\ref{lem:restartmemory}): one may condition on any
event observed up to $r$ and what remains is again a solution.  What a shift
buys is not the restart but the \emph{re-indexing} of the restarted object to
start at time $0$, which an unconditional hypothesis needs and a conditional one
does not; see Remark~\ref{rem:restartnomarkov}.  Second, the conditional form
\eqref{eq:propagation} is available in genuinely non-Markovian situations ---
path-dependent equations, changes of measure, semimartingale problems --- where
no shift system exists, and Section~\ref{ssec:hawkes} exhibits one.

The negative half is sharper still and is not in the sources at all:
Remark~\ref{rem:chainonly} shows that on a partially ordered index the slogan
``uniqueness of the one-dimensional distributions implies uniqueness'' is
\emph{false}, and locates the obstruction in the induction rather than in the
martingale problem.

\subsection{Generalizations that cost nothing}
\label{ssec:addedfree}
